The rewrite matters because "what did we decide about it?" has a pronoun; the model resolves it from recent turns, so we embed "Goa trip hotel decision", which searches far cleaner than the raw words. ¶ That is what the MIND write-up calls four stages: soft-tag search, LLM rewrite plus seed topics, bounded graph expansion, then dense retrieval over notes in scope. Merging and ranking were simple: the graph decided scope and Qdrant similarity ranked inside it. There was no cross-encoder reranker. The one rerank I did add, in April 2026 for COSMOS, is time decay: overfetch long-term facts 3x, multiply each score by `exp(-0.0006 x age_hours)`, and exempt matches at 0.90 or above, so an old but exact memory, like your blood group, still wins. If I added a learned reranker, it would only run on the 20 or so survivors, never the whole set. ¶ Payloads in Qdrant are encrypted, so every read has to decrypt. I searched the six collections of the earlier design in one `asyncio.gather` and decrypted every payload concurrently into deep copies, so decrypting for one caller never mutated an object another caller held. Decrypt is CPU work per point, so it stays cheap only because k is small. ¶ On "under 3 seconds", I want to be precise: it's a team figure, not a measurement I made, and nothing in our code measures it. The MIND write-up had a cost and latency section; I didn't produce it, so I can't tell you which percentile it was or where the clock started. The only timing I added myself were temporary prints around embedding and search that I commented out the same day. If I profiled it properly I'd expect the intent LLM call to dominate, then the embedding call, then vector search and traversal, with JSON parsing and tokenization as noise. I'd confirm it with a span per stage under the turn's trace id, which our in-house LLM call log already carries, and report p50 and p95 per stage, measured on the server from message received to context ready. ¶ Qdrant tuning, honestly, was not HNSW tuning. I added payload indexes on `user_id` and `topic_id` so filtered search doesn't scan, retries for transient errors, and hand-tuned score thresholds. I never measured recall against latency. I'd do it with a labelled query set, compare against Qdrant's exact search for ground-truth recall@k, then sweep `ef` and `m` and try quantization. On sharding: our collections were shared and filtered by `user_id`. Per-user isolation at scale means a tenant-keyed payload index or custom sharding by user, so one user's search hits one shard, with a replication factor of at least two for reads and failover. ¶ In October 2025 I rolled back my own design: retrieval stopped using intent extraction, graph traversal and note search, and each turn used recent working memory plus small per-user scratchpads. The commit doesn't state a why, so here's mine. We were pivoting to WhatsApp personas that needed quick, cheap replies, the funnel put an extra LLM call in front of every answer, and the scratchpads were small enough to just include. The traversal earned its keep on multi-hop questions, which that product rarely asked. Killing your own code when the product changes is the right call. ¶ Context growth is bounded at every step: top-k with thresholds, at most 200 graph nodes, note stubs instead of bodies, with the full note opened by a tool only when needed, the decay rerank, and Omni folds older turns into checkpoint notes. Sensitive memories at the wrong time: we had no sensitivity labels, only relevance gating, and the gate skipping lookups on trivial turns. I'd tag sensitivity at write time and suppress those in proactive or shared contexts unless the user asked. ¶ If I designed it fresh for a 3-second budget: a fast gate decides whether to look up at all, which is what Omni's Jev gate does under a 1.5 second budget; then graph and vector queries run in parallel, not in sequence; merge by reciprocal rank fusion; rerank only the top few; and give each stage a deadline, so a slow stage gets dropped instead of blowing the budget. The COSMOS read we ran through October was close to that: all MTM and LTM searches ran at once in a `TaskGroup` with the decay rerank inline.
  - `L1` Why not just put the whole conversation history into the prompt?
  - `L1` Why a graph plus vectors instead of vectors alone?
  - `L1` Why a graph plus vectors instead of vectors alone?
  - `L2` Walk me through the read path for a question like "what did I plan for Goa?" ↳ embed → similar topics → intent LLM picks topics → traversal + scoped note search
  - `L2` What is the query-intent step and what does its one LLM call return? ↳ topics, rewritten statement, web-search flag, progress message
  - `L2` Why embed an LLM-rewritten statement instead of the raw user message? ↳ pronouns resolved from recent turns; cleaner search
  - `L2` Memory payloads are encrypted in Qdrant. How does retrieval stay fast? ↳ concurrent decrypt into deep copies across six collections
  - `L2` "Under 3 s" — 3 s for what exactly, measured where, at which percentile? ↳ team figure; no measurement in code; be honest
  - `L2` What is time-decay re-ranking and why are strong matches exempt? ↳ score × exp(−0.0006·age_hours), overfetch 3×; ≥0.90 keeps old exact memories
  - `L2` How does retrieval work: vector search, graph traversal, or both? How are results merged and ranked?
  - `L2` What was the retrieval latency budget (under 3 s), and where did the time go?
  - `L3` What is MIND (Memory via Intent-driven Narrowing & Dense Retrieval) and what were its four retrieval stages? ↳ soft-tag search → LLM rewrite + seed topics → bounded expansion → dense notes
  - `L3` In October 2025 the chat stopped using graph traversal for scratchpads. Why roll back your own design?
  - `L3` How did you tune Qdrant at AiRA? Which parameters, and how did you measure recall vs latency?
  - `L3` Profile the AiRA RAG pipeline: where do you expect time to go, and how would you confirm it? ↳ embedding call, vector search, LLM latency, JSON, tokenization
  - `L3` How is Qdrant sharded and replicated, and how would you shard AiRA's memory vectors per user?
  - `L3` How did you stop AiRA's memory from filling the context window?
  - `L3` Did you rerank in AiRA memory retrieval? How did it fit in the 3-second budget?
  - `L3` How do you stop memory from filling the context window?
  - `L3` How do you avoid surfacing a sensitive memory at the wrong time?
  - `L3` Design the memory retrieval path for an assistant with a 3-second budget (AiRA: Memgraph + Qdrant). ↳ parallel graph and vector queries, merge, rerank, budget per stage
- [ ] LongMemEval >95% (team result) — what the benchmark measures, what your part was ↳ LongMemEval paper "Benchmarking Chat Assistants on Long-Term Interactive Memory" (Wu et al., 2024, arXiv) · DHANESH_AIRA_CONTRIBUTIONS.md "Verify before using in a resume" · AIE ch4 "Design Your Evaluation Pipeline" (pdf p224–232)
  - **Answer:** The LongMemEval number is a **team result**, and I want to be exact about my part. The figure is 95.2% on LongMemEval-S, the shorter variant. It's from our MIND paper, "Memory via Intent-driven Narrowing & Dense Retrieval", which lists four authors: Vishnu Vardhan, Ananda Krishnan, me and Sumit Kumar, dated September 18, 2025. My resume says "over 95%" because that's the team's score on a memory system I built a large part of; I didn't run the benchmark. ¶ LongMemEval tests long-term memory in chat assistants. It has 500 questions, each hidden inside a long history of chat sessions, and it checks five abilities: pulling out a fact someone said once, reasoning across several sessions, temporal reasoning like "how long ago did I start", knowledge updates where a later fact replaces an earlier one, and abstention, saying "you never told me" instead of guessing. The S variant uses a history of roughly 115k tokens per question. ¶ My part was the memory engine the benchmark exercised, not the run. The harness and the run were Ananda's: his LongMemEval runner scripts replayed each question's sessions into our chat API with their original dates, including the parser for LongMemEval's timestamp format, and Sumit added the batch models for feeding sessions. My only eval-adjacent change was a one-line fix that made the `ts` field on the chat request optional. What I had built into the system at the time of the run, late August to mid-September 2025: the Memgraph schemas and the weighted, bounded traversal; the topic-only read path I started on August 29; the query-intent step that rewrites the question and picks topics; concurrent decryption across the Qdrant collections; and `storage_v2`, the write side of the topic and note pipeline, on top of Sumit's two-call extraction. The batched writes and the self-healing driver landed a few days after the paper, so they weren't part of the measured system. On the paper, my contribution was that system work and building the first web version of the paper page. ¶ Could I defend "higher than EmergenceMem and Zep"? Not on my own, and I'd say so in the room. Those comparisons are on the page, and to defend them I'd need the exact run config, which answer model and judge were used, whether the baselines' numbers were run by us or quoted from their papers, and the per-category breakdown. I don't have those in front of me, and I won't claim them. Same for what failed most often: the page had a by-category chart, but I didn't run the analysis, so I can't tell you which category was weakest. Temporal reasoning and multi-session questions are usually the hard ones in this benchmark, and I'd expect the same for us. ¶ How I'd evaluate memory myself, without LongMemEval: build a small set from our own consented sessions. Plant facts, update some of them later, then ask questions days later. Score three things separately: retrieval recall at k, meaning was the right memory in the context at all; answer accuracy with an LLM judge checked by hand on a sample; and abstention, meaning did it refuse when nothing was stored. Separating retrieval from answering tells you which half to fix. I'd also keep a regression set of real failures, like a moved city or a renamed contact. ¶ Benchmarks don't tell you whether real users got a better assistant. For that I'd track how often memory was used in a reply, how often users correct it, things like "no, I moved" or "that's not me", how often they repeat something they already told us, and run an A/B with memory on and off for a slice of users, comparing retention and task completion. ¶ What I'd build now is that pipeline in CI: the eval set runs on every change to a memory prompt or threshold, because in this kind of system prompts change behaviour more than code does. Through October we logged every LLM call with a per-turn trace id in Mongo, so failures could be replayed exactly. The thing I'd add first is retrieval recall, because when memory fails it's usually because the right fact never reached the prompt, not because the model ignored it.
  - `L1` What is LongMemEval and what does it test? ↳ long-term chat memory: extraction, multi-session reasoning, temporal, knowledge updates, abstention
  - `L1` LongMemEval score and variant? ↳ 95.2%, LongMemEval-S, team result
  - `L2` Your resume says "over 95% on LongMemEval". Is that your result or the team's, and which variant? ↳ 95.2% LongMemEval-S, MIND paper, four co-authors; team result
  - `L2` What was your part in the LongMemEval run? ↳ memory engine pieces; timestamp parsing for eval harness; say exactly
  - `L2` The MIND paper lists you as a co-author. What was your contribution to the research? ↳ page and memory code; research not code in repos; be precise
  - `L2` What did LongMemEval measure, and what was your part in the >95% team result?
  - `L2` How did you evaluate AiRA memory (LongMemEval)?
  - `L3` How would you evaluate memory quality yourself, without LongMemEval?
  - `L3` The MIND page quotes 95.2% and "higher than EmergenceMem and Zep". Could you defend those comparisons in an interview? ↳ team research; know the setup or say you didn't run it
  - `L3` How do you evaluate memory quality?
  - `L3` How did you evaluate AiRA's agent or memory quality, and what would you build now?
  - `L3` How did you evaluate AiRA memory with LongMemEval? What does the benchmark measure, and what was your part? ↳ say it was a team result; abilities tested
  - `L3` How would you know if memory made the assistant better for real users, not only on the benchmark?
  - `L3` How did you evaluate AiRA memory with LongMemEval? What failed most often?
- [ ] Updating/contradicting memories (user moved city), deleting memories (privacy) ↳ AIE ch6 "Memory" (pdf p328; conflicting information) · bullets.md § aira-api-bg (memory consolidation) · DDIA ch11 "State, streams, and immutability" (pdf p469; immutability and deletion)
  - **Answer:** Contradictions are resolved when short-term memory is consolidated into long-term, not at chat time. When a new long-term fact is written, we look up the three most similar existing facts above cosine 0.88, and for each one a cheap model answers a single yes or no question: does the new memory supersede the old one? If yes, the old fact is deleted. I built that as part of the COSMOS flush in April 2026. ¶ "I moved from Bangalore to Pune" goes like this. During the day the turn's summary joins a mid-term cluster. At the flush, the archivist prompt sees the cluster and is told to resolve contradictions inside it, keep the final state, and turn relative times into real dates. So the fact comes out as something like "lives in Pune, moved from Bangalore" with a date. That fact is embedded, finds "lives in Bangalore" above 0.88, the supersede check says yes, and the old one is deleted. Two weak spots: until the nightly flush the old fact can still be retrieved, and if the two embed below 0.88 both survive, with only time decay favouring the newer one. I'd move to keyed facts, like `home_city` with a validity range, and keep the old one as history instead of deleting it. "Used to live in Bangalore" is still true, and knowledge-update questions need it. ¶ "Delete everything you know about me" is a hard delete across every store, run concurrently. My teammate Shashwat wrote `purge_all`: the user document, every Mongo collection on its list, which covers sessions, turns, checkpoints, notes, agent runs, approvals, connector tokens and vault keys, the user's points in every Qdrant memory collection by `user_id` filter, and some Redis key patterns. The service also revokes the Apple token and deletes the user's Browserbase contexts. Dropping the vault's wrapped user keys makes any sealed credential unreadable, which is the crypto-shredding I built. Gaps I found reading the version we ran through October: blobs weren't deleted, IRIS reminder rows weren't on the list, and other Redis keys only aged out by TTL. The team's November rewrite does purge reminders, and it has no Redis at all. To prove it's gone, I'd add a post-purge count by `user_id` in every store, a deletion log, and a stated backup expiry. ¶ If the LLM extracts a wrong fact, today a user can browse it: the memory API lists long-term memories grouped by date, and their AiRA Notes. But it's read-only. There's no endpoint to delete or edit one memory, so the only fix is telling AiRA, which goes through the contradiction pass. That's a real gap. I'd add per-memory delete and edit, and store provenance on each fact, the turns it came from, so the user can see why AiRA believes it. ¶ On the vector transform: in the background consolidation service I added a secret-seeded permutation and sign flip of stored vectors. A signed permutation is an orthogonal matrix, so it preserves dot products and lengths, which means cosine similarity and search work unchanged. It protects one thing: someone who dumps the vectors without the seed can't feed them straight into an embedding-inversion model. It doesn't protect similarity structure, so neighbours, clusters and "these two are the same text" all leak. Anyone with the seed reverses it, and with a public embedding model, a few known texts let an attacker solve for it. It's obfuscation, not encryption. And in that service the raw embedding was also stored in the payload, so it protected less than it looked.
  - `L2` How are contradictions resolved? ↳ top-3 LTM facts above cosine 0.88, cheap "supersedes?" LLM call, delete superseded
  - `L2` A user says "delete everything you know about me". What exactly gets deleted, and from which stores?
  - `L2` "I moved from Bangalore to Pune." Walk me through how the old fact is replaced.
  - `L2` How do you update or contradict an old memory ("I moved to Pune")?
  - `L3` A secret-seeded permutation and sign-flip of stored vectors keeps cosine unchanged. What does it protect, and what doesn't it? ↳ raw embeddings unreadable without seed; similarity leakage remains
  - `L3` The LLM extracts a wrong fact about the user. How would the user ever find and fix it?
  - `L3` The user says "I moved to Pune". What happens to the old "lives in Bangalore" fact?
  - `L3` How does a user delete a memory, and how do you make sure it is gone from graph, vectors and caches?
- [ ] Memgraph knowledge graph: topic nodes, LLM-scored edges, weighted bounded traversal, restarts and a self-healing driver
  - **Answer:** This was AiRA's graph memory from August to October 2025, built with Ananda: he wrote the neo4j async driver service and node models, and I wrote the schemas, indexes, traversals, batch writes and the self-healing connection. It's history now: I switched traversal off in October 2025, and Ananda deleted the Memgraph code in December. What replaced it is COSMOS, mid-term clusters and long-term facts in Qdrant with Notes in Mongo, and in Omni, session checkpoints plus a fast gate that decides whether to look anything up. ¶ In the topic design a node is a topic the user talks about, like "goa trip" or "mom", lower-cased and scoped by `user_id`. Earlier there were also Entity, Fact and Event nodes. An edge is a relation from a fixed vocabulary, turned into a Cypher-safe upper-snake type, with a `relevance_score` that the storage LLM assigns when it files the turn. So edge weights are the model's judgement of how strongly two topics are linked, written at write time. ¶ The traversal keeps a path only while the product of its edge scores stays above a threshold. Multiplying is the point: two strong links, 0.9 times 0.9, survive, but a chain of weak links quickly falls below the line, so you get the near neighbourhood that matters and long chains of loose associations drop out. Adding scores would do the opposite and reward long paths. The walk never passes through the user's own node, first the literal "user", later their real first name, because that node links to almost everything, and walking through it returns the whole graph. To bound cost, depth shrinks from 6 to 3 as the number of seed topics grows, the result is capped at 200 nodes, and the seed nodes are always returned. ¶ Memgraph keeps data in memory, so a restart is a real question. Our local compose file kept `/var/lib/memgraph` on a volume, which is where Memgraph writes its snapshots and write-ahead log, so a local restart reloads from disk, minus whatever wasn't durable yet. Production was a hosted instance over `bolt+ssc`, and I never checked its snapshot settings, so I won't promise more than that. The safety net was that the graph was an index: the content lived in Qdrant notes, and topic ids were stable `uuid5(user:name)` values, so the graph could be rebuilt from Qdrant. We never wrote that rebuild job, though. ¶ For the self-healing driver: a factory with a pool of 50, a 10 second connect timeout, keep-alive and a capped connection lifetime; `verify_connectivity()` before each use, and on `ServiceUnavailable` close and recreate the driver; plus a background `RETURN 1` ping every 60 seconds with failures logged to Mongo. My first version of that ping awaited the endless loop at startup, which would have blocked boot, and I fixed it in the next commit to run as a background task. ¶ A real question where the graph beat vectors? I don't have a logged A/B example to quote, and I won't make one up. The MIND paper used a team dinner planning case study; here's an illustration of my own to show the shape of the win: "where should we go for the team dinner?" is close in vector space to restaurant notes, but the fact that one teammate is vegetarian sits under that teammate's topic, linked to "team", and shares no words with the question. The traversal walks team to teammate to diet. Vectors alone would have missed it, unless that note happened to mention dinner.
  - `L2` What is a node and what is an edge in your Memgraph graph? Where do edge weights come from? ↳ topic nodes; LLM-scored `relevance_score` on edges from fixed vocabulary
  - `L2` Explain the weighted traversal: why multiply edge scores along a path? ↳ product drops long chains of weak links below threshold
  - `L2` Why must the traversal never walk through the user's own node? ↳ user hub links to everything; walking it returns whole graph
  - `L2` How did you bound graph queries? ↳ depth shrinks 6→3 as seeds grow; max 200 nodes
  - `L2` Memgraph is in-memory. What happens to memory on a Memgraph restart?
  - `L2` How did you make the Memgraph connection self-heal? ↳ verify before use, recreate driver on ServiceUnavailable, 60 s keep-alive ping
  - `L3` Graph traversal vs vector search: give a real question where the graph found something the vector search missed.

## 1.4 AiRA — reminders, Gmail/Calendar agents
- [ ] Redis-based scheduling with at-most-once delivery — why at-most-once, how you dedupe ↳ bullets.md § aira-api_s5 (IRIS scheduler, Redis SET NX locks) + § aira-api_s7 (fires at most once) · DDIA ch11 "Fault tolerance" (pdf p482–484; idempotence) · DBI ch8 "Exactly-once delivery" (pdf p300–302)
  - **Answer:** IRIS, "Intent Recognition & Intelligent Scheduling", is the reminder system I built, and it's deliberately at-most-once: a reminder can be lost in a crash but is never sent twice. At-most-once means "remind me at 9" arrives once or not at all; at-least-once means it always arrives, maybe twice after a retry; exactly-once is really at-least-once plus a receiver that drops the duplicate. I chose it because a duplicate reminder, or in the early version a duplicate scheduled email, is worse than a rare miss. ¶ December 2025: "remind me every Monday at 9" became a `create_iris_task` call into `UserTask`; an hourly cron copied the next hour's due tasks into a small `GlobalTask` run-now table, so the every-minute cron only scanned a tiny table. Recurring tasks advance from their previous trigger, not from now, or each run drifts by the cron's lateness. One of my own commits, titled as a fix, flipped the expiry filter in the due-task query from `$gte` to `$lte`; reading it now, it's inverted, so a recurring reminder with a future end date would silently stop firing and an expired one would still fire. Two tests, "expires tomorrow, must fire" and "expired yesterday, must not", would have caught it. And when the model writes "notify" or "ping", a Pydantic before-validator maps the synonyms to `remind`. ¶ Every instance runs its own APScheduler, so several instances meant every cron fired once per instance. In April 2026 I added a lock per cron, `SET NX EX` with 55 seconds, and in August I wrapped every job in `cron:run:<job_id>` with 50. The TTL sits under the one-minute tick, so the lock is never released, it just expires, and a constant value is fine. A lock you do release needs a random token and a Lua compare-and-delete, which is how TEMPEX and my later leases worked. Through October we ran one production instance, but a slot swap could briefly put two up; the lock made that harmless. ¶ The lock isn't what makes a reminder fire once; the resume line compresses two layers. In August 2026 I rebuilt IRIS on one `Tasks` collection and a per-minute sweep that polls Mongo on a `trigger_time` index, not a sorted set and not keyspace events, which are lost if nobody listens. Before anything runs, one `bulk_write` settles every due row: one-offs and expired tasks deleted, recurring ones moved to their first future slot, which also stops a task that fell behind during downtime from re-firing every sweep. Then they fire, four at a time in my version, each as a system turn plus a push. If the worker dies after the settle, that reminder is lost and never repeats, and the user sees nothing at all; that's the guarantee, not a hole in it. The April version made the same trade by deleting due tasks before running them: it fixed a broken task being retried every minute, and gave up any retry for a failed one. ¶ Where it can still break: read and settle aren't one atomic claim. If a sweep outran the TTL, say 70 seconds against 50, or Redis failed over to a replica that never got the lock key, two sweeps could read the same row and fire it twice. The real fix is a per-row compare-and-set with `find_one_and_update` on the `trigger_time` I read. An evicted lock or dedupe key lets duplicates through too, so that Redis should be `noeviction`; I haven't verified ours. For effectively-once: settle after sending, retry, and dedupe at delivery on task id plus trigger time under a unique index. Silent loss is the weak part: in my version a failed fire wasn't logged. I'd log claims and deliveries, alert when they diverge, and track lateness. Since November, master runs Ananda's rewrite on Cosmos DB, without Redis, across two regions, and it does exactly that: a per-row claim with a renewable 60-second lease, delivery under a stable event id, then advance. ¶ Through October, Redis did a lot at AiRA: locks and leases, the realtime channel, `BLPOP` queues, agent slot counters, working memory and turn logs. For dedupe, `SET NX` with a TTL filters duplicates inside a window the TTL must cover, and anything later gets through. A unique constraint is permanent and atomic with the write. An idempotency table also stores the result for the retry, like our Stripe Link payment ledger. Key before processing risks lost work; after risks duplicates. Two instances getting the same Gmail push 5 ms apart, on my Gmail-push branch that isn't merged yet: both set a `gmail_again` flag, one wins the 120-second token lease, the loser returns, the winner re-checks the flag after releasing, and `history_id` moves by compare-and-set, so it advances once. That path is at-least-once on purpose: a repeated summary is cheap, a missed mail isn't. ¶ Why not Postgres with `SKIP LOCKED`? We don't run Postgres; with it, `SKIP LOCKED` is the better per-row claim. A Kubernetes CronJob replaces the per-tick lock, not the per-task settle. Time zones: the tool takes ISO times with the user's offset, naive times get the user's IANA zone, and everything is stored in UTC. The weakness: recurring steps are whole days in UTC, so with daylight saving a 9 am reminder shifts an hour. The fix is local wall time plus zone, or an RRULE. ¶ At scale: 100,000 reminders at 9:00 would queue behind four concurrent fires, each an LLM turn plus a push. I'd push plain reminders directly and rate-limit the rest through a worker pool. For 10 million users at per-second precision I'd swap the minute sweep for time-bucketed sorted sets: score is the due time, and a Lua script runs `ZRANGEBYSCORE` up to now and removes what it returns, an atomic claim, sharded by bucket and user. Calendars fit the same timer, which already woke agent tasks and renewed the Gmail watch: "remind me before my meeting" is a row due at start minus the lead.
  - `L1` At-most-once vs at-least-once vs exactly-once: define each with a reminder example.
  - `L1` What is a distributed lock? How do you take and release one in Redis? ↳ `SET key token NX PX ttl`; Lua compare-and-delete release
  - `L1` Redis command for a lock? ↳ `SET key value NX PX ttl`
  - `L1` Delivery guarantee for reminders? ↳ at most once
  - `L1` What is IRIS? ↳ the reminder and scheduled-task system
  - `L1` List every way you used Redis at AiRA. ↳ locks, Streams realtime channel, queues, slot gating, dedupe
  - `L1` What does "at-most-once" mean for a reminder?
  - `L2` Walk me through IRIS: how does "remind me every Monday at 9" become a fired reminder?
  - `L2` Why two tables (`UserTask` hourly planning, `GlobalTask` run-now queue) in the first design? ↳ every-minute cron scans only the small run-now table
  - `L2` Why move recurring tasks forward from the previous trigger time, not from now? ↳ stops schedule drift
  - `L2` With three API instances, every cron fired three times. How did you fix it? ↳ Redis lock per job per tick (~50–55 s TTL)
  - `L2` Your resume says "fires at most once, even after a crash". Exactly how? ↳ settle due rows in one `bulk_write` before executing; crash loses, never repeats
  - `L2` Why choose at-most-once for reminders instead of at-least-once? What does the user see on a crash?
  - `L2` The LLM writes "notify" or "ping" instead of `remind`. How did you handle it? ↳ Pydantic before-validator maps synonyms to the one valid name
  - `L2` A code-observed bug flipped the expiry filter from `$gte` to `$lte`. What would users have seen, and how would you catch it?
  - `L2` At-most-once vs at-least-once: which did you pick for reminders and why? What does the user see on failure?
  - `L2` What happens if the worker crashes right after popping a reminder?
  - `L2` How did the scheduler find due reminders: a sorted set by fire time, polling, or keyspace events?
  - `L2` Why at-most-once and not at-least-once? What does the user see when it fails? ↳ duplicate notification is worse than a rare missed one
  - `L2` How did the `SET NX` lock make a reminder fire at most once across workers? What were the key and TTL?
  - `L2` The worker crashes right after taking the lock, before sending. What happens? ↳ reminder is lost: the accepted cost of at-most-once
  - `L2` What if sending takes longer than the lock TTL?
  - `L2` Where did you use `SET NX` for deduplication, and what was the dedupe key?
  - `L2` How did you choose the dedupe TTL? What happens to a duplicate that arrives after it expires?
  - `L2` Set the dedupe key before or after processing? What does each order risk? ↳ before: lost work on crash; after: duplicates in the window
  - `L2` How did you get at-most-once reminders with Redis?
  - `L2` AiRA reminders use Redis locks for at-most-once. Would a CronJob replace that? What changes?
  - `L3` Is "at most once" really guaranteed if the Mongo write succeeds but the process dies before the reminder is sent?
  - `L3` A Redis lock TTL of 55 s and a job that takes 70 s. What happens? How would you fix it properly?
  - `L3` Design reminders for 10M users with per-second precision. Would you keep the minute sweep? ↳ time-bucketed sorted sets or delay queue; partition by time
  - `L3` The global cron deletes due tasks before running them. What did that fix and what did it give up? ↳ no retry storms every minute; a failed task is lost
  - `L3` Where did you handle time ranges at work (reminders, schedules, slots), and how did you store them?
  - `L3` How does `ZRANGEBYSCORE` turn a sorted set into a delay queue for reminders (AiRA)? ↳ score = due time; claim ≤ now atomically with Lua
  - `L3` How did AiRA avoid sending the same reminder twice across workers? Which structure and lock?
  - `L3` Redis fails over to a replica that never got the lock key. Can a reminder fire twice? ↳ yes, async replication; dedupe at sender if it matters
  - `L3` How would you change the design to get effectively-once delivery? ↳ at-least-once plus an idempotency key at the delivery step
  - `L3` How would you know reminders were being silently lost? What did you measure?
  - `L3` Why Redis and not a Postgres table with `SKIP LOCKED`, or cron plus a DB flag?
  - `L3` 100,000 reminders are due at exactly 9:00. What breaks, and how do you spread the load?
  - `L3` `SET NX` dedupe vs a Postgres unique constraint vs an idempotency table: when each?
  - `L3` Is Redis dedupe safe if keys can be evicted under memory pressure? ↳ evicted key lets duplicates through; noeviction instance
  - `L3` Two API instances receive the same webhook within 5 ms. Walk through what your dedupe does.
  - `L3` Where did you need at-most-once vs at-least-once in your own work? ↳ AiRA reminders: at-most-once with Redis locks — why that choice
  - `L3` How did AiRA's reminder workers avoid two workers firing the same reminder? What breaks if Redis fails over? ↳ Redis lock at-most-once; failover may lose lock → duplicate risk
  - `L3` How do you schedule reminders correctly across user time zones and DST changes? (Tie to AiRA reminders.)
  - `L3` At-least-once vs at-most-once for reminders: which did AiRA choose and why? ↳ at-most-once via Redis locks; duplicate reminders worse than rare miss
  - `L3` How would AiRA's reminder engine plug into a calendar like this?
  - `L3` How do you make a scheduled reminder fire at most once, even across crashes?
- [ ] OAuth for Google APIs, token refresh, scopes, revocation ↳ bullets.md § aira-api_s3 (token refresh decorators) + § aira-api_s4 (incremental scopes) · https://developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/web-server
  - **Answer:** We ask for the narrowest Google scopes per feature, keep encrypted tokens per scope, refresh transparently on a 401, and notice revocation the next time a refresh fails. An access token is short-lived, about an hour for Google, and is what you send on every API call. A refresh token is long-lived until the user revokes it, and is only used to mint new access tokens. Google only sends a refresh token on the first consent, which bit us twice: in January 2026 I made sign-in keep the stored refresh token when Google omits a new one, and in April I added `prompt=consent` to connector URLs so connecting always returns one. ¶ A scope is the permission you ask for. In December 2025 I narrowed full mail and full calendar to `gmail.modify` and `calendar.events`, and in January full Drive to `drive.file`, which only sees files the app created or the user opened with it. Those three are still exactly what the connector asks for on master today. In January I also made consent incremental: after consent we read the granted scopes from Google's tokeninfo endpoint, store one encrypted token row per scope, and a Connectors API lets the app list, connect and disconnect each one. So mail access is asked for when you first need mail, not at sign-up. ¶ Refresh-on-401 is a decorator I wrote in April 2026, `refresh_google_token_on_401(connector)`, around every Gmail and Calendar tool. On a 401 it decrypts the stored refresh token, calls Google's refresh grant, encrypts and saves the new access token, and retries the call once. Once, because a second 401 means something else is wrong. A decorator because there are many tools and one error path; tool bodies stay clean, and the decorator also tags the function with its connector, so the runtime knows which token to inject. Tokens are hidden from the model's schema and injected at execution. ¶ Scheduled jobs and background tasks have no user present, so they load the encrypted token from Mongo, refresh on a 401 the same way, and carry on, whether it starts in a minute or hours later. Gmail push has one extra piece: in my version the watch was renewed by an IRIS row a day before it expired. ¶ Revocation is noticed lazily. If the user removes AiRA in their Google account, nothing tells us right away. The next tool call, Gmail push or watch renewal gets a 401, the refresh then fails with a 4xx, we mark the grant as gone, and the connector shows as disconnected in the app so the user can reconnect. Disconnecting a service also ends any background task waiting on it. To notice sooner I'd store the access token's expiry and refresh slightly early, which also saves the failed round trip, and subscribe to Google's cross-account protection events, which push revocations to you.
  - `L1` Access token vs refresh token: what is each and how long does each live?
  - `L1` What is an OAuth scope? Which scopes did AiRA ask for? ↳ narrowed to `gmail.modify`, `calendar.events`, `drive.file`
  - `L2` How does refresh-on-401 work and why a decorator? ↳ catch 401, decrypt refresh token, refresh, re-encrypt, retry once
  - `L2` A user revokes access in their Google account. What does AiRA notice and when?
  - `L3` Scheduled jobs run with no user present. How do they get a valid token? ↳ load and decrypt stored token from Mongo; refresh on 401
  - `L3` How do you keep OAuth tokens for Gmail, Calendar, Drive and Sheets fresh for background runs that start hours later?
  - `L3` How did you handle Google OAuth for AiRA's Gmail and Calendar agents: scopes, refresh, revocation, incremental consent?
- [ ] Rate limits and retries with Google APIs ↳ https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/timeouts-retries-and-backoff-with-jitter/ · https://developers.google.com/gmail/api/reference/quota · bullets.md § aira-api_s2 (Gmail Batch API)
  - **Answer:** Honestly, we designed for Gmail quotas more than we measured them. I don't have a log of us hitting a 429, and I won't quote quota numbers from memory. What I do know shaped the design: Gmail meters per user and per project in quota units, every message fetch costs units, and the batch API saves HTTP round trips but not quota, because each request inside a batch still counts separately. So batching made onboarding faster, not cheaper. ¶ That onboarding scrape is history: it was removed in the March 2026 restructure, and Gmail is now read on demand and by push. But as built, at 10,000 sign-ups in one day, per-user limits aren't the problem, since each user has their own budget. The project-wide limit and our LLM spend are. Onboarding read up to 1,000 emails per user in batches of 25, which is about 40 map calls plus one reduce per user, so roughly 400,000 LLM calls that day, and the December code started each scrape as an in-process background task with no bound across users. A burst like that would hit the project quota, then the LLM provider's rate limit, and every retry would make it worse. ¶ What I'd build: put onboarding jobs on a durable queue with a global worker limit; a token bucket per project sized to the published quota; retry 429s and rate-limit 403s with exponential backoff and full jitter, honouring any retry-after, with a retry budget so retries can't snowball; and process the newest mail first so a user sees value in minutes even when the full read finishes hours later. I'd also put a counter on every quota error, so we know which limit we hit first instead of guessing.
  - `L3` What Gmail quotas did you hit or design for? What happens to the onboarding job at 10k users signing up in one day?
- [ ] Onboarding job: reading 1,000 emails through the Gmail Batch API, cleaning, map-reduce summaries, batch-size trade-offs
  - **Answer:** When a user connected Gmail, onboarding read up to 1,000 relevant emails and built a factual profile of them with a map-reduce over LLM summaries. I built it in December 2025. It's history now: it went in the March 2026 restructure, and what replaced it is reading Gmail on demand, through the inbox skill's searches, and by push when new mail arrives. ¶ The Gmail Batch API lets you send up to 100 API calls in one HTTP request: a `multipart/mixed` body where each part is a GET, and a multipart response back. I built the request by hand and parsed the response with a regex because our stack was fully async, and Google's Python client does batching on its synchronous HTTP layer, so it would have blocked the event loop. ¶ The flow: right after the OAuth callback the scrape starts in the background and marks status in progress. Message ids stream page by page from an async generator with a search query covering jobs, money, education, travel, home and orders, capped at 1,000 and skipping sent and drafts. Each page of ids is fetched with one batch request, and messages are processed concurrently: headers cut down to from, to, subject and date, and HTML stripped by regex, scripts, styles, tags, URLs, email addresses and zero-width characters, to cut tokens. The map step summarises each batch into "profile atoms", type, fact, entities, date, amount and source, saying "unknown" instead of guessing. Each batch's summary starts as its own task the moment the batch arrives, so fetching and LLM calls overlap. The reduce step merges all batches into one profile, dedupes facts, and marks conflicting ones uncertain. Then the user is told it's done. ¶ Map-reduce instead of one call because 1,000 cleaned emails don't fit a context window, and even if they did, one giant prompt is slow, fails all at once, and the model skims. Batches run in parallel, a bad batch only loses its own facts, and the reduce sees short summaries. ¶ Batch size went 100, then 20 on December 4, then 30, then 25 on December 15. The commits say "performance", and I don't have a measured reason, so here's the trade I was tuning. The batch size was both the Gmail fetch group and the LLM map unit. Bigger batches mean fewer round trips and fewer LLM calls, but a burst of 100 fetches against one user's quota, and a prompt too big for a cheap model to summarise carefully. Smaller batches mean more calls and overhead. 25 was where it felt balanced. If I did it again I'd split the two: fetch in 100s, summarise by token count.
  - `L1` Max emails read by onboarding? ↳ 1,000
  - `L1` Gmail Batch API: max calls per batch request? ↳ 100
  - `L2` What is the Gmail Batch API and why build the multipart request by hand? ↳ up to 100 GETs per `multipart/mixed` request; regex-parse response
  - `L2` Walk me through the onboarding job: list, batch fetch, clean, summarize, combine. ↳ stream ids, batch API, strip HTML/headers, parallel map, LLM reduce
  - `L2` Why map-reduce instead of one big LLM call over 1,000 emails?
  - `L2` Why did batch size change 100 → 20 → 30 → 25? What were you trading? ↳ rate limits/quota vs LLM context per batch
- [ ] Gmail, Calendar and Sheets agent tools: contact lookup by sound, A1 ranges, Drive sharing scopes, confirming destructive actions
  - **Answer:** In January 2026 I built a 14-tool Google Sheets agent: read, write, append, clear, create, list, share, delete, plus Drive permission management and pasting a link instead of an id. The flow for one request: the orchestrator picks the `google-sheets` agent, the model chooses a tool, its Pydantic args model is filled, the access token and user id are injected but hidden from the model's schema, and the service calls Sheets v4 or Drive v3 over async REST with refresh-on-401. The response is cut down to a small typed model before going back to the model. ¶ A1 notation is how Sheets names ranges: `Expenses!A1:F50` means the Expenses tab, columns A to F, rows 1 to 50. The 283-line agent playbook spelled out rules because models get it wrong in predictable ways: they guess tab names, write open-ended ranges, or overwrite a header. So the rule is learn the tab names first, then use bounded ranges. Today's sheets skill still says exactly that: list the sheets, then read a range like `Expenses!A1:F50`. When a user pastes a link, `get_sheet_id_from_link` pulls the id with a regex on `/spreadsheets/d/<id>`, so the model never has to guess it. ¶ Narrowing Drive from `drive` to `drive.file` means the token can only see files the app created or the user opened with it, not the whole Drive. That limits the blast radius of a bad tool call or a leaked token, and it's a far lighter Google verification. `share_sheet` was changed to only return the link, and real access changes went through separate permission tools, because "share this" is ambiguous and granting access is the risky part. It should be its own explicit step with its own warning. On those tools, Drive rejected `emailAddress` for `domain` and `anyone` permissions, so I send it only for `user` and `group`. ¶ "Email Rahul" used to go through a phonetic contact index I built in November 2025 and rebuilt in December. Every sender name from incoming mail was turned into Metaphone codes, which encode how a name sounds, so "Raju" and "Raaju" give the same code; the codes were embedded into Qdrant with a dedupe on the code, and a search above 0.75 similarity returned the matching contacts from Mongo. That index is history: it went in the March 2026 restructure. What replaced it is a rule: recipients come only from what the user said or from the sender of a mail read in that task, which is stricter and never invents an address. ¶ Destructive actions went from prompt rules to code. In 2025 the tool descriptions had "USE WHEN" and "WARNING" lines, and the playbook demanded confirmation before overwrite, clear or delete. Delete email was a permanent Gmail delete, and its description told the agent to search first and confirm. That's only as safe as the model's obedience. Today every tool has a tier checked at boot. Sending, replying and trashing mail, calendar changes and every sharing change are ask-tier: the call is held, the user approves a card, and it runs once. Delete became trash, which is recoverable, and in the version I built, the trash card re-reads the sender and subject fresh from Gmail, so the user sees the real mail, not what the model thinks it is. One gap I'd close: updating and clearing sheet values are still automatic today, on the reasoning that Sheets keeps version history so a write can be put back. I'd still make clearing ask-tier, because nobody notices a cleared range until it matters.
  - `L1` What could a user ask AiRA to do with Google Sheets? ↳ read, write, append, clear, create, list, share, delete, manage permissions
  - `L1` What is A1 notation, and why did the agent playbook spell out rules for it?
  - `L2` "Email Rahul" — how does AiRA find the right Rahul? ↳ Metaphone codes of sender names embedded in Qdrant, threshold 0.75
  - `L2` Walk me through one Sheets request, from user message to the Sheets v4 call. ↳ orchestrator picks agent; args model filled; token injected hidden; async REST
  - `L2` Why narrow Drive access from `drive` to `drive.file`? ↳ app sees only files it created or user opened; easier verification
  - `L2` Why does `share_sheet` only return a link, while real access changes go through separate permission tools?
  - `L2` Drive rejected `emailAddress` for some permission types. Which, and how did you handle it? ↳ send it only for `user`/`group`, not `domain`/`anyone`
  - `L2` A user pastes a Sheets link instead of an id. How does the agent cope? ↳ `get_sheet_id_from_link` regex on `/spreadsheets/d/<id>`
  - `L2` How do destructive Sheets tools stay safe? ↳ "USE WHEN / WARNING" descriptions; playbook demands confirmation; later ask-tier approvals
  - `L3` Delete email is permanent. How did you stop the agent deleting the wrong one? ↳ tool description forces search + confirm; later ask-tier approval
- [ ] TEMPEX: merging bursts of short messages into one reply, cancelling stale runs, steering a running task
  - **Answer:** TEMPEX, short for "Temporal Mutual Exclusion & Preemption Exchange", is something I built in November 2025 because WhatsApp users type in bursts: "hi", "can you", "check my mail". Each message started its own LLM run, so they got three overlapping, half-cancelled answers. TEMPEX merges a burst into one reply and cancels the run that the newer message made stale. ¶ Version one was a Redis hash per user and chat with a status, collecting or replying, the merged text and the chunks already sent, all changed under a lock: `SET key token NX PX ttl`, released by a Lua compare-and-delete so only the owner can release it. If a reply was already streaming, the sent part went into the next prompt so the model continued instead of repeating itself. Version two in December added a trace id per request, an ordered stream queue where only the head may speak, a cancel list, and Lua scripts for atomic queue edits, plus polling loops while a run waited its turn. ¶ In February 2026 I cut it down to two fields, `pending_query` and `pending_trace_id`. Under the lock, a new message merges any pending text and takes ownership. The run then checks ownership at two checkpoints, after the pre-LLM step and after the main LLM, and cancels itself if a newer message took over. No queues, no Lua and no polling. Fewer moving parts meant fewer race windows and less Redis traffic, and it was easier to reason about, which mattered more than the extra features. ¶ For "actually make it 6pm" during a running task, version two routed the message: if it answered an in-flight task, it was appended to that task's execution log in Mongo and the new run stopped, and the orchestrator re-read the log on every iteration, so the running task saw the correction at its next step. TEMPEX is history: it went in the March 2026 restructure, with the WhatsApp chat pipeline it served. What replaced it, in Omni as I built it, is a turn buffer that folds messages arriving mid-answer into the running turn, and background tasks get corrections through their inbox.
  - `L1` What does TEMPEX do? ↳ merges bursts of messages into one reply
  - `L2` What problem does TEMPEX solve? ↳ users send 3 short messages; merge into one reply, cancel stale runs
  - `L2` TEMPEX v2 used Lua scripts and an ordered stream queue; the redesign used two fields. Why simplify? ↳ `pending_query` + `pending_trace_id`, ownership checkpoints; fewer races, no polling
  - `L2` A user says "actually make it 6pm" while a task is running. How did that reach the running task? ↳ appended to task execution logs; orchestrator re-reads each iteration
- [ ] Background-agent runtime: per-user 3-slot queue, Mongo state machine, inbox, sleep/wake, boot recovery, upkeep sweeps
  - **Answer:** I built the durable background-agent runtime in October 2026 because some jobs don't fit in a chat turn. Ordering on Swiggy, researching for ten minutes, or waiting a day for the user to approve an email can't block a realtime conversation, and they have to survive a restart. ¶ One task, start to finish: the main model calls `start_task` with a kind, a skill, a title and a brief. The manager checks the skill exists, every service it needs is connected, and any parent task belongs to the user, then inserts a `queued` row in `AgentRuns`. To admit it, it does `INCR` on `slots:{user}`; if that goes over 3 it does `DECR` and the task stays queued; if the Mongo claim fails it decrements again so the counter can't drift. The agent loop then runs step by step, saving a checkpoint each step. It reports with `share_progress`, which becomes an `[AGENT_PROGRESS]` turn, and finishes with a `done` tool whose schema is the skill's findings model. On the way out the app gets a task event and a card, the user gets a push, and an `[AGENT_DONE]` turn hands the findings to the main model, fenced as outside data, so Aira tells the user in the conversation. The slot frees and the next queued task starts. ¶ The row is the state machine: queued, running, sleeping, paused, then ready, failed or stopped. Every write is guarded, so it only moves from an expected status and a late writer can't reopen a finished task. The inbox holds what comes in for the task: steering, answers, credentials, approvals, wake-ups. It's separate from the conversation because those are inputs to the task's own context, not chat history, and the inbox is only drained while the task is running, so a paused task keeps its messages. Slots lived in Redis because a counter needs a fast atomic `INCR` and is disposable, rebuilt at boot; the braces in the key are a cluster hash tag. Run state lived in Mongo because it must be durable, indexed and queryable. ¶ Boot recovery deletes the slot counters, re-adopts every running row that has a checkpoint, fails rows with none as "server restarted mid-run", and only then lets the sweep run. The risk of resuming twice is a side effect that happened but wasn't journalled. So any tool call left without a result is answered "interrupted by a restart; read back before repeating any change", and an approved action that was mid-run is failed, never retried. Two instances would both re-adopt the same rows, which is why it assumed a single instance, as AiRA ran through October. In November the team's rewrite, Ananda's work, moved run ownership and slot gating onto Cosmos DB leases without Redis and deployed two regions, so on master that assumption no longer holds and leases do the fencing. ¶ The minute sweep, bounded to 8 at a time, fires due wakes and Chrome releases, expires deadlines, fixes slot-counter drift, re-fires done turns that were never announced after 60 seconds, expires approvals, and prunes checkpoints older than 90 days. Each part targets a specific way a task gets stuck. I can't point you to a production log of bugs it caught; at our beta scale it's a safety net I designed, not one I've watched fire. ¶ Approvals a day later: an ask-tier call is held as a pending row with a card, and lives eight hours by default unless the skill sets its own lifetime. If the user answers in time, the row flips from pending to consumed in one atomic update and the call runs exactly once, and orders are re-read first in case the cart changed. If it expired, the waiting task is woken with "expired". Questions the agent asks get 24 hours, and an unanswered one ends the task as ready with "no answer to" in its open items. ¶ On Temporal: durable timers, signals instead of my inbox, retries and history would come for free, and a lot of my sweep code would disappear. Harder: LLM and browser calls must become activities because workflows must be deterministic, long message checkpoints fight history size limits, and we'd run a cluster. To prove crash recovery, I'd write the test I haven't automated yet: start a task with a fake tool that blocks, kill the process mid-call, restart, and assert the row is re-adopted, the tool gets the "interrupted" reply, nothing ran twice, and the slot count matches.
  - `L2` Why did AiRA need a background runtime at all, instead of doing everything inside the chat turn?
  - `L2` Walk me through one background task from "start" to the result appearing in chat.
  - `L3` Explain AiRA's background-agent runtime: per-user 3-slot queue, Mongo state machine with checkpoints, inbox, sleep/wake, boot recovery, minute upkeep sweeps.
  - `L3` What does the inbox in that runtime hold, and why is it separate from the conversation?
  - `L3` What does boot recovery do in your runtime? What is the risk of resuming a run twice?
  - `L3` Why did you put slot gating in Redis but run state in MongoDB?
  - `L3` What does the minute upkeep sweep check, and what bugs did it catch?
  - `L3` How would you move that runtime onto Temporal? What would get simpler and what would get harder?
  - `L3` How do approvals with expiring cards work when the agent is asleep and the user answers a day later?
  - `L3` How would you prove crash recovery works? Walk me through killing the worker mid-tool-call.
- [ ] Gmail/Calendar agent flow: finding the right thread and contact, time zones, approvals before sending
  - **Answer:** In the agent runtime I built in October, a request like "reply to Ravi and set up a call next Tuesday" becomes a background task with read tools open and every send or calendar change held for the user's approval. Aira starts a connector task with the `inbox` skill, which is allowed to borrow the `calendar` skill. The task searches with `from:ravi newer_than:7d` and a limit of 10, widening only if nothing comes back, and gets summaries with each mail's `message_id`, sender, subject and links, never full bodies, and always scrubbed. It calls `reply_email` with that message id, his address as `to` and the subject. The call is held as a pending action, a fast model writes the approval card, and the task sleeps. The user approves, maybe after editing, and it runs once. The task reads back `in:sent to:ravi newer_than:1d` before it calls the reply sent. Then it borrows the calendar skill, lists Tuesday's events, and calls `insert_event` with Ravi as a guest, which is another card where the user can change the start, the end or the guests. It reads back the event, finishes with findings, and Aira tells the user. ¶ Finding the right thread and person without inventing addresses is a rule plus a mechanism. The rule: recipients come only from the user's words or the sender of a mail read in this task, and ids come from a summary in this task, never guessed. The mechanism: the reply re-reads the original's `Message-ID` and `References` headers to get the real thread, which I first got right in December 2025, so it lands in Ravi's thread and not a new one. If two Ravis match, the agent can ask the user through Aira instead of picking one. ¶ Time zones: calendar times are RFC 3339 with the user's offset, and the task's first message carries the current time in the user's zone, so 9 pm in India is written as `+05:30`. "Next Tuesday" is computed by the model from that time, and that's where it can go wrong: "next Tuesday" said on a Monday is ambiguous, a request late at night can be off by a day, and daylight saving or a user travelling shifts it. Our guard is the card: it shows the actual date and time before anything is created, and the read-back confirms it. I'd move relative-date resolution into code and show "Tuesday 18 Nov" on the card. ¶ What the email agent never does, even with approval: send or reply because a mail asked it to; add a recipient nobody named; open a link unless code re-reads it from the mail, with the host shown on the card; or use security mails like sign-in codes as anything more than "a code arrived". It also never deletes mail permanently, only moves it to trash. Mail text is treated as data. Every mail-derived string goes through a scrubber before a model sees it, because an inbox is the easiest place to plant a prompt injection.
  - `L2` Walk me through "reply to Ravi and set up a call next Tuesday" from user message to sent invite.
  - `L2` How did the Gmail agent find the right thread and the right person without inventing addresses?
  - `L3` How did you handle time zones and "next Tuesday" in the Calendar agent? Where can it go wrong?
  - `L3` What would you never let the email agent do, even with approval?

## 1.5 Lumen — realtime text-to-video (open source)
- [ ] The LLM writes a small JSON script; Lumen renders it on Canvas, frame-accurate and seekable ↳ https://github.com/Dhanesh-Pottekula/Lumen (README) · bullets.md § lumen_aira_canvas · AIE ch2 "Structured Outputs" (pdf p123–129)
  - **Answer:** A film in AiRA is a short narrated, animated explainer that AiRA makes when a question is better shown than told, like "how does your heart pump blood?", and it plays right inside the conversation on the phone. Lumen is the engine that draws it. In one sentence: Lumen is a deterministic TypeScript engine on Canvas 2D that takes a small JSON script written by an LLM and renders it as a seekable, narrated film, where **every frame is a pure function of time**. I wrote it from scratch and I'm the sole author, 64 commits. The repo is public on my GitHub under Apache 2.0; to be precise, its main branch still shows the summer version, and the current engine, the four packages and the workflows sit on my pushed feature branch, not merged yet. ¶ Why not just generate real video with a video model? Because a video model paints every pixel. It's slow, priced per second, text comes out mushy, and you can't fix one wrong label without regenerating the clip. When the model writes a script instead, it only makes the judgement calls: which pictures, what each step says, what appears on which word. The engine does the pixels. So it's cheap and fast, text is sharp at any resolution, a broken scene can be repaired as data, and the same script always draws the same frames. ¶ What's in a script: a header with the title, theme and film-wide colour categories, then scenes. Each scene has objects, like pictures with named hotspots, text, paths, charts or measures, described with closed vocabularies such as sizes, zones and roles instead of pixel numbers. It has its narration, an optional comprehension check, and beats. A beat is a list of actions like show, hide, motion, attention or label, and it can carry `say`, the narration words it starts on. So timing comes from the voice, not from the model typing seconds. ¶ "Pure function of time" means `render(ctx, t)` keeps no state between frames. No clock inside, no positions accumulated frame to frame, and randomness only from a seeded generator, film grain included. Any t always gives the same frame. That's what makes scrubbing, replay and export identical: if you jump to 1:40 you get exactly the frame you'd get by playing up to it, because nothing was accumulated on the way. MP4 export is just the same function called at fixed steps offline. ¶ Canvas vs SVG vs DOM: the frames are drawn with Canvas 2D, not SVG and not WebGL. With many things moving at once, DOM or SVG means mutating a retained tree and paying style and layout every frame. Canvas is immediate mode: clear and redraw from the function, which fits the pure-time model exactly. It also gives me blend modes and offscreen layer canvases for masks and glows, and the same code runs in Node through `@napi-rs/canvas` for tests. The downside is no free accessibility or hit-testing, so the app adds captions and a text version. `requestAnimationFrame` is the browser callback that fires just before the next repaint with a timestamp; the player uses it to work out t and draw. My loop also stops itself at the end of a scene, at a word gate, or when the page is hidden. ¶ Physics without a physics engine: everything is a closed-form function of t. A fall is y equals y0 plus half g t squared, and the bounce after landing is a sine that decays. An orbit is an angle that grows linearly with t. Spins turn about a pivot with a bounded sweep, a connector pinned to two moving ends is recomputed from those ends each frame, and particles are analytic: position comes from a seed and an age, never simulated. A stepped simulation would break seeking, because frame N would depend on frame N minus one. ¶ The main cost of the pure design is that every frame is recomputed from scratch. I handle it by pushing all the expensive work to compile time. When a scene is added, validation, layout, the placement solver and path parsing and flattening all run once; `render(t)` only evaluates cheap formulas over a flat list of draw components. Figure plans are memoised in a WeakMap, decoded pictures sit in one cache so a frame never waits for a decode, and the device pixel ratio is capped at 2 in Lumen's player and in the app's WebView bridge, because 3x phones gain nothing visible for a lot more fill. ¶ On holding 60 fps on a mid-range phone, I'll be straight: I haven't measured frame times on a cheap Android yet. Those are the levers I built in. To measure it I'd log the gaps between `requestAnimationFrame` calls inside the WebView, send a frame-time histogram back over the bridge, and run the heaviest scenes on a low-end device, looking at p95 frame time, not the average. ¶ And what can't Lumen show that a Veo-style model can? Photoreal footage, real people and faces, a camera flying through 3D space, lighting, and soft things that bend and squash, which I wrote down as the honest gap. In Lumen a picture is a still image that moves whole; the backend enforces that. For teaching a mechanism that's usually better, because a label stays sharp and stuck to the right part. For "show me what swimming through a coral reef looks like", a video model wins, and I'd rather find real footage than fake it.
  - `L1` What is a "film" in AiRA and why not generate real video with a video model?
  - `L1` What is Lumen in one sentence? ↳ deterministic Canvas 2D engine; LLM-written JSON → seekable narrated film
  - `L1` What is Lumen? ↳ your Canvas 2D film engine, pure function of time
  - `L1` What is Lumen, in one sentence?
  - `L1` Why make the LLM write a JSON script instead of generating video pixels? ↳ cost, speed, sharp text, editable, seekable, deterministic
  - `L1` What does a Lumen script contain? ↳ scenes, elements, motions, narration beats, timing
  - `L1` What does "every frame is a pure function of time" mean?
  - `L1` Canvas 2D vs SVG vs DOM for animation: why Canvas?
  - `L1` What is `requestAnimationFrame`?
  - `L1` What draws Lumen's frames: Canvas, SVG or WebGL?
  - `L1` Why make the LLM output a script instead of generating pixels/video?
  - `L2` What does "every frame is a pure function of time" buy you? ↳ scrub, replay, export identically
  - `L2` Why does a pure function of time make scrubbing, replay and export identical? ↳ no accumulated state; any t always renders the same frame
  - `L2` How do you get physics-like motion (falls, bounces, orbits) without stepping a physics engine? ↳ closed-form functions of t
  - `L2` Why make the LLM output a script instead of pixels or video (Lumen)?
  - `L3` Veo-style video costs far more. What can't Lumen show that a real video model can?
  - `L3` How do you keep 60 fps on a mid-range phone?
  - `L3` What is the main cost of the pure-function design, and how do you handle it? ↳ recomputing per frame; memoise per scene, cache paths
- [ ] Playing while still being generated (streaming JSON parsing, buffering) ↳ bullets.md § lumen_aira_canvas (streaming scene-by-scene compiler) + § aira-api_s5 (half-streamed JSON parsing) · HOL ch6 "Grammar: Constrained Sampling" (pdf p216–220)
  - **Answer:** It plays while it's still being generated because everything is scene by scene: the backend releases scenes in order as they're ready, the phone appends each one to a live compiler, and the player only ever waits at the frontier. ¶ Half-streamed JSON is handled on the backend, not in Lumen. The planner streams one JSON object with its keys in a fixed order, title, terms, categories, pictures, steps, so pictures close first and start drawing while the steps are still being written. An incremental reader in `film_ent.py` tracks quotes, escapes and brace depth, and decodes an entry only once its braces have closed. It handles real edge cases I hit, like a stray `]` before `, {` and a spare brace, and an early version raised an exception on every chunk until I made it check that the entry had closed first. A malformed entry is skipped and named in a `refused` list instead of failing the film. A scene writer's reply that isn't one JSON object gets one repair turn, so Lumen's `compiler.add` only ever sees whole scenes. ¶ Why can an already-added scene never change? Because each scene is laid out from the state the earlier kept scenes left on screen: where pictures sit, how big they are, which labels are up. If scene 2 could change after scene 3 compiled, scene 3's layout would be wrong and the timeline would move under the playhead. So the compiler is append-only. Adding a scene compiles only that scene and appends its time window, and a scene that can't be drawn keeps its index with zero time. I have a test that adds scenes one by one, asserts each scene's timing never changes afterwards, and asserts the whole-film compile gives exactly the same timings as the streamed one. ¶ Out-of-order scenes on the phone: each one arrives as an `omni.film_scene` frame with its index. The client store keeps a contiguous list. An index below its length is ignored, so a known index is never replayed. The next index is appended, and any parked successors drain in behind it. A higher index is parked and triggers a resync: after 250 ms the client fetches the film with every scene released so far, retrying every 2 seconds up to five times. I didn't send scenes as Lang deltas, because a Lang statement is replaced whole and the film would be re-sent with every scene. ¶ Seeking: the player moves by scene, and by time inside a scene, using the engine's own scene windows, and a time is clamped inside the film compiled so far. If you go to a scene that hasn't arrived, the player enters `buffering`, the only state that shows a loader, and holds the frame until the scene lands; once the backend says the film is ready, a missing scene means you've reached the end. It stays frame-accurate because earlier scene windows are frozen; new scenes only extend the timeline at the end. ¶ The honest weak spot is time to first scene. In one timed run of film-lab, my local replica of the pipeline, on a heart film, planning streamed for about 130 seconds, the first scene in order was ready at about 159 seconds and the last at about 246. The lab builds everything before playing, so that's not a measurement of AiRA's streaming, but with in-order release it means playback could start about a minute and a half before the film finishes. Still, two and a half minutes to the first scene is the number I'd attack next, by getting the opening step planned and written first.
  - `L2` Scenes arrive out of order on the phone. How does the client cope? ↳ scene store parks out-of-order scenes, never replays known index
  - `L2` How does a video start playing while it's still being generated? ↳ scene-by-scene compile, scenes appended, player waits at the frontier
  - `L2` Why can an already-added scene never change? ↳ later scenes compile from the state earlier ones left on screen
  - `L2` The user seeks to a time whose scene hasn't arrived yet. What happens?
  - `L2` How is seeking frame-accurate if the content is still arriving?
  - `L2` In Lumen, the model writes a JSON scene script. How did you handle invalid or half-streamed JSON?
- [ ] Cost: ~$1.60 per 3-minute video vs ~$79 with Veo — how you measured it ↳ career/profile/resume.tex (AiRA text-to-video bullet) · AIE ch4 "Cost and Latency" (pdf p201–203) · https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/pricing (Veo price)
  - **Answer:** About $1.60 for a roughly three-minute film, and I want to be precise about what that number is: an estimate from one instrumented run, not production billing. ¶ Here's the arithmetic. I ran a film, "How does your heart pump blood around your body?", through film-lab, my local replica of AiRA's film pipeline with the same prompts, models and rules, which logs token usage for every call. The film came out 198 seconds long, 7 scenes and 10 pictures. Across the planner, the seven scene writers and three corrective retries it used about 479,000 input tokens and about 18,000 output tokens, reasoning included. At the planner model's price of $2 in and $10 out per million, from third-party price lists, that's about 96 cents plus 18 cents. Ten pictures at 4.5 cents each, the list price for AiRA's Gemini Flash image model at 512 pixels, is 45 cents. Total, about $1.59, so I say about $1.60. ¶ What's inside and what isn't. The planner is the small part, about 41,000 input tokens. The bulk is the scene writers: each one sees about 43,000 tokens of catalog and rules, and ten writer and retry calls account for about 438,000 of the 479,000. Left out: the eight part-finding vision calls, which weren't logged; narration, which in AiRA is Cartesia speech made per scene, while the lab used a different TTS; image search; and the conversation turn that commissioned the film. The lab also drew with OpenAI's image model, because it had no Gemini key, so the picture line is what AiRA would pay, not what the lab paid. On the other side, I gave no credit for prompt caching. The writers share a cache key and an identical 43,000-token prefix, so the real bill should come in lower. So I say "about" on purpose. ¶ The $79 for Veo is not a measurement either; it's a list-price comparison. Veo 3.1 was listed at about 40 cents a second when I checked, and 198 seconds times 40 cents is about $79. That's all it is: list-price arithmetic on this film's length. A flat 180 seconds would be $72, and I never generated a Veo version to compare quality or real billing. On top of that, a video model makes short clips, so a three-minute film is many clips stitched together, with consistency problems between them. So roughly fifty times cheaper, at list prices, for this kind of explainer. ¶ If I were to measure it properly, I'd do it in production, not the lab. Every model call in a film run is already stored as an iteration on the run row with its stage and timing. I'd add token usage and the image and speech calls to that, keep a per-model price table, sum it per film, and report cost per film minute at p50 and p95 across real films, with cached tokens counted at the cached price.
  - `L1` About what does one 3-minute Lumen video cost?
  - `L2` "About $1.60 per 3-minute video." Show me the arithmetic. ↳ estimate; break into planner tokens, image gen, inspector, scene writers, TTS
  - `L2` About $1.60 per 3-minute video vs about $79 with a video model: how did you measure it? ↳ LLM tokens + TTS + images per film vs Veo per-second price
  - `L2` Redo your Lumen numbers: how did a 3-minute video come to about $1.60 versus about $79 with Veo?
- [ ] 17 animation modules across 4 packages — the package boundaries and why ↳ https://github.com/Dhanesh-Pottekula/Lumen · bullets.md § lumen_aira_canvas (17 modules, four npm packages) · DDIA ch1 "Evolvability: making change easy" (pdf p41–42)
  - **Answer:** Lumen had 17 animation primitive modules in its library, and it's packaged as 4 npm packages. ¶ The 17 modules all landed in one commit in July, and every one is a pure function of time or progress. Motion has easing, springs, stagger and a seeded random generator. Strokes and stroke verbs do draw-on along an arc-length window, erase, traced paths, arrowheads and a hand-drawn wobble. Reveal does wipes, iris, blinds and dissolves, with soft edges from an offscreen mask. Focus does dim-everything-except, spotlight, highlight rings and a magnifier. Callout places labels with leader lines in eight directions. Sequence does progressive disclosure and predict-then-reveal. Type motion is kinetic type: counters, typewriter, word reveal. Then particles, which are analytic rather than simulated, a camera with pan and log-interpolated zoom, charts, timelines, shape morphing that resamples and aligns two outlines before interpolating, icons, a small canvas math typesetter I chose over KaTeX to stay offline and deterministic, map projection with borders over time, and the first JSON storyboard format. The library has moved on since: the storyboard format was replaced by the validated JSON compiler, and figures, physics diagrams and cards were added. So 17 is the original primitive set. ¶ The four packages are split by dependency. `@aira/lumen` is the core: schema, validation, compiler, canvas renderer and the capability catalog, with no dependency on React, Cartesia, an MP4 muxer or any LLM SDK. `@aira/lumen-react` is the player component with play, pause, seek, captions and audio sync. `@aira/lumen-cartesia` is optional narration: speech with word timestamps, a local IndexedDB cache and alignment to scenes. `@aira/lumen-mp4` is the optional in-browser exporter. The rule is simple: anything that needs a vendor key, a UI framework or a browser-only API is an optional adapter, and the core stays pure, so it runs in Node, a browser or a phone WebView. ¶ Why it matters in practice: AiRA ships only the core, bundled into one file inside a WebView, with no React DOM there and narration handled by our own backend, while someone on the web can use React plus Cartesia. Both the player and the exporter call one shared frame function, so playing and exporting can't drift apart. The weak spot is that the four packages are versioned together at 0.1.0-beta.1 and none is on npm yet; the split lives on my pushed feature branch, while the repo's main branch still shows the earlier single-app layout.
  - `L1` How many animation modules and npm packages does Lumen have?
  - `L2` Name some of the 17 animation modules and what they do. ↳ draw-on strokes, reveal masks, callouts, kinetic type, charts, camera, morphing
  - `L2` Why 4 npm packages? What is the boundary of each?
- [ ] Compile pipeline: JSON Schema with AJV, semantic checks, typed path diagnostics, validate-and-repair, dropping undrawable scenes
  - **Answer:** JSON Schema is a standard way to describe the shape of JSON: types, required fields, enums and ranges. AJV is the fast JavaScript validator that compiles a schema into a validation function. I run it on the 2020-12 draft in strict mode, with all-errors on, so a scene gets every problem at once instead of one at a time. ¶ The pipeline, per scene: decode; the AJV schema check; semantic checks a schema can't express, like duplicate ids, unknown targets, placement cycles, and lifecycle rules such as acting on something before it's shown; a layout resolver that turns words like sizes and zones into real boxes on a 540 by 960 portrait view; compile into a flat list of draw components with entrances, exits and motions; canonical geometry checks, like refusing an orbit whose first frame would jump from where the object rests; then repair, or refuse the scene. In AiRA it runs in strict mode, one scene at a time, as scenes stream in. ¶ "Validate and repair" has three outcomes. Fixed: only where exactly one reading exists. A field that can only mean one thing gets corrected, objects are auto-fitted and clamped into the safe frame, overlaps are pushed apart, and a picture that would be too small to read is raised to the smallest size that reads. Trimmed: an unknown word, object or action is dropped with a `DROPPED_*` warning, along with everything that named it, and a motion that would go nowhere is refused while the rest of the scene plays. Rejected: schema errors, broken references, lifecycle errors and motion geometry that can't be repaired drop the whole scene. The lenient ones are lenient for a practical reason: refusing a scene over one misspelt word blanks part of a film. ¶ What should never be auto-repaired is anything with more than one reading, or where the repair changes meaning. In the geometry repair step, anything that can't be fixed one unambiguous way is fatal for that scene rather than guessed. The backend follows the same rule: if a picture lost a part, a reference to that part is dropped, never widened to the whole picture, because a label meant for the left ventricle pointing at the whole heart teaches something false. Wrong and confident is worse than missing. ¶ Why one undrawable scene doesn't break the film: compiling is per scene, a dropped scene keeps its index with zero time on screen, and only kept scenes pass layout state forward, so a dropped one changes nothing for the others. The rest plays. ¶ Typed, path-addressed diagnostics mean every problem comes back as code, path, message and received value, like `UNKNOWN_TARGET` at a specific beat and action. With those, code can drop exactly that action, decide blocking or not from the code, and give the scene writer one corrective retry that names each reason and the exact ids it may use. One error string can't be acted on that precisely. And invalid JSON mid-stream never reaches this layer: the backend only decodes entries whose braces have closed, and a writer reply that isn't one JSON object gets one repair turn first. ¶ The paint recorder catches what validation can't, because validation checks what the model wrote and the recorder checks what the engine actually drew.
