It records every paint on the layer canvases with the engine function and component that made it, then flags paint nobody asked for, glows in the wrong place, labels far from their thing, collisions with a picture's silhouette, motions that contradict their statement, jumps between scenes and stale leftovers. All of that is valid JSON. ¶ And the flash limiter: WCAG 2.3.1 says content shouldn't flash more than three times a second, because of seizure risk for people with photosensitive epilepsy. A model told to "emphasise" can stack pulses, so I enforced it in the engine, not the prompt: no burst within a third of a second of the previous one, and glows never fade faster than 0.3 seconds or grow past a size cap. A prompt rule is a suggestion; an engine rule always holds.
  - `L1` What is JSON Schema, and what does AJV do?
  - `L2` How does the compile pipeline handle invalid LLM output? ↳ schema → semantic checks → layout → geometry repair; fix, trim or reject scene
  - `L2` One scene is undrawable. Why doesn't the whole film break? ↳ scene-by-scene compile; drop it, keep index
  - `L2` Walk me through the compile pipeline. ↳ AJV strict → semantic checks → layout → components → geometry checks + repair
  - `L2` What does "validate and repair" mean? What gets fixed, trimmed or rejected?
  - `L2` Why typed, path-addressed diagnostics instead of one error string? ↳ the exact field can be fixed or fed back to the model
  - `L2` How do you handle invalid JSON from the model mid-stream?
  - `L3` The model writes a scene that can't be drawn. What happens to the film? ↳ drop that scene, keep its index, the rest plays
  - `L3` Which errors should you never auto-repair, and why?
  - `L3` What did the paint recorder catch that schema validation could not? ↳ stray paint, misplaced glows, collisions, contradictory motions
  - `L3` Why a WCAG 2.3.1 flash limiter? ↳ no more than 3 flashes a second; seizure risk
- [ ] AiRA image films: streaming planner, picture lanes, vision inspector hotspots, scene writers, word-synced narration
  - **Answer:** The main model writes one line, `Film(title, brief)`, in its screen program, and everything after that is a background worker that streams scenes to the phone. I built this pipeline in October, after the drawn-SVG version failed; what I describe is my branch through October, and the team's November rewrite carried it over into the new codebase. ¶ The flow: the phone sees the Film statement and commissions it, and the agent manager starts a film worker. A streaming planner, OpenAI's Sol model at medium reasoning, loads its prompt layers and then writes one JSON object: title, terms, categories, the pictures needed, then the steps, each with its narration and the exact screen. As each picture entry closes, a picture lane starts. It either finds a real photo through image search or draws one with Gemini's Flash image model at 512 pixels on a flat ground, in the style of the film's first drawing. Pillow and numpy cut the subject out, it's uploaded to blob storage, and a vision model finds the named parts. Once a step's pictures are measured, its scene writer runs, with four lanes each for drawing and writing. An assembler, all in code, holds each scene to the engine's rules, keeps continuity between scenes, allows one corrective retry, and publishes scenes strictly in order. ¶ Labels on parts of a generated picture: first, part names are never sent to the image model, because a map told "serbia" came back with SERBIA written on it. The vision model returns boxes as fractions, missing parts and which way the subject faces. The outlines are traced in code, not by the model: flood fill from the box centre over its commonest colours, then a Moore-neighbour border walk resampled to 64 points. Those become named hotspots and outlines on the picture in Lumen, so a label, an arrow, a dimmed hole or a tint targets `heart.left-ventricle` and rides with the picture when it moves. In the engine, one placement solver keeps labels off the real drawn outlines, and the path layer gives drawn shapes named corners and sides. ¶ Word sync: each beat names the narration words it starts on with `say`. The engine turns that into a word index and emits per-word cue times for each scene. On the phone, each scene's narration plays, the app tracks how many words have actually been heard, and the player gates on that: it holds the picture at the next cue until that word is spoken. If the voice pauses, the picture waits. Earlier versions aligned Cartesia word timestamps to captions with a piecewise time remap; estimated timings drifted, so I moved to gating on heard words. ¶ The planner's three prompt layers: a generic rules file that's always loaded, with the priority right kind of visual at believable sizes first, right pictures second, motion last. Exactly one of four narratives: explain, how-to, story or weigh. And one to four of 19 visual skills named for the shape of the idea, like mechanism, cause and consequence, change over time, parts and structure, size and amount. The planner picks them with a single load call before it writes anything. These replaced nine subject skills like biology and physics, because the subject doesn't tell you how to show something; the shape of the idea does. ¶ Why model-drawn SVG failed, which I wrote up in a 418-line research doc: a model writing SVG can't see its own output, coordinates eat its token budget, it doesn't know paint order so shapes hide each other, and curves are the worst. With only nine ready-made drawings, the model fell back to hand-writing whole SVGs; in one gravity film, six of twelve objects were hand-drawn. Circles survived; an onion, a hand and a knife didn't. So now the image model draws, a vision model measures, the engine owns positions, and the LLM only makes calls about meaning. The cost is more model calls and latency, and pictures that move only whole. The narration's emotion tags came from my teammate Ananda's TTS work, which I merged into the planner.
  - `L2` Walk me through the image film pipeline from `Film(title, brief)` to scenes on the phone. ↳ streaming planner → picture lanes → inspector → scene writers → assembler
  - `L2` How do labels and arrows attach to parts of an AI-generated image? ↳ vision inspector boxes, outlines, named hotspots
  - `L2` How is narration synced word by word with motion? ↳ beats name start words; engine emits per-word cue times
  - `L2` What are the three prompt layers for the planner? ↳ generic rules + one narrative + 1–4 of 19 visual skills
  - `L2` Why did model-drawn SVG fail, so that you moved to image-based films?
  - `L2` How do labels and arrows attach to parts of a generated picture? ↳ named hotspots, outlines, SVG path geometry with named sides
  - `L2` How is narration synced to the words on screen? ↳ beats name their start words; engine emits per-word cue times
- [ ] Keeping prompts in sync with the engine and judging quality: generated catalog, drift checks, placement solver, film report
  - **Answer:** Two different answers here: for films the prompt is generated from the engine and the build fails on drift; for the screen UI language the generator lives in the app, and keeping it in step across repos is still partly manual. ¶ Films first. Lumen's schema is the single source of truth. A catalog module builds the scene writer's catalog from it, one entry per kind, verb, placement and composition, reading types, limits and enum values straight from the schema, and it refuses to render if anything lacks a description. When I rebuild the engine, one bundler script builds it for the app and writes that catalog into the backend as the film prompt, so the engine the phone runs and the catalog the writers read come out of the same run. On the way it strikes out withheld vocabulary, then checks that no withheld name survived, and throws, writing nothing, if one did. It also throws if fewer entries were struck than expected, which catches the schema changing shape. To be exact about "can't drift": that holds for the generated catalog. It's a script I run, not a CI gate, and the hand-written planner and step prompts around it are prose that can still mention things. When I rebuilt it for film-lab in October, the regenerated catalog came out byte-identical to the API's copy. ¶ Why fail the build? Because of what happens when the model keeps using a feature you removed. My lesson was that prohibitions in prose only teach the vocabulary. If the catalog lists a word and says "don't use this", the model uses it. So the fix is three layers: strike it from the contract the model reads, have the validator drop it with a warning if it still appears, and fail the build if it survives in the catalog. A real case: once the engine started moving older pictures aside on its own, the `aside` verb was withheld from the model. ¶ The placement solver: one solver places a box above, below, beside, near, on or inside a referent, avoiding the real drawn outlines and strokes, not bounding boxes, staying inside the frame and taking the nearest free spot first. Labels go on the shape if they fit, else beside it, else into a tidy pointer column like a key. The same solver picks motion end points and where a character's speech line goes. Everything is sized for a 540 by 960 portrait phone view, with a minimum text size of about 12 points on a phone and a 4.5 to 1 contrast floor. ¶ Readable on a phone: a film report script renders every beat of real films at phone scale and measures text size, contrast of the text's ink against the worst background pixel behind it, since WCAG judges the least contrast, plus crowding, sync and pacing. It can compare two engine builds. Next to it, the paint recorder checks what was actually drawn. ¶ Does a film actually teach? Honestly, we haven't measured that with users. Today's proxies are a plan lint that stores about 25 counts per plan, like terms never spoken or words on screen the voice never says, plus the film report and the paint checks. I'd measure first-try answers on each scene's comprehension check, the scene where people drop off, rewinds per scene as a confusion signal, and follow-up questions that re-ask the same thing. At scale, without watching every film: run the automatic checks on every film, send Lumen's deterministic keyframes to a vision judge with a rubric calibrated against a small human-rated set, have people review the lowest scorers, and track all of it per prompt version. ¶ On the UI side it's weaker. In September I wrote a TypeScript-compiler script in the app that reads each component's signature and props and fails on a mismatch, and my teammate Sahad later extended it to check the parser's own table. But the catalog the backend actually teaches from, `ui_catalog.md`, is a prose file several of us hand-edit in the API repo, so the two can drift. I'd close that with an API CI check that compares its signature lines against the app's component table.
  - `L2` How do you keep the prompt in sync with what the engine supports? ↳ build generates prompt catalog from the live schema; fails on drift
  - `L2` How does the placement solver decide where a label goes? ↳ avoids drawn outlines; 540×960 view, 12 pt text, 4.5:1 contrast
  - `L2` How do you check a film is readable on a phone? ↳ film report: text size, contrast, crowding, pacing
  - `L3` How do you know a film actually teaches well? What would you measure?
  - `L3` The bundler fails the build if withheld vocabulary survives in the prompt catalog. Why? ↳ prompts can never drift from the engine schema
  - `L3` The model keeps using a feature you removed. What do you do? ↳ strike it from the prompt, validator rejects, build fails if it survives
  - `L3` How would you evaluate film quality at scale without watching every film?
- [ ] Packaging, export and release: one-IIFE WebView bundle, WebCodecs MP4 export, CI checks, provenance-signed npm, golden frames
  - **Answer:** The engine reaches the React Native app as one minified file inside a WebView. A bundler builds the core package with esbuild into a single IIFE that exposes a global `Lumen`, and writes it into the app as a string constant. The app loads it into a WebView shell whose content security policy allows no network at all, images only as data URLs and inline script only, and talks to it through a small bridge: set the header, append scenes, hand over pictures, sync a scene, report heard words. The engine never fetches anything, so the app prefetches pictures and passes them in as data URLs. One honest catch: the policy allows `unsafe-eval`, because AJV compiles its validators with `new Function`; precompiling them at build time would remove that. The backend never runs the engine at all; only the generated catalog crosses into the API. ¶ MP4 export is in the open-source playground, not the phone app. It renders every frame offline at a fixed frame rate with the same frame function the player uses, encodes video with WebCodecs' `VideoEncoder` as H.264 and the narration with `AudioEncoder` as AAC, and muxes them with mp4-muxer. It runs faster than real time, it's deterministic rather than a screen capture, and picture and voice stay in sync because they come from one timeline. The limit is browser support for WebCodecs encoding. ¶ Before a release, CI runs typecheck, build, a public-API check that compares exports against a snapshot you have to update on purpose, a package-content audit so only allowed files go into the tarball, an install smoke test from a locally packed build, `npm audit`, and gitleaks for committed secrets. Two honest gaps: the engine's own test scripts aren't in CI, I run them locally; and the workflows live on my pushed feature branch, not on main yet, so I'd call CI wired rather than proven. ¶ Provenance: the release workflow publishes with `npm publish --provenance` from GitHub Actions using an OIDC token, so npm attaches a Sigstore-signed statement that this tarball was built from this repo, this commit and this workflow. The attack it stops is someone with a stolen npm token publishing a tampered version from their own machine: it won't carry valid provenance, and users can check with `npm audit signatures`. It doesn't stop malicious code committed to the repo or a compromised dependency. To be clear, the workflow is wired but nothing has been published; npm has no `@aira/lumen` package today. It only runs by manual dispatch from main, and the AiRA work is on a feature branch. ¶ Testing a rendering engine: determinism is what makes it testable. Today the tests are Node scripts that render with `@napi-rs/canvas`. One compiles probe lessons and checks layout and visibility. A path suite of about 2,500 lines checks properties, like a label placed next to a part landing next to that part, and that compiling scene by scene gives exactly the same timings and layout as compiling the whole film. The film report measures contrast on real rendered pixels and can compare two builds, and the paint recorder checks every draw call. What I don't have yet is golden frames: PNG snapshots at fixed times, compared with a small tolerance. The engine already picks deterministic keyframes, and the seeded randomness makes snapshots stable, so that's the next thing I'd add, along with fuzzing scenes from the schema.
  - `L2` How does MP4 export work in the browser? ↳ WebCodecs H.264 + AAC, mp4-muxer, frames rendered offline
  - `L2` How does the engine get into a React Native app? ↳ bundled into one minified IIFE, run inside a WebView
  - `L3` What does CI check before a release? ↳ typecheck, build, public-API check, package audit, install smoke test, npm audit, gitleaks
  - `L3` What is a provenance-signed npm release, and which attack does it stop?
  - `L3` How do you test a rendering engine? ↳ golden frames at fixed t, paint diffs, determinism checks

## 1.6 Jigsaw — hiring platform
- [ ] AI candidate matching (10,000+ recommended): embedding/ranking pipeline, how a match score is built ↳ career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw) · HOL ch2 "Embeddings for Recommendation Systems" (pdf p89–93) · HOL ch8 "Dense Retrieval" (pdf p250–262)
  - **Answer:** At Jigsaw I built and scaled two AI pipelines, candidate recommendation and job extraction. Over my time there, August 2024 to July 2025, the recommender recommended 10,000+ candidates and the extractor processed 2,000+ jobs. I'd say plainly that those are recommendations made, not hires. Jigsaw's product covered the whole hire: hiring companies and their recruiters posting jobs and running interviews, video assessments and chat, and candidates applying or being referred. [FILL: one line on who Jigsaw's customers were and roughly how big the candidate pool was.] Recommendation means a job goes in and a ranked shortlist of candidates from our pool comes out. [FILL: what the recruiter actually saw per candidate: a score, a reason, or just the ranked order.] ¶ An embedding is a model turning text into a vector so that things that mean the same land close together. That's why it fits matching. A job asking for a frontend engineer with React and a profile that says built UIs in Next.js share few exact keywords, but their vectors sit near each other, and keyword search alone would miss that. The trade-off is that an embedding is fuzzy: it knows topic well and hard facts like years of experience or city badly. ¶ How the score was built: [FILL: the embedding model, where the vectors lived, and whether the score was pure similarity, keyword rules, an LLM, or a blend, with the weights if there were any]. The shape I'd defend on a whiteboard is filter first, then rank. The new job is parsed into fields by the extraction pipeline. Hard constraints like location, experience band and notice period are filters, not similarity, and they belong inside the vector query as metadata filters, so you never rank someone who can't take the job and never filter after a top-k and end up with three results. Then you embed the job's requirement text, compare it with the stored candidate vectors, blend that similarity with explicit signals such as must-have skills present and experience fit, attach a short why, matched skills and missing ones, and treat recruiter shortlist or reject as feedback. ¶ Batch versus on request: [FILL: whether recommendations were computed in a batch job or on request, and why]. The trade-off I'd explain is that candidate pools change slowly and jobs arrive a few at a time, so computing the shortlist when a job is created or changed and caching it keeps the recruiter's screen instant and the model cost bounded. On request you only read the cached list and apply fresh filters, like dropping someone who was just hired. ¶ How do I know it was good? I don't have a precision number to quote, so I won't make one up. [FILL: how quality was actually judged at Jigsaw: recruiter feedback, spot checks against manual shortlists, or nothing formal.] The way to prove it is a labelled set of past jobs with the candidates recruiters actually shortlisted, then precision at 5 and at 10 against it; live, the recruiter accept rate on recommendations; and a side-by-side against a manual shortlist for the same job. Bias I'd check the same way. Strip names, photos, gender and obvious proxies from what gets embedded, compare selection rates across groups on that labelled set, and run counterfactuals: swap the name or college on one profile and the score shouldn't move. ¶ If a recruiter says the top 5 are always wrong for senior roles, I'd pull those jobs and look at the parts of the score. The usual cause is that embeddings match on skills, not seniority, so a junior with the same stack scores as high. The fixes are seniority as a hard filter or a strong feature, a check that the extractor actually reads the experience range from senior posts, and a small labelled senior set kept as a regression test. ¶ Freshness: when a profile changes, I'd put an event on a queue and have a worker re-embed it and upsert by candidate id with a content hash, so a retry or a duplicate event is a no-op and an unchanged profile isn't re-embedded, and I'd mark any cached shortlist that includes that candidate as stale. Switching embedding models is a migration, not an update, because vectors from two models can't be compared. Build a new index next to the old one, backfill in batches, write new updates to both, shadow-compare top-k overlap and accept rates on a sample of jobs, then flip reads behind a flag and keep the old index until you're sure. ¶ A re-ranker goes after retrieval. Take the top 50 to 100 from the vector search and rescore each job and candidate pair with a cross-encoder or an LLM judge. A cross-encoder is one forward pass per pair, so cost grows with k. An LLM judge takes seconds and real money per job. Both belong in the job-created batch path, not on a click. ¶ With today's models I'd use hybrid retrieval, keyword plus dense, then an LLM re-ranker that has to quote the lines of the resume behind its verdict. For trust, I'd show the why next to every score: matched must-haves, missing ones, the experience fit. I'd show bands like strong, possible, stretch instead of a fake-precise 87 percent, and let recruiters correct a match so it becomes training and eval data.
  - `L1` What did Jigsaw build, and who were the users? ↳ recruiters, candidates, hiring companies
  - `L1` What does "AI candidate recommendation" mean in your system? What goes in and what comes out?
  - `L1` What is an embedding, and why use one to match candidates to jobs?
  - `L1` How many candidates did your matching recommend? How many jobs were extracted?
  - `L2` Walk me through the matching pipeline, from a new job to a ranked candidate list. ↳ parse, filter, embed/score, rank, explain, recruiter feedback
  - `L2` How was a match score built: embeddings, keyword rules, an LLM, or a mix?
  - `L2` Hard filters (location, experience, notice period) vs soft similarity: where does each go? ↳ filter first, then rank; metadata filters inside the vector query
  - `L2` 10,000+ recommendations: did they come from a batch job or on request? Why?
  - `L2` How do you know the matching was good? How did you evaluate it? ↳ labelled set, precision@k, recruiter accept rate, compare with manual
  - `L2` How do you evaluate whether candidate matching is good?
  - `L2` How did you use embeddings for candidate matching at Jigsaw? How was a match score built?
  - `L3` How would you check a candidate ranker for bias?
  - `L3` A recruiter says "the top 5 are always wrong for senior roles". How do you debug it?
  - `L3` How do you keep recommendations fresh when a candidate updates their profile? ↳ re-embed on change, queue, idempotent upsert
  - `L3` You switch embedding models. How do you migrate all stored vectors without downtime? ↳ new index, backfill, shadow compare, switch reads
  - `L3` How would you add a re-ranker (cross-encoder or LLM judge), and what does it cost in latency?
  - `L3` How did matching work at Jigsaw, and what would you change with today's models?
  - `L3` How would you design Jigsaw's AI candidate matching so recruiters trust the score?
- [ ] Job extraction (2,000+ jobs, 400+ hours saved): extracting structured fields from job posts with LLMs ↳ career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw) · AIE ch2 "Structured Outputs" (pdf p123–129) · HOL ch6 "Output Verification" (pdf p213–220)
  - **Answer:** Job extraction takes a job post as it arrives and turns it into the structured record the rest of the system runs on, because matching filters and ranks on those fields. At Jigsaw it processed 2,000+ jobs. [FILL: the fields you actually extracted, for example title, location and remote or not, experience range, must-have and nice-to-have skills, employment type, salary.] The rule I'd hold to for any of them is that salary and years are only filled when the post states them. ¶ How to make an LLM reliable here: [FILL: the model and the validation approach you actually used, and what happened when an output failed]. The pattern I'd describe, and would build again, is to not trust the model's text. Ask for output against a JSON schema and validate against the same schema in code. If validation fails, retry once with the validation error in the prompt. If it still fails, keep what parsed, set the rest to null, and send the job to a human review queue instead of guessing. For invented values the rule is grounding: a salary or a number of years is only accepted if that number actually appears in the source text, otherwise it's null. A model filling an empty salary field is the classic failure, and a cheap substring check catches most of it. ¶ Normalising inputs: [FILL: which formats you actually received, PDFs, HTML pages or plain text, and how you turned them into text]. The general approach is to get everything to clean text first: the text layer out of PDFs, HTML stripped of navigation and boilerplate down to the main content, whitespace collapsed, length capped. After that one prompt and one schema serve every source, and a hash of the normalised text gives you exact de-duplication for free. Near duplicates, the same job reposted, match on title, company and location or on embedding similarity above a threshold. Candidate profiles de-duplicate on email, phone or LinkedIn URL. [FILL: how duplicates were actually handled at Jigsaw, if they were.] ¶ The 400+ hours on my resume is an estimate, not a timesheet, and the resume counts it as manual screening saved across the pipelines. It's the manual minutes a person would spend per item times the volume. [FILL: the per-item minutes you assumed, and which work is counted: job entry, candidate screening, or both.] For scale, if it were all job extraction, 400 hours over 2,000 jobs is 12 minutes a job, which is why I'd state the real assumption out loud rather than let the number stand alone. ¶ Cost: I don't have a per-extraction figure to quote. [FILL: the per-extraction cost or monthly LLM spend, if you know it.] The levers are clear though, and they're how I'd make it 10 times cheaper. Use a small model for the easy majority and escalate to a bigger one only when validation fails. Trim the input to the main content. Cache by content hash so a reposted job is never extracted twice. Use a provider's batch API when nothing is waiting on the answer. Pull easy fields like 3 to 5 years with a regex before the model sees the text. ¶ Personal data: job posts are mostly public, but resumes are personal data, so only the fields extraction needs should leave the building. Email, phone and address get masked before the call and joined back by id afterwards, and the provider has to be on terms that don't train on or keep the data. [FILL: exactly what you sent to the provider at Jigsaw and what you masked.] ¶ If I designed it fresh: a versioned schema; normalise, then extract, then validate, retry and ground; a review queue for failures; and a gold set of a few hundred hand-labelled posts with field-level accuracy, run on every prompt or model change, plus a weekly spot check of live output.
  - `L1` What is job extraction? Which fields did you pull out of a job post?
  - `L2` How did you extract structured fields with an LLM reliably? ↳ JSON schema, validation, retries, fallbacks, spot checks
  - `L2` "2,000+ jobs extracted, 400+ hours saved": how did you compute the hours? ↳ manual minutes per job × volume; say it's an estimate
  - `L2` The LLM returns invalid JSON or invents a salary. What does your pipeline do?
  - `L2` Job posts arrive as PDFs, HTML pages and plain text. How do you normalise them?
  - `L2` What did one extraction cost, and how did you keep LLM cost down? ↳ smaller model, batching, caching, trimmed prompts
  - `L2` How did you handle duplicate jobs and duplicate candidate profiles?
  - `L3` Resumes are personal data. What did you send to the LLM provider, and what did you mask?
  - `L3` How did Jigsaw's job extraction work, and how would you make it 10× cheaper?
  - `L3` Design an extraction pipeline for job posts (Jigsaw): schema, validation, retries, accuracy checks.
- [ ] Reports 12 min → 30 s: what was slow (N+1? no index? in-app aggregation?) and the fix ↳ DDIA ch3 "Aggregation: Data cubes and materialized views" (pdf p120–122) · https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/using-explain.html · https://use-the-index-luke.com
  - **Answer:** A Jigsaw report went from 12 minutes to 30 seconds, about 24 times faster, and the lesson was measure first, then fix the real cause. [FILL: what the report contained and who read it: which entities, which period, which users.] Twelve minutes hurts because someone sits waiting for it, and a request that long also runs past normal HTTP and proxy timeouts. ¶ Two terms first. An N+1 query is when code fetches a list with one query and then runs one more query per row, so 1,000 rows means 1,001 round-trips. An explain plan is the database telling you how it will run a query: index scan or full scan, rows examined, join strategy and where the time goes. ¶ Finding the time: [FILL: what you measured first, step timings, query logs, EXPLAIN or counting queries]. The order I'd go in is to time each step of the report, count the queries one run makes from the query log, and run EXPLAIN on the heavy ones, which splits the 12 minutes into round-trips, slow single queries and time spent in the app. The cause and the fix: [FILL: the real one, per-row lookups batched into one query, a missing index added (name the columns and their order), aggregation moved into the database (which GROUP BY or pipeline), or a mix, with the round-trips before versus after and whether rows were streamed or loaded]. That's the before-and-after I'd draw on a whiteboard. ¶ Why moving aggregation into the database wins over doing it in Node: the database aggregates where the data lives, uses its indexes, and sends back a few summary rows instead of every raw row over the network to be looped over in JavaScript. One GROUP BY, or an aggregation pipeline, replaces thousands of round-trips. App code is still right when the logic is genuinely hard to express in a query or needs data from another system. Indexes go equality columns first, then the range column, for example company id, then created_at, so the index seeks to one tenant and scans only the date range. You check it with EXPLAIN: the plan should show an index scan and rows examined close to rows returned. ¶ Proving the fast report gives the same numbers as the slow one: run both on the same real data for several date ranges and diff the output field by field, including empty ranges and edge days. Any difference is a bug in one of them, not rounding. [FILL: how you actually checked it.] For the 30 seconds, [FILL: whether it was one run or a median of repeated runs on production-size data]. I don't have a load-test number for ten people running it at once, so I won't claim one. On memory, a big export should stream: a cursor or paginated reads piped into the CSV or PDF writer, never everything loaded first. ¶ Thirty seconds is still too long for a synchronous request. I'd make it a background job: return 202 with a job id and a status URL, and poll or notify when it's ready. That also handles two people asking for the same report. Key the job by its parameters, so a second request joins the job already running instead of starting another, and cache the result under that key until the data underneath changes. ¶ Where the remaining 30 seconds goes: [FILL: what it's spent on today]. If it's a live scan of raw rows, getting under 2 seconds, or sub-second, means not recomputing history. Keep summary tables or materialised views per day or hour, update them incrementally as records change or with a nightly job, and compute only today's slice live. The trade-off is freshness and an extra write path you must keep correct, against reports that answer from a few hundred summary rows. ¶ Heavy reports on the primary slow the app for everyone. The options in order: run them as background jobs off-peak, move them to a read replica, or move them to a warehouse once reporting becomes its own product. At 10 or 100 times the data, a live scan grows linearly with rows, so that's the first thing to break. With rollups the cost grows with the number of buckets, not rows, which is why precomputing is the long-term fix. ¶ To stop the regression coming back: a test that counts queries per report run so an N+1 can't sneak back in, a perf test on seeded production-size data in CI, and a slow-query alert in production. If I did it again, I'd want the query-count guard and the background-job shape from day one rather than after it hurt.
  - `L1` What were the reports, who used them, and what did they contain?

  - `L2` A report takes 30 seconds. Do you keep the HTTP call synchronous or make it a background job? ↳ job + status poll or notify; HTTP timeouts

  - `L2` How do you export a huge CSV or PDF without loading everything into memory? ↳ streams, cursors, pagination

  - `L2` How did you find the bottleneck: EXPLAIN, query logs, profiling the app, or counting queries?

  - `L2` Did you move aggregation into SQL? Which GROUP BY or window functions did you use?
  - `L2` Which indexes did you add, with which column order, and why that order?
  - `L2` How did you cut the Jigsaw report from 12 min to 30 s?
  - `L2` What did the report show, who used it, and why did 12 minutes hurt?

  - `L2` Show me the before and after: the old loop or query, and the new one.

  - `L2` Did you move aggregation into the database? Why is that faster than doing it in Node?

  - `L3` 30 seconds is still slow for a user. How would you get to under 2 seconds?

  - `L3` Did the fix add load to the primary? Would you move it to a replica or run it as a background job?
  - `L3` If the data grew 100×, which part of your fix breaks first?
  - `L3` Precompute the report (materialized view or summary table) or compute it live? Trade-offs.
  - `L3` Your report took 12 minutes; you got it to 30 seconds. Was the fix batch, precomputation or indexing? Explain.
  - `L3` What was the remaining 30 s spent on? Could it go to 3 s, and what would that take?
  - `L3` Would you precompute the report (materialised view, nightly job, incremental aggregates)? Trade-offs vs freshness.
  - `L3` Should the report be generated in a background job with a 202 + status URL instead of in the request?
  - `L3` What happens when ten users generate the report at the same time? Did you load-test that?
  - `L3` What happens when data grows 10×? Does the new design scale linearly?
  - `L3` How did you make sure the faster report returns exactly the same numbers as the slow one?
  - `L3` How did you stop the regression from coming back? ↳ query-count test, perf test in CI, slow-query alert
  - `L3` What would you do differently if you did it again?
- [ ] NestJS backend: referrals, assessments, UniPile LinkedIn outreach; modules, guards, RBAC ↳ https://docs.nestjs.com/guards · https://docs.nestjs.com/modules · career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw)
  - **Answer:** At Jigsaw I built a NestJS backend for referrals, assessments and LinkedIn outreach through UniPile. NestJS is an opinionated framework on top of Express or Fastify. Express gives you routing and middleware and leaves structure to you; Nest adds modules, decorators and dependency injection. A module groups a feature's controllers and providers, controllers map routes, and providers are injectable services, so a service declares what it needs instead of importing singletons. ¶ Request order in Nest is middleware, then guards, then interceptors on the way in, then pipes, then the handler, then interceptors on the way out, with exception filters catching errors. Guards answer whether this caller may do this at all, which is where role checks live. Pipes validate and transform input. Interceptors wrap the call for logging, timing or response shaping. DTO validation belongs in a global ValidationPipe with class-validator: whitelist strips unknown fields, and forbidNonWhitelisted turns them into a 400, so a client can't slip in a field like role or companyId. ¶ [FILL: the actual module split, for example referrals, assessments, outreach, auth and users, and the actual roles, for example admin, recruiter, hiring manager, candidate.] In Nest, role-based access is normally a Roles decorator plus a RolesGuard that reads the required roles from route metadata and compares them with the role in the JWT. [FILL: confirm that's how yours worked, or say what differed.] The guard only says a recruiter may call this route. Whether this recruiter may touch this candidate is an ownership check in the service. For tenant isolation, the rule I hold to is that the company id comes from the token, never from the request body, every query is scoped by it through a scoped repository, and tests try another company's ids and expect 404 rather than 403, so you don't even leak that the record exists. ¶ [FILL: the referral flow, who refers, what's stored, which statuses it moves through.] [FILL: how assessments worked, question types, timer, scoring, who saw the result, and how video was recorded, stored and played back.] Two things I'd insist on whatever the details. The timer is enforced on the server, by storing the start time and deadline and rejecting a late submit, because a client timer is only a suggestion. And video goes straight from the browser to object storage on a short-lived signed URL, so the API never streams video, with playback through another signed URL and transcoding if the format needs it. ¶ Chat: [FILL: the transport, socket.io, raw WebSockets or a hosted service, and how messages and read receipts were stored]. The shape I'd describe is a room per conversation, messages stored before they're broadcast, and read receipts as a last-read message id per participant rather than a flag per message. At 10 times the users you run several Node instances, add the socket.io Redis adapter so an emit on one instance reaches sockets on the others, use sticky sessions if the polling fallback is on, and keep presence in Redis with a TTL. ¶ UniPile is one API over LinkedIn and email messaging through the user's own connected account. The reason to use it is that LinkedIn's own API keeps messaging and most profile data for approved partners. I ran into that on a small personal spike in November 2024, an Express app on my own GitHub, not Jigsaw code. It exchanged the authorization code at LinkedIn's token endpoint and called the OpenID userinfo endpoint; the education and skills calls I tried next are restricted for ordinary apps, so they stayed commented out. Honestly, it never stored or refreshed per-user tokens, and the client secret sat in a committed .env with an access token hard-coded in index.js; both need rotating and purging. The right design keeps the secret in a secret manager and stores each user's tokens encrypted at rest, keyed to the user, refreshing before expiry where the provider issues refresh tokens and asking the user to reconnect where it doesn't. ¶ Outreach: [FILL: how sends were scheduled, caps, delays, a queue, and how UniPile webhooks were handled]. The risk is getting the account flagged, so the design I'd defend is a queue with daily caps per account, randomised delays, working hours only, and the sequence stopping the moment someone replies. If a batch crashes halfway, every message has its own status row, pending, sent or failed, with an idempotency key of campaign plus recipient, so a restart sends only what's still pending and never sends twice. Webhooks like reply received or connection accepted get verified, de-duplicated by event id, acknowledged with a fast 200, then processed async to update status and stop follow-ups. ¶ Time to hire down 60 percent is a product number, not something my code measured on its own. [FILL: how it was measured, for example days from job open to offer, before versus after, over what period, and which features drove it.] The features on that resume line are interviews, video assessments, real-time chat and role-based access. On where Nest ended and the AI pipelines began: [FILL: whether they ran inside Nest or as a separate service, and how they talked]. ¶ At StreetDine, where I can show it from code, roles were RESTAURANT, STAFF and RESTAURANTADMIN: Rohit's backend had a JWT middleware per role and separate staff endpoints with ownership checks, and my staff app gated screens on the client. On both, I'd now keep guards for coarse roles, a small policy layer in the service for ownership, and tenant scoping built into the repository so it can't be forgotten.

  - `L1` What is RBAC? Which roles did the hiring platform have?
  - `L1` Which framework did your Jigsaw backend use?

  - `L2` Walk me through the referral flow: who refers, what is stored, how status is tracked.

  - `L2` How did video assessments work: recording, upload, storage, playback? ↳ direct upload to object storage, signed URLs, transcoding
  - `L2` How did you build real-time chat: transport, rooms, storage, read receipts?

  - `L2` An outreach job crashes halfway through a batch. What happens on restart? ↳ per-message status, idempotency keys, resume
  - `L2` How did you handle UniPile webhooks (reply received, connection accepted)?
  - `L2` "Time to hire down 60%": how was it measured, and which features drove it?

  - `L3` Chat at 10× users: how do you scale WebSockets across several Node instances? ↳ Redis adapter/pub-sub, sticky sessions, presence store
  - `L3` How do you stop a recruiter from one company seeing another company's candidates? ↳ tenant id on every query, scoped repositories, tests
  - `L3` How did you validate DTOs, and what happens to unknown fields? ↳ class-validator, whitelist, forbidNonWhitelisted
  - `L3` Where did you draw the line between the NestJS backend and the AI pipelines? How did they talk?

  - `L3` How did you implement role-based access at Jigsaw, and how did you stop one company's recruiters seeing another's candidates?
- [ ] TypeScript migration strategy (incremental, `allowJs`, strict flags), SEO +30% ↳ https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/migrating-from-javascript.html · https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig#strict · https://developers.google.com/search/docs/fundamentals/seo-starter-guide
  - **Answer:** I migrated the Jigsaw codebase to TypeScript. [FILL: which codebase, the Next.js app, the Node backend or both, and confirm it was incremental with no feature freeze.] The why is simple. Shape errors get caught at compile time instead of in production, refactors become safe because the compiler finds every caller, and the types document the API for the next developer. For a growing team, that last one matters as much as bugs. ¶ The tsconfig switches: allowJs lets .js and .ts files live in one project and import each other. checkJs type-checks the JavaScript files too, using inference and JSDoc comments, so you get errors in files you haven't converted yet, and JSDoc types are the bridge for those files. strict is the family of flags, noImplicitAny, strictNullChecks, strictFunctionTypes and others. any switches checking off. unknown means I don't know yet, so you must narrow it before using it, which makes unknown the right type at boundaries. ¶ Big-bang versus incremental: a big-bang rewrite stops feature work and lands one huge risky diff. Incremental keeps shipping and lets the team learn as it goes, at the cost of a long mixed period. The incremental path I'd walk through: tsconfig with allowJs and a CI type-check, tsc with noEmit, so nothing new can break the build. Then convert leaves first, utils, constants and models, and move up the dependency graph to services and then pages or controllers, because each conversion makes everything above it better typed. Every new file is TypeScript, and a file gets converted when someone is already touching it for a feature, so the migration rides along with feature work instead of competing with it. ¶ To keep new any out: no-explicit-any as a lint error for new code, review, and tracking the any count so it only goes down. Progress is measurable as files converted, the any count, a type-coverage tool's percentage, and which strict flags are on. If strict suddenly shows 2,000 errors, don't fix them in one go. Take a baseline, turn strict on per folder, allow a small expect-error budget with a ticket each, and ratchet the budget down every sprint. ¶ Types lie at the boundaries, and that's where runtime still breaks even when everything type-checks. A request body, a JSON.parse, a third-party response or a database document is whatever the outside sends, and an as cast just silences the compiler. The fix is validation at the edge, DTOs with class-validator in Nest or a schema parse, so the inside of the system trusts validated types. A big legacy object whose shape changes across the code becomes a discriminated union on a kind field, or starts loose and gets narrowed module by module. For a Mongoose or Sequelize model layer, write an interface per document and pass it into the model generics, or infer the type from the schema, so queries return typed documents. Frontend and backend stay in agreement through a shared types package, or better, types generated from the backend's DTOs or OpenAPI spec. [FILL: one real bug TypeScript caught after the migration, and one runtime break that still type-checked.] ¶ Adoption is about review and conventions more than training: a short written guide, the first few conversions done together, and review comments that point at the rule. [FILL: how you actually got the team on board, and how you justified a sprint the migration slowed.] [FILL: any numbers on bug rates or onboarding time; if none were measured, say so.] ¶ SEO: the Core Web Vitals are LCP, how fast the main content paints, under 2.5 seconds is good; INP, how fast the page responds to input, under 200 ms; and CLS, how much the layout jumps, under 0.1. For SEO, SSR and SSG beat client-side rendering because the crawler gets real HTML with content and meta tags instead of an empty shell waiting on JavaScript. The 30 percent: [FILL: the metric, for example Search Console clicks or impressions or a Lighthouse SEO score, the period, and what changed, SSR or SSG pages, meta tags, sitemap, structured data, page speed]. If I can't name the metric and the period, I shouldn't quote the number. ¶ If I did it again, I'd turn on strictNullChecks early, because null bugs are the most common ones, and generate API types from day one instead of hand-writing them on both sides.
  - `L1` Why move a JavaScript codebase to TypeScript? What did the team get from it?
  - `L1` What do `allowJs`, `checkJs` and `strict` do in `tsconfig.json`?
  - `L1` `any` vs `unknown`: what is the difference?
  - `L1` What are the Core Web Vitals (LCP, INP, CLS)?
  - `L1` SSR vs SSG vs CSR: which helps SEO, and why?
  - `L1` Why would a team move a Node.js codebase from JS to TS?
  - `L2` How did you migrate without stopping feature work? ↳ incremental: allowJs, leaf modules first, file by file, CI typecheck
  - `L2` How did you stop new `any` from creeping in during the migration? ↳ no-explicit-any lint, strict per folder, review
  - `L2` Name one real bug TypeScript caught after the migration.
  - `L2` How did you type API responses so frontend and backend agree? ↳ shared types package or types generated from DTOs/OpenAPI
  - `L2` "SEO up 30%": which metric, measured how, over what period? ↳ impressions, clicks, rankings, or Lighthouse — name it
  - `L2` What did you change to improve SEO? ↳ SSR/SSG pages, meta tags, sitemap, structured data, page speed
  - `L2` How did you migrate to TypeScript without stopping feature work?
  - `L2` How did you migrate Jigsaw to TypeScript incrementally? Walk me through the steps.
  - `L2` What do `allowJs` and `checkJs` do, and how do JSDoc types help during migration?
  - `L2` Big-bang rewrite vs file-by-file migration: trade-offs.
  - `L2` In which order do you convert modules? ↳ leaves first (utils, models), up the dependency graph
  - `L2` How do you stop new `any` from creeping in? ↳ lint rules (`no-explicit-any`), CI type-check, tracking `any` count
  - `L2` What broke at runtime during the migration even though it type-checked?
  - `L2` How do you type a big legacy object that changes shape across the code?
  - `L2` How did you handle runtime data (request bodies, DB documents) that the new types could not guarantee?
  - `L2` How do you migrate a Mongoose / Sequelize model layer to typed models?
  - `L2` How did you get the team to adopt TS (reviews, conventions, training)?
  - `L2` How do you keep shipping features while the migration is half done?
  - `L2` How did you migrate Jigsaw to TS incrementally?
  - `L3` The migration slowed the team for a sprint. How did you justify it?
  - `L3` Turning on `strict` shows 2,000 errors. What's your plan? ↳ baseline, fix per module, expect-error budget, ratchet down
  - `L3` How do you measure migration progress and type coverage? ↳ files converted, `any` count, type-coverage tool, strict flags enabled
  - `L3` What would you do differently if you migrated again?
  - `L3` How did the migration change bug rates or onboarding time? What numbers can you share?
  - `L3` How did you migrate Jigsaw to TypeScript incrementally?
- [ ] Mentoring 3 developers — code review practices ↳ https://google.github.io/eng-practices/review/ · career/profile/resume.tex (Jigsaw)
  - **Answer:** I mentored three developers at Jigsaw. [FILL: their level, and one real example: what a mentee struggled with and what changed.] [FILL: what a normal week looked like, for example reviews on every pull request, a weekly one-to-one, pairing on the hard tickets.] ¶ In a review, I look at the contract first. Does it do what the ticket says, what happens on the edges, empty input, failure, retries, and does it touch a boundary like auth, tenant data or money. Then design: is this the right place for the logic, will the next person understand it. Naming and style come last, and as much of that as possible should be a lint rule, not a comment. Comments should explain why, and I try to make every one teach a reason rather than just ask for a change. With a newer developer I also ask them to walk me through why each change is there. If they can't explain a line, that's the conversation to have, not the diff. ¶ If a mentee keeps shipping the same kind of bug, fixing it in review each time isn't working. I'd sit with them on the next one and find out whether it's a gap in understanding or a missing guardrail. Then I'd make that class of bug hard to write: a test, a lint rule, or a helper that does it right by default. And I'd write the rule down. That's how we work at AiRA today, where I lead a four-person team: the review rules live in AGENTS.md with numbered codes, so a review comment can point at a rule instead of a personal preference.
  - `L1` How many developers did you mentor at Jigsaw?
  - `L2` How did you mentor 3 developers? What did a normal week of that look like?
  - `L2` What do you look at first in a code review?
  - `L3` A mentee keeps shipping the same kind of bug. What do you do?

## 1.7 StreetDine — restaurant software
- [ ] Live order board: realtime updates (sockets), order state machine (new → accepted → ready → served → closed) ↳ DHANESH_PERSONAL_AND_PRIOR_WORK.md "1. Live order dashboard and the order state machine" + "10. Real-time order sync with socket.io" · https://socket.io/docs/v4/ · DDIA ch11 "Event sourcing" (pdf p464–466)
  - **Answer:** StreetDine was a restaurant ordering startup where I was a founding member alongside Rohit Raj, who did the architecture and most backend domains, and Mukesh Negi, who did most of the visual UI. I was full-time from January 2024, and after I joined Jigsaw in August 2024 I kept contributing part-time until April 2025, which is why the dates overlap. The partner web app is what restaurants ran service on: owners, counter staff, waiters on their phones, and a kitchen screen. My core work there was the live order board and the order lifecycle behind it; 380 of that repo's 732 commits are mine. ¶ Each order is a card, and the main button changes with its state. The five actions are accept, as Accept Paid Order or Accept Payment Later, Mark Order Ready, served, Accept Cash and Close Order. Around those sit assign waiter, change table, add items, cancel an item and print. Dine-in and take-away differ in serving: a dine-in item can be marked served once the order is accepted or ready, because a waiter brings things to the table as they come, while a take-away item is only handed over once the order is READY. Accept-cash-before only appears for a dine-in, unpaid, pending order, and never for staff. So the button is a function of status, dine-in or take-away, the paid flag and the role. A KOT is a kitchen order ticket, the slip that tells the kitchen what to cook. ¶ The statuses were PENDING, ACCEPTED, PREPARING, PREPARED, READY and SERVED, and closing sets `is_order_open` to false and frees the table. Illegal moves are things like going backwards, closing a pending order, or serving a take-away before it's ready. **Here's the honest part: those rules lived in my frontend helpers, not on the server.** Rohit's status handler checked who you were, the assigned staff member or the owning restaurant, then wrote whatever status the body sent. A button is a suggestion; it has to be a transition table on the server plus a conditional update on the current status. ¶ Add more items is modelled as `order_version`: each batch of new items gets the next version, the kitchen screen groups items by batch, and each batch prints its own KOT, so the kitchen sees only what's new. Cancelling one item leaves it on the order, struck through; my `isAllItemsServed` counts cancelled items as done, and the amount helpers exclude them from the bill. Waiters, the STAFF role, see new orders plus their own, and can't assign staff, change a pending order's table, take payment up front, or see menu, staff, reports or settings; owners see everything and assign waiters. ¶ Realtime was socket.io with one room per restaurant. Staff join their `associated_restaurant`, and the kitchen screen is the same app, so it sits in the same room. Rohit wrote the socket client and the server; I wired it into every order view, restaurant and staff. A mutation emits an event carrying just the restaurant and the order id, and every open screen in that room refetches its list. I chose refetch over applying the payload because the database is the truth and you never apply an event to the wrong state; the cost is an extra request per device per event. I added an in-flight guard, `isGetOrderLoading`, against refetch storms, but it was a plain variable, not a ref, and later I took it out of the check, so today every event refetches. A ref-based flag with one trailing refetch, or a short debounce, is the real fix. The device that made the change also refreshes itself locally, so it never waits on the round trip. ¶ Only the clicked button spins, because loading flags are keyed by order or item id through `meta.arg`. Long lists got slow, so I made each card render lazily with IntersectionObserver. Push and sockets both refresh because they cover different cases: OneSignal push reaches a backgrounded app, and the socket keeps open screens in sync without a notification. ¶ If the DB changed but the board didn't, start with the fact that the server is only a relay. It rebroadcasts what a client emits after its own mutation, so a change made any other way, like an order created by the payment webhook, emits nothing on the socket and only arrives through push. Next, reconnects: socket.io reconnects the transport by itself, but my `connect_error` and `disconnect` handlers only log, and the room join happens on profile load, so after a reconnect the socket isn't back in the restaurant room until the page reloads. That's a real gap; rejoin in a connect handler. So the socket should be the signal and the database the truth. Change streams fix the root cause: MongoDB pushes every write on the orders collection to the server, which emits to the restaurant room whoever wrote it, with a resume token for reconnects. ¶ The same roughly 15 order handlers ended up copy-pasted across about 8 hooks. That's largely on me: after Rohit's restructure each view got its own hook, and I added each action to each one as features landed. I'd refactor into one shared order-actions hook that takes the order and a refresh callback and maps the role to the right endpoint. For an audit trail I'd `$push` a status-history entry, from, to, who and when, in the same atomic update as the status change. Full event sourcing gives replay and history, but costs projections and migrations a restaurant doesn't need. At 100 times the scale: server-emitted events from change streams, the Redis adapter across socket nodes, auth on the socket handshake and room joins, which today aren't checked, server-side transitions, and indexes on restaurant and status.
  - `L1` What was StreetDine, and who used the partner app? ↳ restaurant owners, counter staff, waiters, kitchen
  - `L1` What are the 5 actions on an order card? ↳ accept, ready, served, accept cash, close
  - `L1` Dine-in vs take-away: how does the order flow differ?
  - `L1` What is a KOT (kitchen order ticket)?
  - `L1` What library carried StreetDine's realtime order updates?
  - `L2` Draw the order state machine. Which transitions are illegal? ↳ PENDING → ACCEPTED → PREPARING/READY → SERVED → closed; cancel rules
  - `L2` Where is a transition enforced: the button, a frontend helper, or the backend? Why must it be the backend?
  - `L2` How did an order card decide which main button to show? ↳ status + dine-in/take-away + paid flag → one next action
  - `L2` How did waiter assignment work, and what can a waiter do that the owner can't, and vice versa?
  - `L2` "Add more items" to a running order: how did you model it? ↳ `order_version` per batch; each batch prints its own KOT
  - `L2` Cancelling one item: how does it change the bill and the "all served" check? ↳ cancelled items excluded from totals, counted as done
  - `L2` How did the counter, the waiters' phones and the kitchen stay in sync? ↳ socket.io room per restaurant; events trigger a refetch
  - `L2` Why refetch on a socket event instead of applying the event payload directly? Trade-offs.
  - `L2` The socket disconnects. What does the app do? ↳ dispatch locally, reconnect handlers, rejoin the room
  - `L2` How did you stop a burst of socket events from causing a refetch storm? ↳ in-flight loading guard; could also debounce
  - `L2` How did you make only the clicked button show a spinner? ↳ loading flags keyed by order/item id via `meta.arg`
  - `L2` Long order lists were slow to render. What did you do? ↳ IntersectionObserver lazy-renders cards
  - `L2` Push notifications and sockets both refresh orders. Why both? ↳ push reaches a backgrounded app; socket syncs open screens
  - `L2` Draw the order state machine. What transitions are illegal and where is that enforced?
  - `L2` How did you build the StreetDine live order board? Which rooms did each restaurant and kitchen join?
  - `L3` The same 15 order handlers were copy-pasted across 8 hooks. How did that happen, and how would you refactor? ↳ one shared order-actions hook/service
  - `L3` How would you add an audit trail of who changed each order's status, and when?
  - `L3` Would you model the order lifecycle with event sourcing? What would you gain and lose? ↳ full history and replay vs complexity, projections, migrations
  - `L3` An order status changed in the DB but the board did not update. How do you debug it?
  - `L3` Should the order board trust socket events as the source of truth, or refetch? Why?
  - `L3` What are change streams, and how could they drive a live order board?
  - `L3` What did StreetDine's ordering flow look like, and what would you change for 100× scale?
- [ ] Reporting APIs: one shared aggregation pipeline replacing 4 copies ↳ DHANESH_PERSONAL_AND_PRIOR_WORK.md "8. Reports and analytics" · https://www.mongodb.com/docs/manual/aggregation/ · DDIA ch3 "Aggregation: Data cubes and materialized views" (pdf p120–122)
  - **Answer:** The reporting API is my strongest StreetDine backend work: 689 of the 785 lines in the dashboard controller are mine by blame. It's on MongoDB with Mongoose. Owners got sales, orders, staff, tables and payments, each bucketed by hour, weekday, month or year, or a custom day range, plus trending categories and items, dine-in versus take-away, top staff, busiest tables, cash versus UPI, and the last 10 transactions. ¶ An aggregation pipeline is a list of stages each document flows through. I used `$match`, `$lookup`, `$unwind`, `$project`, `$group`, `$sort` and `$limit`. `$lookup` is a join to another collection, per document. It's fine when the foreign field is indexed, like `_id`, but it costs a lookup for every document that reaches it, and `$unwind` after it multiplies the rows. That's why `$match` goes first: it can use an index and cut the set before anything expensive runs. A report on restaurant plus a `createdAt` window wants a compound index on restaurant then `createdAt`, equality first, then range. Honestly, the order and payment models declared no index beyond `_id`. I ran `explain("executionStats")` while building the pipelines, IXSCAN versus COLLSCAN and documents examined versus returned, then took it out. Adding that index is the first thing I'd do. ¶ The shared pipeline came from a refactor I did in April 2024. Each report had four near-identical copies, one per range. I replaced them with one base pipeline: `$match` on restaurant and the window, `$lookup` the joined collection, `$unwind`, and a `$project` that adds the time-zone offset to make a local timestamp. Then a `switch (range)` pushes the range's stages: today groups by `$hour`, weekly by `$dayOfWeek`, monthly by year and month, yearly by `$year`, and custom by day, month and year. The commit shows 715 lines deleted and 129 added, but I'd be precise if asked to show the diff: that commit also moved the other reports off that branch, and they came back on their own branches the same day. The dedup itself took the sales report from about 320 lines to about 215. One route per report then dispatches on `body.type`, graph, chart or trending, so one endpoint serves every view of that report. ¶ The revenue source changed twice. First it was the order's own amount. In June 2024 I moved to quantity times variant price over the order items, so it reflected what was actually ordered. In December 2024 I rebuilt it on the payments joined to each order, so reports count money actually collected, which is what an owner closes the till against. Money is stored in paise, as integers, because floats can't represent 0.1 rupee exactly and errors add up across thousands of orders. The backend does the paise maths and the app divides by 100 for display. ¶ Time zones were hard-coded IST at first. Then the client sent its IANA zone and the server worked out the offset. **That fix has a real bug: the offset is computed once, for now.** Across a daylight-saving change, buckets on the other side shift by an hour. India has no DST, so it never showed, but it's wrong. The right fix is passing the timezone into the date operators, since `$hour` and friends accept one, and computing day boundaries in the restaurant's zone instead of the server's. ¶ Two more bugs I'd own up to in review. The staff, table, trending and payments pipelines don't `$match` on the restaurant, so they aggregate across every tenant: a data leak and wrong totals. Every query has to be scoped. And bad range input returns 500 instead of 400. That matters because 500 says we broke, it pages the wrong people and invites retries, while 400 tells the client to fix its request. A smaller one: after `$unwind` on payments, an order paid in two parts is counted twice in the count. ¶ Every owner opens reports at closing time, so the load spikes all at once. I'd pre-aggregate into an hourly rollup collection keyed by restaurant and hour, updated with `$inc` when an order closes or a payment lands, or by a cron with `$merge`. Reports then sum a few dozen rollup rows instead of scanning orders. To test pipelines: seeded fixtures with orders at known times, expected buckets asserted exactly, plus an empty range, orders either side of midnight in the restaurant's zone, a DST date, and a second restaurant whose data must never appear.
  - `L1` Which reports did owners get? ↳ sales, orders, staff, tables, payments; by hour, weekday, month, year
  - `L1` What is a MongoDB aggregation pipeline? Name the stages you used. ↳ $match, $lookup, $unwind, $project, $group, $sort, $limit
  - `L1` What does `$lookup` do, and what does it cost?
  - `L1` Why should `$match` come first in a pipeline?
  - `L1` Which database did StreetDine use?
  - `L1` How many lines did the shared aggregation pipeline remove?
  - `L2` Explain the shared pipeline that replaced 4 duplicated ones. ↳ base pipeline, then `switch (range)` pushes the `$group` stage
  - `L2` How did today / weekly / monthly / yearly / custom map to `$group` keys? ↳ $hour, $dayOfWeek, {year, month}, $year, {day, month, year}
  - `L2` How did one route per report serve graph, chart and trending views? ↳ dispatch on `body.type`
  - `L2` How did you handle time zones? ↳ was hard-coded IST; client sends IANA zone, server computes offset
  - `L2` Revenue moved from order amount, to price × quantity, to collected payments. Why each change?
  - `L2` Money was stored in paise. Why, and where did you convert? ↳ integers avoid float errors; divide by 100 for display
  - `L2` Which index does a report filtered on restaurant and `createdAt` need?
  - `L2` How did you check a pipeline was efficient? ↳ explain("executionStats"): IXSCAN vs COLLSCAN, docs examined
  - `L2` Why can `$lookup` + `$unwind` over order items get slow, and what are the alternatives? ↳ snapshot fields on order, pre-aggregate, index foreign field
  - `L2` How did you design the shared aggregation pipeline?
  - `L2` How did you build the shared aggregation pipeline at StreetDine that removed 715 lines? What did you parameterise?
  - `L3` Find the bug: the staff, table and payment reports don't `$match` on restaurant. What's the impact? ↳ cross-tenant leak and wrong totals; scope every query
  - `L3` The time-zone offset is computed once for "now". What breaks across a daylight-saving change? ↳ buckets shift an hour; pass timezone to date operators
  - `L3` Bad range input returned 500 instead of 400. Why does that matter?
  - `L3` Every owner opens reports at closing time and the database spikes. How would you pre-aggregate? ↳ hourly rollup collection, updated on order close or by cron
  - `L3` How would you test aggregation pipelines? ↳ seeded fixtures, expected buckets, time-zone and empty-range cases
- [ ] Concurrency: two waiters updating one order ↳ DDIA ch7 "Preventing lost updates" (pdf p255–259) · DBI ch5 "Optimistic Concurrency Control" (pdf p174–176)
  - **Answer:** Today both waiters would get a success and the last write wins. That's a lost update, and the fix is a conditional update where the loser gets a 409. In the StreetDine backend the staff accept handler, Rohit's on main, reads the order, checks whether `assigned` is set, and then does an unconditional `findByIdAndUpdate`. Two requests at the same moment both read unassigned, both pass the check, and both write. Each waiter sees Order accepted and walks to the table, and the order ends up assigned to whoever wrote second. The general status handler has the same read-then-write shape. My own change on a branch, assign the staff member when they accept an unassigned order, kept that shape too, so I'm not claiming I'd solved it. ¶ The fix is to put the check inside the write: `findOneAndUpdate` filtered on the order id, the restaurant, `assigned: null` and `status: PENDING`, setting assigned and ACCEPTED in one atomic operation. A single-document update is atomic in MongoDB, so exactly one request matches. The other gets null back and returns 409 Conflict with the current order, and the app refetches and shows who took it. Today that case returns a 500 with Staff already assigned, which reads like a server failure. ¶ For general edits to an order I'd add a version number, put it in the filter of every update and `$inc` it, so a stale screen can't overwrite a newer change. Mongoose's `optimisticConcurrency` option does the same thing, but only for `save()`. The socket refresh tells the other waiter quickly, but it can't stop the race; only the conditional write can.
  - `L3` Two waiters press "accept" on the same order at the same moment. What happens, and how would you fix it? ↳ conditional update on current status/version; loser gets 409
- [ ] Payments: Razorpay order → checkout → webhook, signature over the raw body, item snapshots, cash and partial payments, idempotency
  - **Answer:** Razorpay works like this. Our server creates a Razorpay order for the amount, the browser opens Razorpay Checkout with that order id, the user pays, and Razorpay tells our server through a signed webhook. The server is what decides the order is paid. A webhook is the provider calling our endpoint when something happens, so we learn about the payment even if the user's tab is gone. You never trust the browser's success callback, because anyone can call it, fake it, or close the tab before it runs. ¶ I built the customer-side checkout end to end. Proceed Payment re-posts the cart with the order options, take-away, vehicle number for dine-in-car, cooking instructions. Then Pay online calls `POST /payments/create-online-payment/:cartId`; Rohit's backend creates the Razorpay order and returns its id and amount. I load `checkout.js` on demand, open Checkout with that order id, and restrict methods to card and UPI by switching off netbanking, wallets, EMI and pay-later. The case for that restriction is that card and UPI confirm straight away, while the others add redirects and slower, messier failure modes. ¶ On success, the handler doesn't create anything. It redirects to the cart page with the Razorpay order id, shows Validating payment, and polls the cart every 3 seconds until the backend webhook has turned the cart into an order, then routes to the order. My first version, in June 2024, posted the order id, payment id and signature for a server-side check instead. The signature is HMAC-SHA256 of order_id, a pipe, then payment_id, keyed with the secret, compared with the signature Razorpay returns. I switched to webhook plus polling so a closed tab or a forged callback can't create a paid order. My HMAC check and my webhook rewrite stayed on branches; the webhook on main is Rohit's. ¶ A webhook signature has to be checked over the raw body. Razorpay signs the exact bytes it sent. Re-serialising the parsed object with `JSON.stringify` can change key order, spacing or number formatting, and then a genuine webhook fails or the check means less than you think. Both the main webhook and my branch version did that after `express.json()`. The fix is `express.raw` on that one route, or capturing the raw buffer in the json parser's verify hook. ¶ The order itself comes from my service, `createOrderFromCartOnPaymentSuccess`. It snapshots every cart line into an `OrderItem`: name, category, veg flag, discount, image, variant name and price, the take-away flag, `order_version` 1, because a later menu or price edit must never change a past order or its bill. It takes the next per-restaurant token with an atomic `$inc` upsert, links the payment and closes the cart. Cash went through three designs: a CASH payment for the total, then a pending payment on an unpaid order, and finally an unpaid order with no payment row, which won because the money isn't collected until staff collect it. When staff accept cash, the due is the sum of non-cancelled items after discount, in paise, minus payments already on the order, so partial and repeat payments add up. ¶ A bug I fixed: the Razorpay key was read from a plain `process.env` variable, and Next.js only exposes variables prefixed `NEXT_PUBLIC_` to the browser, so checkout had no key. It moved to `NEXT_PUBLIC_RAZORPAY_API_KEY`, and I replaced hard-coded demo prefill details with the logged-in user's. ¶ The gaps, honestly. **The webhook isn't idempotent today.** A duplicate delivery saves a second payment and creates a second order with a new token, because nothing checks whether the cart already has an order. The fix is a unique index on the Razorpay payment id, a check that the cart is still active inside the same step, and answering a duplicate with 200 so Razorpay stops retrying. If the webhook never arrives, there's no timeout on the polling. I'd stop after a minute or two, show we're confirming your payment, don't pay again, and run a reconciliation cron that asks Razorpay's API about any payment request without a result. If payment succeeds but order creation fails, the payment row is already saved, which is right, and the handler returns 500 so Razorpay retries; with idempotency in place that recovers cleanly, plus an alert, and a refund if the order can never be made. ¶ Refunds for items cancelled after payment: the partner app already works out refund owed when cancelled items drop the due below what's paid. The backend side I'd add is a partial refund through Razorpay's refund API on that payment id, for that amount in paise, recorded as a refund row against the order so reports and the bill both see it.
  - `L1` How does a Razorpay payment work end to end? ↳ server creates order, client checkout, webhook/signature, server confirms
  - `L1` What is a payment webhook, and why do you need one?
  - `L1` Why should the server never trust the browser's "payment succeeded" callback?
  - `L1` Which payment gateway did StreetDine use?
  - `L2` Walk me through your checkout: cart → Razorpay order → checkout.js → confirmation. ↳ poll the cart every 3 s until the webhook creates the order
  - `L2` Why poll instead of trusting the success handler? What does it protect against? ↳ closed tab or forged callback can't create a paid order
  - `L2` How does Razorpay signature verification work? ↳ HMAC-SHA256 of `order_id|payment_id` with the secret, compare
  - `L2` Why must a webhook signature be checked over the raw body, not `JSON.stringify(req.body)`? ↳ re-serialised JSON may differ byte-for-byte from what was signed
  - `L2` The cart-to-order service snapshots every item into an `OrderItem`. Why snapshot? ↳ later menu or price edits must not change past orders
  - `L2` Cash checkout went through three designs. Which won, and why? ↳ unpaid order, no payment row; staff collect cash later
  - `L2` How did partial and repeat payments on one order add up? ↳ due = non-cancelled items after discount − already paid, in paise
  - `L2` Why restrict checkout to card and UPI?
  - `L2` The Razorpay key was read from a server-only env var in Next.js. What was the bug? ↳ only `NEXT_PUBLIC_` vars reach the browser
  - `L3` The webhook arrives twice. How do you make order creation idempotent? ↳ unique payment id; skip if cart already has an order
  - `L3` The webhook never arrives. What does the user see, and how do you recover? ↳ timeout UI; reconcile via payments API; cron
  - `L3` Payment succeeded but creating the order failed. Now what? ↳ store payment first, retry, alert, refund path
  - `L3` How would you handle refunds when items are cancelled after payment?
- [ ] PWA and staff app: service worker caching, push worker scope, KOT printing, role guards, Netlify static export
  - **Answer:** A PWA is a website that installs and behaves like an app. To be installable it needs HTTPS, a web manifest with a name, icons, a start URL and a standalone display mode, and on most browsers a service worker. A service worker is a script the browser runs apart from the page. It can intercept network requests, serve from a cache, receive push messages and run sync events when no tab is open, none of which a page can do. Cache-first is for things that rarely change, like icons and fonts. Network-first is for data that must be fresh, like orders, with the cache only as a fallback. A Trusted Web Activity wraps a PWA in an Android app for the Play Store, full screen in Chrome, verified through `assetlinks.json`. ¶ At StreetDine the PWA came in layers. In March 2024 I set up the first version, next-pwa with Workbox, the manifest and icon sets. In October 2024 Rohit re-set it up with next-pwa 5.6, the static export and `assetlinks.json` for the Android TWA. In December 2024 I wrote the hand-made service worker. **Offline really meant the app shell and icons, not orders.** It precached the icon set and served it cache-first. There was no offline order queue. Background Sync replays a queued action when the network returns, and Periodic Background Sync wakes the worker on a schedule. I registered a `sync-orders` tag and a periodic sync with a 24-hour minimum interval, with feature detection, but both handlers only post `SYNC_ORDERS` to open tabs so they refetch. No writes are queued. ¶ The push worker needed its own scope because only one service worker controls a scope. Register OneSignal's worker and the PWA's at `/` and they replace each other, so I moved OneSignal to `/onesignal-push/`. Two problems I'd fix. next-pwa's `sw.js` and my `service-worker.js` were still both at `/`. And my activate step deleted every cache, not just old versions. Name caches with a version and delete only the ones that don't match, otherwise every update throws away caches you still need. ¶ The staff app was a separate STAFF-role surface: its own dashboard, My Orders, a staff POS, and a parallel staff API slice. The client hides screens, but the real enforcement is on the server, with a staff JWT middleware, separate staff endpoints and ownership checks in the handlers. The weak spot was the client guard. After a Next middleware guard lived for five days in February 2024, role checks ended up as hooks that wait `setTimeout(500)` and then redirect. That's a race: on a slow load auth isn't ready at 500 ms and you bounce a logged-in user, or on a fast one you flash a screen you shouldn't. The fix is one route guard that waits on the auth loading state, not a timer, or middleware on a cookie. ¶ Printing used react-to-print with a hidden print component. A shared `printData(order, version)` prints either an invoice or a KOT, and the KOT filters items to that `order_version`, so each add-items batch prints only the new items for the kitchen. If the restaurant's `kot_order_print` setting is on, creating an order or adding items auto-prints. ¶ Netlify static export: `output: export` pre-renders pages, but a dynamic route like an order id has no HTML file for ids that didn't exist at build time, so a refresh or a deep link 404s. The fix is rewrites in a `_redirects` file that send those paths to the shell page, and the client reads the id. The trap, and it was my commit: in the partner app I named the file `_rereedirects`, and Netlify silently ignores a misspelt file. The customer app had it right. ¶ Street-vendor mode is a one-tap dashboard for a single-person stall. One call creates the order, records a CASH payment, marks it served, paid and closed, and takes a token, and the app prints the bill.
