It's a separate endpoint because it's one atomic business step with its own rule, only street-food restaurants may use it, and chaining four normal calls from a phone could fail halfway and leave a half-made order. I built the frontend in May and August 2024. The backend endpoint is mine too, but it stayed on a branch and was never merged. ¶ For real offline for waiters, I'd keep an IndexedDB outbox of actions, each with a client-generated id as an idempotency key, replay them in order when the network returns, have the server de-duplicate on that id and reject illegal transitions, and show pending state on the card. On iOS that's harder. Web push only arrived in iOS 16.4 and only for home-screen apps, there's no Background Sync, and Safari can evict storage for sites you haven't used in a while. Our iOS support was icons and a manifest, nothing more. With no tests and no CI anywhere, I'd add CI first, lint plus the static-export build, which would have caught broken routes. Then backend tests on the money and state logic, since that's where bugs cost real money: amount due in paise, order transitions, webhook idempotency and report buckets.
  - `L1` What is a PWA? What makes a web app installable?
  - `L1` What is a service worker? What can it do that a page can't?
  - `L1` Cache-first vs network-first: when do you use each?
  - `L1` What is a Trusted Web Activity (TWA)?
  - `L2` What did your service worker cache, and what did "offline" really mean? ↳ icons and app shell only; no offline order queue
  - `L2` What do Background Sync and Periodic Background Sync do? What did yours do? ↳ posted a message to open tabs to refetch; no queued writes
  - `L2` Why did the push-notification worker need its own scope? ↳ two workers at scope `/` compete
  - `L2` Your activate step deleted every cache. What's wrong with that? ↳ delete only old versioned caches
  - `L2` How did the staff app differ from the owner app, and where was access really enforced? ↳ client hides screens; separate staff endpoints enforce on server
  - `L2` Role checks waited `setTimeout(500)` before redirecting. What's wrong, and what's the fix? ↳ race on auth load; a proper route guard
  - `L2` How did per-batch KOT printing and auto-print on order create work? ↳ react-to-print, filter by `order_version`, restaurant setting
  - `L2` Next.js static export on Netlify: what breaks for dynamic routes, and how did you fix it? ↳ `_redirects` rewrites; a misspelt file is silently ignored
  - `L2` Street-vendor mode creates, pays, closes and prints in one call. Why a separate endpoint?
  - `L3` How would you build a real offline mode for waiters? ↳ IndexedDB outbox, idempotent replay, conflict rules
  - `L3` What is limited for PWAs on iOS? ↳ late push support, no Background Sync, storage eviction
  - `L3` The repos had no tests and no CI. What would you add first, and why?

## 1.8 Side project — Perplexity Mini
- [ ] Redis queue → Playwright crawler → Qdrant → Llama 3.1 behind nginx ↳ DHANESH_PERSONAL_AND_PRIOR_WORK.md summary "Perplexity Mini" · https://github.com/Dhanesh-Pottekula/perplexity_mini · LEH ch4 "The vanilla RAG framework" (pdf p130–136)
  - **Answer:** Perplexity Mini is a small answer engine I built alone, September 19 to October 6, 2025, over a web index that refreshes itself nightly. A Redis queue feeds a Playwright crawler that opens 10 pages at a time, pages become 384-dimension vectors in Qdrant, the same vector DB we use at AiRA, and Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama behind nginx answers. ¶ `POST /api/urls` takes `{url, depth}` and does an `LPUSH` of that JSON onto the Redis list `urls_queue`. A Redis list works as a queue because `LPUSH` adds at one end and `BRPOP` pops from the other, blocking until there is an item, so the worker never polls. A blocking pop holds its connection, so the worker gets its own subscriber connection, separate from the publisher. Jobs deeper than 1 are dropped. A p-queue with concurrency 10 opens a page per URL on one shared headless Chromium (`domcontentloaded`, 60 second timeout) and takes the title, `document.body.innerText` and every `a[href]`. I upsert a Mongo `Url` row, cut 500-character chunks with 50 of overlap, extract topics, embed, and upsert chunks into Qdrant with `url_id`, `text`, `index`, `topic_ids` and `updated_at`. If the depth was 0, every outlink goes back on the queue at depth 1. That's one hop, because outlinks multiply fast and the second hop drifts off topic. ¶ Playwright drives a real browser, which beats `requests` plus BeautifulSoup on pages that build content with JavaScript; the cost is that every page is a heavy, slow tab. Cleaning was basic: `innerText` gives rendered text but keeps menus and cookie banners, so I'd add a readability-style extractor. Backpressure is weak: the `BRPOP` loop calls the handler without awaiting it, so Redis drains as fast as it can pop, jobs pile up in memory, and only the p-queue bounds open pages. And `BRPOP` removes the job with no ack, so if the process dies, that URL and everything waiting in the p-queue is gone. The fix is `LMOVE` into a processing list, removed on success, with a reaper for stale entries, or Redis Streams with a consumer group and `XACK`. Redis still earns its place: it decouples the API from slow crawling, absorbs bursts, and lets the crawler feed links back in. ¶ Topics are extracted per page: the chunks go to DeepSeek V3.1 through OpenRouter, which returns 3 to 5 broad lowercase topics with 2 to 5 tags each as schema-constrained JSON. Then a merge-upsert into a Qdrant `topics` collection: existing topics keep their id and get the union of tags, new ones get a UUID, and each is embedded on its tags. The embedder is a FastAPI sidecar on Sentence Transformers `paraphrase-MiniLM-L12-v2`: 384 dimensions, cosine distance, small, free and fast on CPU, at the price of retrieval quality. 500 characters suits that model's short input, and the overlap keeps a sentence cut at a boundary whole in one chunk; splitting by characters rather than sentences is a weakness. Mongo keeps URL, title, links, depth, status and `sourceTopic`. ¶ Refresh is three node-cron jobs. At midnight, ask the Perplexity Sonar API for 10 current URLs per known topic, inserted at depth 0. At 06:00, re-enqueue every URL with depth up to 1. At 05:00 and 17:00, delete URL rows found more than 2 days ago. It never detects change: it re-embeds everything with random point ids, so a recrawl adds duplicates. The fix is a content hash per page to skip unchanged ones, ids derived from URL plus chunk index, and a delete by `url_id` when a page changes or disappears. The cleanup once cascaded to Qdrant like that, but I removed the cascade, so today Mongo rows go and their vectors stay: answers can cite deleted pages and the index only grows. Same family: status is set to `visiting` and never to `visited`, so I can't tell done from crashed and nothing can retry. I'd add `visited` and `failed` states with attempts and a lease time. ¶ Ollama runs open models locally; I chose it as free, private and unlimited, at the cost of weaker 8B answers and latency I never measured. nginx is the reverse proxy: an upstream with `least_conn` over two Ollama instances, 3600 second proxy timeouts and a 500 MB body limit. I didn't set TLS, rate limiting or `proxy_buffering off`. Citations, honestly, didn't exist: the context was chunk text joined together with no URLs. I'd number each chunk with its URL, ask for `[n]` citations, and send the source list as an SSE event. ¶ The Compose file runs mongo, qdrant, redis and nginx. The three data stores have named volumes, nginx mounts its config read-only, there are no healthchecks, and all four publish ports: 27017, 6333, 6379 and 8080 to 80. The Node app and the embedder ran outside it. Only nginx should be published; the rest belongs on the internal Compose network, because an open, unauthenticated Redis or Mongo is a classic breach. I never built a crawler image; I'd start from the official Playwright image, install only Chromium, and raise `--shm-size`, because Chromium crashes on Docker's small default. ¶ Politeness: there's no robots.txt check or per-domain limit today; I'd add cached robots.txt, per-domain rate limits, backoff on 429 and 503, and an honest user agent. Dedupe is only the unique URL; I'd add canonical URLs, a content hash, and SimHash or MinHash. Prompt injection: a page saying "ignore previous instructions" lands straight in the context, and "use only the context" doesn't help, because that text is the context. I'd fence context as quoted data, flag instruction-like text at ingestion, and show citations. With no tools, the worst case is a wrong answer.
  - `L1` What does Perplexity Mini do, in one sentence?
  - `L1` How is a Redis list used as a queue? What do `LPUSH` and `BRPOP` do?
  - `L1` What is Playwright, and why crawl with a real browser?
  - `L1` How many pages does the Perplexity Mini crawler open at once?
  - `L1` What is Perplexity Mini, in two sentences?
  - `L1` Which vector DB did Perplexity Mini and AiRA use?
  - `L1` Embedding dimension in Perplexity Mini?
  - `L1` Which local tool did Perplexity Mini use to run Llama 3.1 8B?
  - `L2` Walk me through one URL from `POST /api/urls` to vectors in Qdrant.
  - `L2` Why separate publisher and subscriber Redis connections? ↳ BRPOP blocks its connection
  - `L2` How did you limit the crawler to 10 pages at once? ↳ one shared Chromium, p-queue concurrency 10, a page per URL
  - `L2` The BRPOP loop calls the handler without awaiting it. What does that do to backpressure? ↳ jobs fan out freely; only p-queue bounds them
  - `L2` How deep does the crawl go, and why stop at one hop?
  - `L2` How does the index refresh itself? ↳ nightly topic → URL seeding via Perplexity Sonar, recrawl, 2-day expiry
  - `L2` A worker pops a job and crashes. What happens to that URL? ↳ lost; use LMOVE processing list or Streams with acks
  - `L2` How did you chunk pages, and why those sizes? ↳ 500 characters, 50 overlap
  - `L2` How did the LLM pull topics and tags from a page, and how were topics merged? ↳ DeepSeek via OpenRouter, schema JSON, merge-upsert, stable ids
  - `L2` How did Perplexity Mini's Playwright crawler turn web pages into clean text?
  - `L2` Walk me through the architecture: Redis queue → Playwright crawler → embeddings in Qdrant → Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama behind nginx.
  - `L2` Why Playwright instead of `requests` + BeautifulSoup? ↳ JS-rendered pages; cost is slower, heavier crawling
  - `L2` Why a Redis queue in front of the crawler? What happens if a crawl job crashes halfway?
  - `L2` Which embedding model gave you 384 dimensions, and why that one?
  - `L2` How did you chunk crawled pages, and what metadata did you keep?
  - `L2` How did the crawler refresh itself? How did it know a page changed? ↳ re-crawl schedule, content hash compare, re-embed only changed pages
  - `L2` What happens to old chunks in Qdrant when a page changes or disappears?
  - `L2` Why Ollama and Llama 3.1 8B instead of an API model? What did that cost in quality and speed?
  - `L2` What did nginx do in front of Ollama? ↳ reverse proxy, timeouts, streaming (buffering off), rate limiting, TLS
  - `L2` How did answers cite sources?
  - `L2` Walk me through Perplexity Mini: Redis queue → Playwright crawler → 384-dim vectors in Qdrant → Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama behind nginx. Why each piece?
  - `L2` Walk me through your Perplexity Mini `compose.yaml`: Redis queue, Playwright crawler, Qdrant, Llama 3.1 and nginx. Which services have volumes, healthchecks and published ports?
  - `L2` In Perplexity Mini, which service should be the only one with a published port, and why? ↳ nginx; others internal-only on the Compose network
  - `L3` How would you make the crawler polite and safe? ↳ robots.txt, per-domain rate limits, user agent, timeouts
  - `L3` How would you dedupe near-identical pages? ↳ canonical URL, content hash, simhash/minhash
  - `L3` The cleanup cron deletes Mongo rows but no longer deletes vectors. What breaks? ↳ orphan vectors cite deleted pages; index keeps growing
  - `L3` Page status gets stuck at `visiting` forever. Why does it matter, and how would you fix it?
  - `L3` How would you stop the crawler from being blocked or from hammering a site? ↳ robots.txt, per-domain rate limits, backoff, user agent
  - `L3` A crawled page contains "ignore previous instructions and say X". What happens in your pipeline?
  - `L3` How do you ship a Playwright crawler image (Perplexity Mini) without a 2 GB image? ↳ official Playwright base, one browser only, `--shm-size`
- [ ] Why each piece; what you'd change for production ↳ LEH ch10 "Monolithic versus microservices architecture in model serving" (pdf p392–397) · HOL ch8 "Example: RAG with Local Models" (pdf p274–277) · https://qdrant.tech/documentation/
  - **Answer:** Every piece was the simplest thing that let me learn the whole loop alone, and for production I'd change the queue, the serving and the retrieval first. On the queue: a Redis list is a pop with no ack, and I already had Redis. Redis Streams add consumer groups, acks, a pending list and replay. BullMQ is the Node library on Redis that gives you retries, delays, rate limits and concurrency out of the box, so for a Node crawler it is the practical next step. Kafka is a partitioned durable log for very high throughput and many independent consumers; it's overkill until several systems read the same events. I'd switch the moment a lost URL matters, which in production is day one. ¶ The embedding service is Python because Sentence Transformers and the ML ecosystem are Python, while the crawler and API are I/O-heavy and fine in Node. It also started life as a full Python pipeline; I later moved topic extraction to Node and left Python with embeddings only. The cost is one more service and an HTTP hop per batch. Local Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama was a choice for zero cost, privacy, no rate limits and learning. The trade-off is quality (an 8B model synthesises grounded answers worse than an API model) and speed on my own hardware, which I never benchmarked. At 1,000 users I'd move answers to an API model or to a GPU server with continuous batching such as vLLM, because Ollama on one box serves very few generations at once. ¶ Two Ollama servers behind nginx `least_conn`: answer lengths vary a lot. Round-robin can stack a long generation behind another long one, while `least_conn` sends each request to the server with fewer open connections. To be honest, both instances ran on one LAN machine on two ports, so it spread load across two model processes, not two machines. Compose ran mongo, qdrant, redis and nginx; the Node app and the embedder ran outside. Data stores are trivial to containerise, the app was still changing hourly under tsx and PM2, and Playwright in Docker needs extra care. I deployed without Kubernetes in the sense that it only ever ran on my machines; there are Minikube notes in the README but no manifests. What broke was found by reading back, not in production. A fresh install fails because the `topics` collection is no longer created after my move to Node. The two-stage retrieval silently became a flat search. The cleanup job leaves orphan vectors. ¶ Could it crawl 4,000 pages a minute? No, and that number is off my resume because nothing measured it. 4,000 a minute is about 67 pages a second. I have 10 browser pages at once, each allowed up to 60 seconds, plus one LLM topic call per page through OpenRouter and an embedding call. Even if a page took a single second, which I never measured, 10 slots give about 600 pages a minute before the LLM step, and the LLM step would need about 67 calls a second. To get near it: plain HTTP fetch first and a browser only for JavaScript pages, several crawler processes, batch or drop the per-page topic call, and per-domain limits so speed does not become abuse. ¶ How many users could it serve? I never load-tested it, so I reason about it. What breaks first is Ollama: each answer holds the model for the whole generation, with a prompt of up to 100 chunks, so a handful of concurrent users queue up. Next is crawler throughput, since the crawler shares the same Node process as the API. 10,000 users a day is only about 0.1 requests a second on average, maybe 1 to 2 at peak, so the fix is not exotic. Split API and crawler workers, use a queue with acks, put serving on a GPU or an API, trim context to 5 to 10 reranked chunks, cache embeddings and repeat answers, stream real tokens, publish only nginx with auth and rate limits, and run managed Qdrant and Mongo with backups and metrics. ¶ Retrieval was dense only. Queries with exact tokens fail on dense-only search: version numbers, product codes, rare names. A paraphrase model blurs them toward similar-sounding text. I didn't keep a log of failed queries, so I can't quote one. I'd add a sparse BM25-style vector in Qdrant next to the dense one and fuse with reciprocal rank fusion. Evaluation, honestly, was me eyeballing answers. Properly: a golden set of questions with their known source pages, recall at k for retrieval, faithfulness and answer relevance with an LLM judge spot-checked by hand, run on every change. ¶ If it became agentic, the index turns into a cache. I already had a search endpoint that fans queries out in parallel to Perplexity Sonar and normalises results to `{title, url, snippet}`, capped at 25 per query. The model would decide to search, fetch the top pages live, answer with citations, and write useful pages back into Qdrant. The costs are latency and per-query API spend, plus a step budget, a loop limit and stronger prompt-injection defences, because live pages are untrusted input.
  - `L2` Redis list vs Redis Streams vs BullMQ vs Kafka: why the list, and when would you switch?
  - `L2` Why a separate Python FastAPI service for embeddings when the main API is Node?
  - `L2` Why run Llama 3.1 8B locally on Ollama instead of calling an API model? Trade-offs.
  - `L2` Why two Ollama servers behind nginx, and why `least_conn` over round-robin? ↳ generations vary in length; send to the less busy server
  - `L2` What ran in Docker Compose, what didn't, and why? ↳ mongo, qdrant, redis, nginx in Compose; Node app and embedder outside
  - `L2` Why did you run Llama 3.1 8B on Ollama for Perplexity Mini instead of an API? What would change at 1,000 users?
  - `L2` Why did you use a 384-dimensional model in Perplexity Mini? What would you change for quality?
  - `L2` What would you change in Perplexity Mini for 10,000 users a day?
  - `L2` How did you deploy without Kubernetes, and what broke? ↳ e.g. Perplexity Mini: Compose + nginx over 2 Ollama servers
  - `L3` Could this design crawl 4,000 pages a minute? Show the maths. ↳ no: 10 pages, up to 60 s each, an LLM call per page
  - `L3` What would you change to serve 1,000 users? ↳ separate workers, queue with acks, GPU serving, caching
  - `L3` How would Perplexity Mini change if it became agentic (search the web live, then answer)?
  - `L3` How many concurrent users could it serve, and what breaks first? ↳ Ollama single-GPU concurrency; then crawler throughput
  - `L3` Did you use hybrid search or only dense? What queries failed because of that?
  - `L3` How did you evaluate answer quality, and how would you do it properly now?
  - `L3` What would you change to take it to 10,000 users a day?
- [ ] Retrieval and answers: 384-d embeddings, Qdrant search, Llama 3.1 on two Ollama servers behind nginx least_conn, SSE, grounding
  - **Answer:** RAG means you retrieve relevant text first and make the model answer from it. The loop: embed the question, search a vector index, put the top chunks in the prompt, generate, and ideally cite. In Perplexity Mini the vectors are 384-dimensional from Sentence Transformers `paraphrase-MiniLM-L12-v2`, stored in Qdrant with cosine distance. Cosine compares direction, not length, which is what sentence-embedding models are trained for. On normalised vectors cosine and dot product give the same ranking, and Euclidean ranks the same too. Answers come from Llama 3.1 8B on two Ollama servers behind nginx, and `least_conn` sends each request to the server with the fewest open connections. ¶ One question end to end: `POST /api/chat` embeds the query through the Python embedder. It searches the `topics` collection (top 100, score at least 0.5), gathers those topics' `url_ids` into a Qdrant `match.any` filter, then searches the chunk collection (top 100, score at least 0.1) under that filter. It joins the chunk texts into a context and prompts Llama. The system prompt says to use only the context, prefer the newest information, and say "I don't know" if it is not there. The reply goes back over SSE: `data:` frames, then `event: end` with `[DONE]`, and writing stops if the client disconnects. nginx had 3600 second proxy timeouts because a local 8B model with a huge prompt can easily pass nginx's default 60 second read timeout when nothing comes back until generation ends. An hour is far too generous; with real streaming a few minutes is plenty. ¶ SSE is a plain HTTP response kept open with `text/event-stream`: the server writes `data:` lines and the browser's EventSource reconnects on its own. An answer only flows one way, so SSE is simpler than a WebSocket and passes through ordinary proxies. But I have to be straight: it was not real streaming. Ollama was called with `stream: false`, and I cut the finished reply into 100-character pieces with an async generator. Time to first token equals the full generation time. The fix is `stream: true`, piping tokens as they arrive, and `proxy_buffering off` in nginx so the proxy doesn't hold them back. ¶ Against making things up I had only the prompt rule (use only the context, say you don't know) and score thresholds. The chunk threshold of 0.1 is effectively none, and there were no citations, so a user couldn't check anything. For failures, the Ollama chat call went through exponential backoff with a 1 second base and a 30 second cap, 3 retries by default. The Ollama service also capped structured calls with a p-queue of 5, but topic extraction had moved to OpenRouter, which had neither retries nor a cap, so overload protection was thin. ¶ The bug I'm least proud of: after I moved topic extraction from Python to Node, the topic upsert stopped writing `url_ids` onto topic points. The filter always came out undefined, and two-stage retrieval silently became a flat top-100 search. Nothing failed, so nothing told me. I'd catch it with a small retrieval eval (known question, expected page, recall at k), by logging the filter size per query and warning when it's empty, and with an integration test that topics carry `url_ids` after ingestion. ¶ Top 100 chunks at a 0.1 score into an 8B model is wrong twice. Most of that is noise that dilutes the answer. And 100 chunks of 500 characters is roughly 50,000 characters. Ollama's default context window is a few thousand tokens unless you raise `num_ctx`, so most of it gets cut off silently. Better: retrieve maybe 50, rerank with a cross-encoder, keep 5 to 10, drop near-duplicate chunks from the same page, and size the context on purpose. ¶ Evaluation, properly: a golden set of questions with known source pages and reference answers. Measure context recall and precision for retrieval, and faithfulness and relevance for answers with an LLM judge, spot-checked by hand. Run it on every change, which is exactly what would have caught the refactor. Hybrid search: keep the dense vector, add a sparse BM25 or SPLADE vector in Qdrant, query both, and merge with reciprocal rank fusion so exact names and numbers still match. ¶ Last lesson: a fresh install fails because nothing creates the `topics` collection anymore. The Python startup that made it was deleted when I moved to Node, and Node only creates the chunk collections. The Qdrant wrapper logged the failed upsert and returned false, so it failed quietly. The lesson is idempotent startup that creates everything it uses, fails loudly, and is tested from an empty environment. At AiRA we create our Qdrant payload indexes at boot in the entity that owns them, safe to re-run, and an error there is suppressed instead of failing startup.
  - `L1` What is RAG? Draw the basic loop.
  - `L1` Why 384 dimensions? Which model produced them? ↳ Sentence Transformers paraphrase-MiniLM-L12-v2
  - `L1` Cosine vs dot product vs Euclidean distance: which did you use, and why?
  - `L1` What does nginx `least_conn` do?
  - `L1` Vector size and vector database in Perplexity Mini?
  - `L1` Which LLM answers in Perplexity Mini, and how many servers run it?
  - `L2` Walk me through answering one question. ↳ embed query, search topics, filter chunks, build context, Llama, SSE
  - `L2` What nginx timeouts did you set for LLM calls, and why so long? ↳ long generations; 3600 s proxy timeouts
  - `L2` How does SSE work, and why SSE rather than WebSockets for answers?
  - `L2` SSE was real, but Ollama ran with `stream: false` and you chunked the full reply. Is that streaming? ↳ no: first token waits for full generation
  - `L2` How did you stop the model from making things up? ↳ "use only the context, say you don't know", thresholds, citations
  - `L2` How did you handle LLM failures and overload? ↳ exponential backoff 1–30 s; p-queue cap on concurrent calls
  - `L3` After a refactor, two-stage retrieval silently became a flat top-100 search. How would you have caught it? ↳ retrieval evals, log filter sizes, pipeline tests
  - `L3` Top 100 chunks at score ≥ 0.1 go into an 8B model's prompt. What's wrong? ↳ noise and context overflow; rerank, top-k 5–10
  - `L3` How would you evaluate answer quality? ↳ golden Q&A set, faithfulness, context recall, LLM judge
  - `L3` How would you add hybrid search (keywords + vectors)? ↳ sparse/BM25 alongside dense, reciprocal rank fusion
  - `L3` A fresh install fails because a Qdrant collection is never created. What's the lesson? ↳ idempotent startup setup; test on an empty environment

## 1.9 AiRA — sign-in, connectors and secrets (the important flows)
Each flow is drawn step by step, with its code files, in `~/Desktop/airaai/aira_temp/docs/AIRA_FLOWS_INTERVIEW_PREP.md` (shown below as **FLOWS §N**). Study order: tools → Key Vault → session → Google → Apple → email_token → connectors → Gmail push → PKCE → vault → WebSocket → files. Short on time: do the crypto tools, Key Vault, Google native, Gmail push and the vault.

- [ ] The three crypto tools: `tokenize` (HMAC-SHA256, one-way, to search), `encrypt_transient` (AES-256-GCM, two-way, to store), signed JWT (sessions, short codes) — and why a plain SHA-256 of an email is not enough ↳ FLOWS §1 · `aira-api/app/adapters/crypto_adp.py` · https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc2104 (HMAC) · https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/38/d/final (AES-GCM)
  - **Answer:** We use three different tools, and the skill is knowing which job each one does. `tokenize` is HMAC-SHA256 with a secret key: one-way and deterministic, so the same email always gives the same token, which means I can put a unique index on it and search by it. `encrypt_transient` is AES-256-GCM with a fresh random 12-byte nonce on every call: two-way, but the same email encrypts differently every time, so you can store it and read it back, but you can't search on it. And the session is a signed JWT: anyone can read it, nobody can change it without our key. All three live in `crypto_adp.py`. My teammate wrote that base; I added the bound variants later for the credential vault. ¶ So a user's email is stored twice in Mongo: an `email_token` to find them and an `encrypted_email` to read the real address when we actually need it. Google and Apple refresh tokens and connector access tokens are stored encrypted the same way. The plain email never sits in the database. ¶ Why not a plain SHA-256 of the email? Because emails are guessable. Someone with a leaked table can hash a list of a few million common addresses and join on it, and they'd have most of our users in minutes. HMAC needs our secret, which lives in Key Vault, so a database dump alone doesn't let you test guesses. We also lower-case and strip before tokenizing, otherwise `A@Gmail.com` with a trailing space and `a@gmail.com` would become two accounts. ¶ For Qdrant I want to be precise, because it changed. In our 2025 memory system, memory payloads in Qdrant were stored as encrypted envelopes, with HMAC tokens for lookups, and my part there was making the read side decrypt concurrently, into deep copies, across six collections. In the current memory code, Qdrant payloads are stored as plain JSON and isolated by a `user_id` filter on every query, and that same filter is what account deletion uses to purge them. The strong per-user encryption today is the credential vault, where every user has their own data key wrapped by Key Vault. ¶ Two weak spots I'd name. `tokenize` and the session JWT are keyed by the same `tenant_secret`; I'd split them so one key does one job and a rotation of one doesn't touch the other. And if I wanted memory text protected against a Qdrant leak again, I'd bring back field encryption on the text fields and keep only the filter fields in the clear, accepting that you can't filter or search on encrypted text.
  - `L2` How are PII fields stored at rest in Mongo and Qdrant? ↳ AES envelope-encrypted payloads; HMAC tokens for lookups
- [ ] Startup + Azure Key Vault secrets: `DefaultAzureCredential` (no stored password), every secret fetched in parallel, validated by a pydantic-settings `Secrets` model of `SecretStr`, encrypted local-only cache, the app refuses to boot without them ↳ FLOWS §2 · `app/main.py` lifespan · `app/adapters/azure_adp.py` `fetch_secrets` · https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/python/api/overview/azure/identity-readme · https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/general/overview
  - **Answer:** All our secrets live in Azure Key Vault, and the app holds no password to reach it. At boot, the FastAPI lifespan builds the adapter registry, and the Azure adapter uses `DefaultAzureCredential`, which resolves to the App Service managed identity in the cloud and to `az login` on a laptop. It lists every enabled secret, fetches them all at once with `asyncio.gather`, and validates the bundle with a pydantic-settings `Secrets` model. Each field is a `SecretStr` with an alias like `docdb-uri`, because Key Vault names use dashes, and `SecretStr` prints as stars in logs and reprs, so you have to call `.get_secret_value()` on purpose. The vault URL has the word `environment` swapped for the environment name, so local and production read different vaults through one code path. My teammate built this loader; I extended it. ¶ Two things I added. Optional integrations like Browserbase, Stripe Link and Instacart are typed `SecretStr | None`, and each feature stays off until its secret exists in Key Vault, so the server answers 'not available here' instead of crashing. And the same vault holds a key, not a secret: `aira-vault-kek`, an RSA key used only to wrap and unwrap per-user data keys for the credential vault. That key never leaves Key Vault. ¶ If Key Vault is down at startup, the fetch or the validation fails and the app refuses to boot. That's deliberate, because half-configured is worse than down. And since deploys go to a preview slot that's health-checked before the swap, a version that can't load its secrets never takes traffic; the running version keeps the secrets it loaded at its own boot. Locally only, the bundle is cached in an AES-GCM encrypted file with mode 600 so restarts are fast. Honestly, that cache key is derived from config values, so it's a convenience, not real protection, and production never uses it. Rotating a secret means changing it in Key Vault and restarting. ¶ LLM provider keys sit in Key Vault like everything else, one vault per environment. To limit damage from a leak, I'd want separate keys per environment, provider-side spend caps and usage alerts, and keys scoped to a single project where the provider supports it, so rotation is a Key Vault edit plus a restart. I haven't verified that spend caps are set on every provider, so I won't claim it. And I'll be straight that we've committed keys to git in the past, and there are still a couple of temporary fallback defaults in the settings model that should be removed and rotated. ¶ On AKS with no secret at all, I'd use workload identity. The pod's Kubernetes service account is federated with a user-assigned managed identity: Azure trusts the cluster's OIDC issuer for that namespace and service account, the pod gets a projected token, and `DefaultAzureCredential` picks it up with no code change. Then Key Vault RBAC gives that identity Key Vault Secrets User for secrets and Key Vault Crypto User for wrap and unwrap on the KEK, nothing more. We already use the same federated idea in CI: GitHub Actions logs into Azure with OIDC, with no stored password.
  - `L1` Where are secrets and key-encryption keys kept? ↳ Azure Key Vault
  - `L2` Where are secrets kept, and how does the app read them at boot? ↳ Azure Key Vault-backed `Secrets` settings; features off until secret exists
  - `L2` Where do your secrets live, and what happens if Key Vault is down at startup?
  - `L3` Where did AiRA keep its LLM provider keys, and how would you limit damage if one leaked? ↳ per-env keys, spend limits, scoped projects, alerts
  - `L3` AiRA keeps credential-vault keys in Azure Key Vault. How would the pods on AKS reach it with no secret at all? ↳ workload identity (federated token) + Key Vault RBAC
- [ ] Session token: one JWT `{sub, iat}` used as an httpOnly cookie (web) or `Authorization: Bearer` (phone, WebSocket); expiry = `iat` + configured hours; logout unbinds the device's push token ↳ FLOWS §3 · `app/entities/dependency_ent.py` · `app/entities/auth_ent.py` `get_user_id` · https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Session_Management_Cheat_Sheet.html · https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7519
  - **Answer:** Every login, whatever the provider, ends the same way: we issue our own JWT with just `sub`, the user id, and `iat`, signed with our secret. The web gets it as an HttpOnly cookie, the phone gets it as `access_token` and sends `Authorization: Bearer`, and the WebSocket handshake goes through the same dependency, which reads the cookie first and then the Bearer header. Expiry isn't an `exp` claim. It's `iat` plus a configured number of hours, 168 today, so changing the config shortens or extends every session already issued. Logout deletes the cookie, and if the app sends its device id, we unbind that device's push token so a logged-out phone stops getting notifications. ¶ Moving the token into a cookie was my change, in August 2025. Before that, after Google login the API redirected to the web app with the token in a `?token=` query string. A token in a URL ends up in browser history, in server and proxy logs, and in the Referer header if the page loads anything third-party. And anything JavaScript can read, an XSS can steal. So I made the Google callback set an HttpOnly cookie, and did the same for the email-code login, which then returned JSON and let the frontend decide where to go. ¶ `Secure` means the browser only sends the cookie over HTTPS. `SameSite` decides whether it rides on cross-site requests: `Lax` sends it on top-level navigations but not on cross-site POSTs or fetches, and `None` sends it everywhere, but browsers only accept `None` together with `Secure`. In August I made `HttpOnly` and `Secure` production-only with `SameSite=Lax`. In September, when I rewrote the Google callback, the cookie outside local became `Secure`, `SameSite=None` on the `.airaai.in` domain; as I remember it, that was because the web frontend called the API cross-site with credentials. Locally, frontend and API were both plain http on localhost, same site, so we didn't need `None`, and a Secure-only cookie isn't reliable over plain http. That's why the flags were production-only. I also made every set and delete call use the same attributes, because a cookie set with one domain can't be cleared with another. Today the cookie is `Secure` outside local and `SameSite=Lax`, since our current web pages sit on our own domain next to the API. ¶ The weak spots, honestly. The token is stateless, so logout can't kill a copy of it until it expires. And the mobile auth-code exchange should check the `purpose` claim; today it decodes any token we signed. I'd add a token version on the user row for revocation and a purpose check on every decode.
  - `L1` Why move the login token from a URL query string into an HttpOnly cookie?
  - `L1` What do `Secure` and `SameSite=None` do, and why did you need them only in production?
- [ ] Google native sign-in (phone): verify the `id_token` ourselves (RS256 via JWKS, `aud`, `iss`, `email_verified`), find by `google_sub` → else link by `email_token` atomically → else create; 409 for external emails Google can't vouch for; 2-try loop on `DuplicateKeyError` ↳ FLOWS §4 · `app/routers/auth/services/auth_s_v1.py` `google_native_signin` · `app/entities/user_ent.py` `resolve_google` · https://developers.google.com/identity/gsi/web/guides/verify-google-id-token · DDIA ch7 "Preventing lost updates" (pdf p255–259)
  - **Answer:** On the phone, Google's SDK proves who the user is, and our server checks that proof itself before it trusts anything. My teammate built the native flow in October; before that the phone used our browser OAuth flow with a deep-link auth code. I know it end to end, so let me walk it. ¶ The user taps Continue with Google. The SDK is configured with `offlineAccess: false`, so we ask only for identity, no Gmail or Drive access; those are separate connectors with their own consent. A returning user gets a 'Continue as' prompt and a silent sign-in with no Google screen, and an in-progress flag stops a double tap. Google hands back an ID token, a signed JWT, and the phone posts it with the timezone to `POST /v1/auth/google`, where the request model forbids extra fields. ¶ The server verifies it, and that's the answer to 'why verify when the phone already did': anyone can POST a made-up token to our API, so the phone's word means nothing. We read the `kid` from the header, fetch Google's public key for it, and check the RS256 signature. Then `aud` must be our client id, so a token Google minted for some other app can't log into ours; `iss` must be Google; `aud`, `exp`, `iat`, `iss` and `sub` must be present and unexpired; and `email_verified` must be a real boolean true. Any failure is a 401; if Google's keys are unreachable it's a 503. For Apple it's the same idea plus a nonce check. In the browser flow the difference is that the server exchanges the authorization code itself, server to server with the client secret, and then verifies the ID token from that response. ¶ Then we find the account. First by `google_sub`, Google's permanent id, because emails can change and the sub never does. If there's no match, by `email_token`, the HMAC of the email, which links an older account that signed up through the browser. That link is one atomic `find_one_and_update` filtered on `google_sub` still being null, so two simultaneous logins can't both link. If nobody matches, we insert a new user. Unique indexes on `email_token` and on `google_sub` back that up, and if a parallel request wins the race we catch `DuplicateKeyError` and go round once more, at most two tries. A new user's scratchpad is created as a background task so login isn't slowed down. ¶ Linking by email is only safe when the provider really owns the address. For a gmail.com or Google Workspace address, Google is authoritative. For a personal domain on a Google account, Google can't prove the person still owns that domain, so we return 409 and the app switches to the browser sign-in automatically. In general, one account per email is enforced by the unique index, and we only merge when the identity provider vouches for the address. In our older email-code login, the one-time code proved the address, and it used the same `email_token` lookup, so it landed on the same account. ¶ Finally we drop Google's token and issue our own JWT, valid seven days. The phone stores it in the Keychain as this-device-only, loads the profile with a 4-second cap so a slow network never blocks sign-in, marks the user signed in, and fires a login event that opens the WebSocket with `Bearer`. From then on every request carries that header through the API client.
  - **Notes:** [Mobile Google sign-in, the full flow chart](notes/aira-mobile-google-signin.md)
  - `L2` Walk me through what happens from tapping "Sign in with Google" to the first authenticated API call.
  - `L2` Why verify Google's or Apple's token on the server when the phone already did? Which claims do you check?
  - `L2` How did you build "Sign in with Google" at AiRA? What did the backend verify? ↳ code exchange server-side, ID token signature, `aud`, `email_verified`
  - `L2` A user signs in with Google and later with email using the same address. How do you link the accounts safely?
- [ ] Google browser OAuth: HMAC-signed `state` + 10-minute `iat` + one-time Redis nonce (get-and-delete), code exchange, `call_type` (auth / docs / delete / connector); the phone gets a 2-minute auth code, never the session in a URL ↳ FLOWS §5 · `app/entities/auth_ent.py` `get_signed_state` / `parse_and_verify_state` · https://developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/web-server · https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc6749#section-10.12 (CSRF and `state`)
  - **Answer:** The `state` parameter exists so that when Google calls our callback, we can prove that this login is one we started, and as I built and ran it through October, it's an HMAC-signed payload plus a one-time Redis nonce. It went through a few versions, and the first one was mine and wrong. ¶ In January 2026 I built the connectors API, and the connect endpoint sent `state=user_id` to Google. That's not CSRF protection at all. A raw user id is guessable and unsigned, and the callback trusted it to decide whose account gets the tokens. So an attacker could send someone a consent link carrying the attacker's id, the victim approves with their own Google account, and the victim's Gmail ends up connected to the attacker's AiRA account. In April I moved connectors onto the HMAC-signed state with the user id as its own `uid` field, and about a week later my teammate added the nonce. The state is `s1.` plus a base64 payload of `iat`, a random nonce, the app and call type, the timezone and optionally the uid, plus an HMAC of that payload. The nonce is also written to Redis with a 10-minute expiry. ¶ On the callback we check three things. The HMAC must match, compared in constant time, so nobody can edit the payload. `iat` must be under 10 minutes old. And we GETDEL the nonce from Redis, which only succeeds once, so a replayed state is rejected as consumed. That's why it's both: the signature proves we minted it and nobody changed it, but a signed value can be replayed any number of times until it expires, and the Redis nonce makes it single use. ¶ Adding Google login to FastAPI, the way we did it: a login endpoint builds the state and redirects to Google with `response_type=code`, the scopes and `access_type=offline`. The callback verifies the state, exchanges the code server to server with the client secret, verifies the returned ID token's signature, audience, issuer and `email_verified`, finds or creates the user, and issues our own session JWT. We use the code flow rather than the implicit flow so tokens never pass through the browser. The `call_type` in the state routes the same callback: `auth` for login, `docs` for the API docs, which only our own domain can open, `delete` for the public account-deletion page, which never creates a user, and `connector`. I added an `upload` type for the AiRA Drive web page. ¶ For the phone, my teammate made sure the session never goes into a URL. The callback redirects to `aira://authcallback` with a two-minute signed auth code, and the app swaps that code for the real session over a POST. The gap I'd still close: the state isn't bound to the browser that started the flow, so a crafted consent link is still possible as a social-engineering attack. I'd add a short-lived cookie or an app-held verifier and compare it on the callback, the way PKCE does. In November the team, led by my teammate Ananda, rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions, so on master that single-use check has to live somewhere other than Redis; what I've described is the version I built and ran.
  - `L2` The OAuth `state` once carried the user id directly. What is wrong with that and what replaced it? ↳ not anti-CSRF; HMAC-signed state with `uid` field
  - `L2` What is the OAuth `state` parameter for? Why sign it *and* store a nonce in Redis?
  - `L2` How do you add Google OAuth login to a FastAPI app (as at AiRA)? ↳ authorization code flow, verify ID token, issue own session/JWT
- [ ] Apple sign-in (iPhone): nonce hash check against replay, Apple JWKS cached in Redis with a refetch on unknown `kid`, spend the one-time code only after verifying, key users by `sub` (email comes once, may be a private relay), signed-JWT `client_secret`, revoke the refresh token on account deletion ↳ FLOWS §6 · `app/adapters/apple_adp.py` `SignInClass` · `app/routers/auth/services/auth_s_v1.py` `apple_signin` · https://developer.apple.com/documentation/sign_in_with_apple/sign_in_with_apple_rest_api · https://developer.apple.com/documentation/sign_in_with_apple/revoke_tokens
  - **Answer:** I built Sign in with Apple end to end, and the short version is: verify Apple's identity token ourselves, check the nonce against replay, spend the one-time code only after that, and key the user by Apple's `sub`, not by email. ¶ The iPhone generates a random raw nonce and gives Apple only its SHA-256. Apple returns an identity token, which is a JWT with that hash inside, an authorization code, and the name and email, but only on the very first sign-in. The app posts all of it to `POST /v1/auth/apple/signin`. To verify, I read the `kid` from the token header and look up Apple's public keys, which I cache in Redis for an hour. If the `kid` isn't there, Apple has rotated keys, so I clear the cache and fetch once more. Then RS256 signature, audience equal to our bundle id, issuer equal to `https://appleid.apple.com`, and `sub`, `iss`, `aud`, `exp` and `nonce` all required. Finally I hash the raw nonce we received and compare it to the nonce in the token. A stolen token is useless without the original raw nonce, and that's what the nonce is for. ¶ Only after that do I exchange the authorization code. Apple's `client_secret` isn't a fixed string; it's an ES256 JWT I sign on each call, with Apple's 180-day maximum expiry, using a dedicated Sign in with Apple key from Key Vault, kept separate from our push-notification key. I first ran verification and the exchange in parallel with `asyncio.gather` to save a round trip, then deliberately made them sequential again. Apple's codes are one-time. If the token turned out to be bad, the parallel version had already burned the code on a login we were about to reject, and then we'd never get a refresh token for that sign-in. One round trip is cheaper than that. ¶ How it differs from Google: Apple needs the nonce check, the email arrives once and may be a private relay address, the client secret is a signed JWT, and Apple requires you to revoke the token when the account is deleted. So we key users by a tokenized `sub` stored in the same `email_token` field Google uses, so one lookup works for both providers. We flag private relay addresses, and the name falls back to 'unknown'. In late June I also built Google and Apple linking by tokenized email with an `apple_sub` lookup for later sign-ins, but a teammate reverted that PR on master on July 1st, and only the guarded migration runner came back, which I re-landed the next day. So today a person who signed up with Google and later uses Apple gets a second account. That's a known gap. ¶ We keep Apple's refresh token, encrypted, mainly for deletion. When a user deletes their account, I decrypt and revoke it at Apple, which fully unlinks the app from their Apple ID. Then we drop their Browserbase browser profiles, and my teammate's concurrent purge deletes every Mongo collection keyed by the user, including the vault's credentials and keys, the connector tokens and the Gmail watch, plus their Qdrant points and Redis keys. What it doesn't do yet: uploaded blobs are left orphaned, Google tokens are deleted rather than revoked at Google, and a session JWT stays valid until it expires because sessions are stateless. Those are the next three things I'd add.
  - `L2` How did you verify an Apple identity token? ↳ JWKS by `kid`, RS256, audience, issuer, nonce = SHA-256 of raw nonce
  - `L2` Why did you make token verification and code exchange sequential again after parallelizing them? ↳ Apple codes are one-time; don't waste on rejected login
  - `L2` What must happen on account deletion? ↳ revoke Apple token, purge sessions and keys, drop Browserbase Contexts
  - `L2` How is Apple sign-in different from Google? What is the nonce for?
- [ ] `email_token`: HMAC of the lower-cased email (or the Apple `sub`), unique index for login lookup, also maps a Gmail push's mailbox address back to users — no plain email stored anywhere ↳ FLOWS §7 · `app/entities/user_ent.py` (index `users_email_token_unique`) · `app/entities/connectors/google_ent.py` `process_gmail_push`
  - **Answer:** `email_token` is a one-way, repeatable fingerprint: the HMAC-SHA256, with our secret, of the lower-cased, stripped email, or of the Apple `sub`. Same input, same token every time, and you can't turn it back into the email. That's how we look users up by email without storing the email. ¶ It's used in three places. First, login: there's a unique index on it, `users_email_token_unique`, which my teammate added, so two accounts can never share an email, and the user lookup is just an equality query on the token. Second, Apple: Apple may never send an email, so I store the tokenized Apple `sub` in the same field, behind one `tokenize_lookup_key` helper, so the same lookup works for both providers. Third, Gmail push, which I built: Google's push only says 'mailbox x changed'. When a user connects Gmail, I store the tokenized mailbox address on their watch row, and on a push I tokenize the address and find every watch for it. Two AiRA users could connect the same mailbox, so it returns all of them. That path never needs a plain email in the database either. The real email exists only as an AES-GCM `encrypted_email` for when we must read it. ¶ Linking works on top of that. For Google, we look up by `google_sub` first, then by `email_token` to link an older account, atomically and only when Google can vouch for the address. For Apple, my June version looked up by tokenized email on the first sign-in, because that's the only time Apple sends the email, and by `apple_sub` afterwards. That linking was reverted, so today Apple users are keyed by the tokenized `sub` alone. ¶ The index story is a good lesson. I put a sparse unique index on `apple_sub`, expecting Google users without the field to be skipped. It still collided. Sparse only skips documents where the field is missing, and Pydantic was writing `apple_sub: null` explicitly on every Google user, so they all had the field with the same null value. The fix was a partial index with a filter of `apple_sub` of type string, so only real values are indexed. Changing options under the same index name needs a drop and recreate on the live database, which is why I built the guarded admin migration runner. In the end we took the `apple_sub` index out of startup, and my teammate later used the same partial-index pattern for `google_sub`. ¶ The trade-offs: you get exact match only, no prefix or fuzzy search on email. And rotating the HMAC secret means re-tokenizing every user. That's doable because we keep the encrypted email and the raw `apple_sub` to recompute from, but it's a migration, not a config change.
  - `L2` How does Google/Apple account linking work without storing plain emails? ↳ lookup by HMAC-tokenized email; `apple_sub` on later sign-ins
  - `L2` A sparse unique index on `apple_sub` still collided. Why? ↳ Pydantic wrote explicit null; partial index on string type
  - `L2` How do you look up a user by email without storing the email?
- [ ] Google connectors (Gmail / Calendar / Drive): user id inside the signed state, same callback as login, save only the scopes actually granted, encrypted tokens, fall back to the sign-in refresh token when Google omits it, a 4xx on refresh marks the grant gone, wake tasks waiting on the connector ↳ FLOWS §8 · `app/entities/connectors/connector_ent.py` `upsert_scope` / `refresh_scope` · https://developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/web-server (refresh tokens, incremental auth)
  - **Answer:** A connector is a second, separate Google consent, for Gmail, Calendar or Drive, layered on top of login. The design rule is: ask for a scope only when the user wants that feature, and store exactly what they granted. ¶ Connecting Gmail goes like this. The logged-in app calls `GET /v1/connectors/connect/gmail` with its timezone. The server builds a signed state with call type `connector` and the user's id inside it, because Google's redirect back to us carries no cookie or Bearer we could trust. The scopes are 'email profile' plus the Gmail scope, with `access_type=offline` and `prompt=consent`. The app opens the URL, the user agrees, and Google calls the same callback we use for login. We verify the state, exchange the code, and verify the returned ID token to learn which Google account was connected. Google returns the granted `scope` string, and we keep only connectors whose scope is actually in it, since a user can untick boxes. Each granted connector is upserted into `ConnectorScopes` with the access and refresh tokens AES-GCM encrypted, one row per user and connector. Then, in parallel, we wake any background task that was sleeping on 'I need Gmail' and start the Gmail push watch, and deep-link back with `aira://connectorcallback?status=success&connectors=gmail`. On first scrape: our earlier version read up to 1,000 emails through the batch API and map-reduced them into a profile. The current flow doesn't bulk-scrape on connect; agents read mail on demand and new mail arrives through push. ¶ The refresh token problem bit us. Google only returns a refresh token on the first consent for a given client and user. On later consents it's simply missing, and the early code ended up with no refresh token for the connector, so the access token died after an hour and every Gmail tool call failed. The fixes were mine. Keep the stored refresh token when Google omits a new one. When the connector callback has none, fall back to the refresh token saved at sign-in. And send `prompt=consent` on connector URLs, which makes Google issue a fresh refresh token. Honestly, `prompt=consent` is the real fix; the fallback is a safety net, because a refresh token belongs to the grant it came from. ¶ Refreshing is `refresh_scope`: decrypt the refresh token, ask Google for a new access token, store it encrypted. If Google answers with a 4xx, the user has revoked access, so we mark the grant gone and the app shows the connector as disconnected instead of retrying forever. A 5xx just propagates without marking anything. If there's no refresh token at all, we raise 're-consent required'. Switching a connector off keeps the tokens; disconnecting stops the Gmail watch, cancels its renewal job, deletes the row and ends the tasks that needed it. ¶ Incremental authorization means requesting scopes over time instead of all at login. My January version used Google's tokeninfo endpoint to read granted scopes and stored an encrypted `user_scopes` row per scope, with retry decorators that refreshed the right scope's token on a 401. Today it's the per-connector `ConnectorScopes` row, built on my teammate's base. We don't merge grants with `include_granted_scopes`, so each connector stands on its own and can be revoked on its own.
  - `L2` Walk me through connecting Gmail: consent URL, callback, token storage, first scrape.
  - `L2` Google returns no refresh token on later sign-ins. What broke and how did you fix it? ↳ keep stored refresh token; `prompt=consent` on connector URLs
  - `L2` What is incremental authorization and how did your per-scope token store work? ↳ tokeninfo for granted scopes; encrypted `user_scopes` row per scope
  - `L2` Google didn't return a refresh token on re-consent. What breaks, and how did you handle it?
- [ ] Gmail push: watch → Pub/Sub → our endpoint; verify the OIDC push JWT (audience + our service account), always 204, then pull the History API in a background task; Redis lease + "again" flag (one reader, no lost mail), compare-and-set `history_id` (idempotent), renew the watch a day early through the IRIS scheduler ↳ FLOWS §9 · `app/entities/connectors/google_ent.py` · https://developers.google.com/gmail/api/guides/push · https://developers.google.com/gmail/api/guides/sync · https://cloud.google.com/pubsub/docs/authenticate-push-subscriptions · DDIA ch11 "Fault tolerance" (pdf p482–484; idempotence)
  - **Answer:** Gmail push is 'push to notify, pull to read', and I built it. Google tells us a mailbox changed; we fetch the changes ourselves. ¶ It starts at connect. I call Gmail's profile and `users.watch` together, pointing the watch at our Pub/Sub topic, and store a watch row with the user, the tokenized mailbox address, the current history id and the expiry. Watches last about seven days, so the IRIS scheduler renews each one a day early. Renewal only re-arms the watch and updates its expiry; it never touches the history id, which only the reader moves. A failed renewal retries in an hour, and a revoked grant stops renewing. ¶ When mail arrives, Gmail publishes to Pub/Sub, and Pub/Sub POSTs to `/v1/connectors/gmail/notify` with a Google-signed OIDC token in the `Authorization` header. That's how we secure it: I verify the JWT's signature, that the audience is our configured audience, and that it was issued for our own push service account with a verified email. Never trust the body alone; anyone can POST 'you have new mail'. The body is just base64 of the mailbox address and a history id. I queue a background task and always return 204, even for bad input, because an error makes Pub/Sub redeliver and you get a retry storm, and answering fast keeps Pub/Sub's deadline from ever waiting on Gmail. ¶ In the background, I find watches whose tokenized address matches and whose history id is lower than the pushed one. That filter alone drops duplicate and out-of-order pushes. For each user, I read the history API since the bookmark, following pages; if the history is too old Gmail returns 404, and I restart from the profile's current history id. I keep INBOX additions, fetch the newest ten in parallel, parse them, and hand one note to the mail trigger, which starts a system turn so Aira can tell the user. Then I move the bookmark only if it still holds the value I read, a compare-and-set, so two readers can never both advance it. ¶ If ten pushes arrive at once, each one sets an 'again' flag in Redis for that user and tries to take a lease. Only one gets it, so there's one reader per user. The reader loops while the flag is up: take the flag with get-and-delete, read, renew the lease. After releasing, it checks the flag once more, because a push could land between its last take and the release, and it would otherwise be lost. So no double reads and no lost mail, and the history-id filter plus the compare-and-set make the whole thing idempotent. ¶ Historically, in late 2025, my first Gmail push sent LLM-summarized digests to WhatsApp. This version wakes the assistant instead. Weak spots: it's at-least-once, so a crash after the note is handed off but before the bookmark moves can repeat a note, and returning 204 on a bad token means a misconfigured audience fails silently except for a warning log. I'd alert on that. That's how it ran through October. In November the team, led by my teammate Ananda, rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions, so there the lease and the flag would have to move to another store; the history-id compare-and-set carries over unchanged.
  - `L2` How does Gmail push work end to end? ↳ `users.watch` → Pub/Sub → webhook → `history` since id → summarize
  - `L3` How do you secure and de-duplicate a webhook like Gmail push? What if 10 pushes arrive at once?
- [ ] Swiggy / Stripe Link connect with PKCE: verifier kept on the server, S256 challenge sent out, one-time random state in a Redis hash, dynamic client registration cached, callback URL built from the configured base URL (never the `Host` header) ↳ FLOWS §10 · `app/entities/connectors/connector_ent.py` `open_state` / `take_oauth_state` · https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7636
  - **Answer:** PKCE is how a client with no secret proves that the one redeeming an authorization code is the one that asked for it, and I used it for both Swiggy and Stripe Link. I generate a random verifier and keep it on the server, send only its SHA-256, base64url encoded, as the S256 challenge, and send the original verifier when I exchange the code. It stops authorization-code interception: a code stolen from a redirect, a log or a malicious app on the same URL scheme is useless without the verifier, which never left our server. ¶ For Swiggy, I wrote the MCP client on the official SDK over streamable HTTP, against three servers: food, Instamart and Dineout. Swiggy uses dynamic client registration, so we register as a public client once per redirect URI and cache it in Redis. On connect I create a 32-byte random state and a Redis hash holding the user, the service and the verifier, with a 10-minute expiry, and the callback reads and deletes it in one Lua script, because Redis Cluster pipelines aren't transactional, so it's strictly one-time. The callback URL is built from our configured public base URL, never the `Host` header, because that client registration is shared by every user and a spoofed Host must never reach it. On top sit 51 hand-declared tools, each with a Pydantic args model that maps our snake_case to Swiggy's camelCase and forbids extra fields, plus an approval tier. ¶ Four tools are never retried: `place_food_order`, `checkout`, `book_table` and `check_payment_status`. If a placement call times out, the order may already exist, and a retry could double-order or double-charge. So the agent is told the order may or may not exist and to read orders back first. Everything else retries up to five times inside a 30-second budget with jittered backoff. And the approval card for an order is built from fresh reads of the cart and address, not from the model's arguments. ¶ Being the client side was harder than I expected. The SDK hides HTTP status codes, so a 401, a 429 and a 503 all came back as the same generic error. I added a response hook that records the status, `Retry-After` and the body, and a classifier that turns them into typed errors. The SDK's task groups must exit in the task that entered them, so each live session is owned by its own task and closed by an event. There's one session per user per server, closed after 10 idle minutes, reopened on an expired-session 404. Server sampling is refused and elicitations are surfaced to the agent. ¶ Stripe Link is how a browser task pays without the model ever seeing a card number. The tool creates one spend request with the idempotency key `run_id:tool_call_id` and a ledger row. The user approves in Link, like entering a UPI PIN, and we poll every two seconds; if Link asks for 3-D Secure, the user gets a bank-verify card and a second window. On approval I fetch the one-time card, fill it into the checkout through the browser agent's secret fill, which keeps values out of the model's context, drop it from memory, and return only the brand and last four. Even our validation errors are raised without their cause, because that error could quote a card number. ¶ Why build Link and Instacart when our users are in India? Honestly, partly building ahead. India has no agent card rail: RBI requires a bank OTP on every online card payment, Stripe Issuing isn't offered there, and Link excludes India, so Indian users pay in the live view. Swiggy is the India rail. Link and Instacart are gated by the country the app reports and stay off until their keys exist. Instacart's self-serve door is two tools with a partner key. They were cheap, and they reuse the same ledger, approval and handoff pieces as everything else.
  - `L2` How does Swiggy ordering work through MCP? ↳ streamable-HTTP MCP client, dynamic client registration + PKCE, 51 hand-declared tools
  - `L2` Which Swiggy tools are never retried, and why? ↳ place order, checkout, book table, payment status; may already exist
  - `L2` How does Stripe Link payment work without the model seeing a card number? ↳ idempotent spend request, user approves + 3-D Secure, card typed via variables
  - `L2` What is PKCE and which attack does it stop?
  - `L3` Instacart and Stripe Link are US/Canada only and your users are in India. Why build them?
  - `L3` You wrote a Swiggy MCP **client** with OAuth dynamic registration and PKCE. What was hard about being the client side?
- [ ] User vault (envelope encryption): per-user DEK sealed by the Key Vault KEK (RSA-OAEP-256, never leaves Key Vault), values AES-GCM with AAD `user:handle:field`, 15-minute DEK cache with one shared unwrap per user, race-safe key minting, background re-wrap on KEK rotation (8 at a time), crypto-shred on "forget all" ↳ FLOWS §11 · `app/entities/vault_ent.py` · `app/adapters/azure_adp.py` `wrap_key` / `unwrap_key` · https://cloud.google.com/kms/docs/envelope-encryption · https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/key-vault/keys/about-keys
  - **Answer:** The vault is where the browser agent keeps a user's saved website logins, and I built it on envelope encryption. Each user has their own 32-byte data key. Every value is sealed with AES-256-GCM under that key. And the data key itself is stored only wrapped, by a key-encryption key in Azure Key Vault called `aira-vault-kek`, an RSA key, using RSA-OAEP-256. The KEK never leaves Key Vault. Mongo holds two things: `UserKeys` rows, with the user, a version, the wrapped data key and the KEK version that wrapped it, and `Credentials` rows, one per user per site, with the handle `login:<site>`, the input fields, one ciphertext per field and the key version. So the data key lives only in wrapped form, plus briefly in process memory. ¶ Why not encrypt every value inside Key Vault directly? Each one would be a network round trip, it costs per operation, and Key Vault throttles. With envelope encryption, I call Key Vault once per user to unwrap, and everything else is local AES. It also means one compromised data key exposes only one user. ¶ Why AES-256-GCM: it's authenticated, so any tampering fails decryption instead of returning garbage. Each value is stored as base64url of the 12-byte nonce, the ciphertext and the 16-byte tag together. The AAD isn't stored, it's recomputed: `user_id:handle:field`. AAD is data that's authenticated but not encrypted. Binding to it means a ciphertext copied into another user's row, another site's row, or swapped from the password field into the username field fails to open. Nonces are 12 random bytes from `secrets` on every seal. I'll be precise: with random nonces the guarantee is probabilistic, not absolute. What makes it safe is that each per-user key seals only a handful of values, far below the roughly four billion messages per key that the guidance allows for random 96-bit nonces. ¶ Unwrapping is a network call, so the plain data key is cached in memory for 15 minutes. If 20 requests for the same user miss at once, one unwrap task runs and the rest await it through `asyncio.shield`, so one caller cancelling doesn't kill it for the others. There's an epoch counter: revoking bumps it, so an unwrap already in flight can't re-cache a key for a user we just forgot. The risk is that a plain key sits in process memory for up to 15 minutes, visible to a memory dump or a debugger. To shrink that window we forget it on revoke, on account deletion, and when the browser task that needed it ends; what's left is the 15 minutes while a task is running or after an API save. Minting is race-safe: a unique index on user and version, and if two saves both try to create version one, the loser gets `DuplicateKeyError`, re-reads and uses the winner's key. ¶ Rotation is where envelope encryption pays off. Key Vault keeps KEK versions. New wraps always use the current version and record it, and unwraps always use the exact version that wrapped, so old keys keep opening. At startup, a background pass finds active data keys on an older KEK version and re-wraps them, eight at a time behind a semaphore. Each write is a compare-and-set, so a key retired meanwhile stays retired. The data is never re-encrypted; only the 32-byte keys are. For zero downtime: create a new KEK version, let the pass re-wrap, watch the logged count of stale keys reach zero, and only then disable the old version. Disable it early and those users read as 'vault unavailable'. ¶ Across many instances, every instance would run that pass at boot, so you'd pay duplicate Key Vault calls and risk throttling. Correctness holds, because the compare-and-set makes the second writer a no-op. Through October we ran one instance by decision; with more, I'd put the pass behind a Redis lock or run it as a one-off job. Since the team's November rewrite runs two API regions without Redis, the one-off job is what I'd pick now. And since it only runs at boot, a rotation waits for the next deploy. ¶ Crypto-shredding means deleting the key instead of chasing every copy of the data. 'Revoke all' deletes every credential row, retires the active key by blanking `wrapped_dek` and setting `retired_at`, forgets the cached key and deletes the browser profiles. It refuses with 'in use' if a live task is holding a site. The proof for the user is the retired key row with its timestamp, and any leftover ciphertext can't open. One honest caveat: a database backup taken earlier still holds the wrapped key, and the KEK is shared and still alive, so someone with unwrap rights and an old backup could open it. True shredding across backups needs a per-user KEK you delete, or backup retention shorter than the promise. ¶ If a developer with production read access dumps the vault tables, they get wrapped keys and AAD-bound ciphertext. Neither opens without `unwrapKey` on the KEK, which only the app's managed identity holds. They do see which sites a user saved logins for, because handles are plaintext. The emails and OAuth tokens elsewhere are encrypted under the app's data-encryption key, which is a Key Vault secret, not something in the database. The real threat left is anyone who can run code as the app identity. ¶ Audit is the gap. There's no per-decrypt ledger today; the credential row stamps when it was last used and how the last fill went, and logs carry only ids. I'd add an append-only ledger of user, handle, run and time, readable by the user in the app and by a small on-call group. One-time codes typed on the phone go to Redis as `otp:<run_id>`, encrypted with the app key, for five minutes. Reading one leaves it in place so a failed fill can retry, and it's deleted only once it has been typed successfully.
  - `L1` KEK wrap algorithm? ↳ RSA-OAEP-256
  - `L2` What is associated data and what did you bind into it? ↳ `user_id:handle:field`; ciphertext moved elsewhere won't open
  - `L2` Where does the data key live, and in what form? ↳ only wrapped by Key Vault RSA-OAEP-256 KEK, with version
  - `L2` Why RSA-OAEP-256 wrapping in Key Vault instead of encrypting every value in Key Vault directly? ↳ latency, cost, rate limits; one unwrap per user key
  - `L2` How does key rotation work? ↳ at boot, re-wrap stale keys with current KEK version, 8 at a time
  - `L2` What is crypto-shredding and how does "revoke all" use it? ↳ blank wrapped key; every old ciphertext becomes unreadable
  - `L2` The data key is cached in memory 15 minutes. Why, and what are the risks? ↳ avoid unwrap per call; epoch counter, `forget_user` after task
  - `L2` What is envelope encryption? How do you rotate the master key without re-encrypting the data?
  - `L2` What is crypto-shredding?
  - `L2` Why AES-256-GCM? What do you store next to each ciphertext? ↳ nonce, key id/version, auth tag, AAD fields
  - `L2` How do you generate nonces, and how do you guarantee you never reuse one under the same key?
  - `L2` What did you bind as AAD so a ciphertext copied to another user's row fails to decrypt?
  - `L3` What is AAD in AES-GCM, and what attack does binding to `user:handle:field` stop?
  - `L3` Explain AiRA's credential vault: AES-256-GCM data keys wrapped by Azure Key Vault (RSA-OAEP-256), rotation at boot, one-time codes in Redis.
  - `L3` What are the risks of rotating keys at boot across many instances?
  - `L3` How would you rotate the vault's master key with zero downtime?
  - `L3` What audit log do you keep for every decrypt, and who can read it?
  - `L3` A developer with production DB read access dumps the vault table. What can they do with it?
  - `L3` How would you let a user delete all stored credentials and prove they are gone?
  - `L3` Design per-user encryption so one compromised data key exposes only one user's credentials.
- [ ] WebSocket auth + Redis pub/sub fan-out: authenticate on the handshake, register the socket on this worker, publish to the `notify` channel so any worker can reach a socket that lives on another; cleanup in `finally` ↳ FLOWS §12 · `app/routers/websocket/services/websocket_s_v2.py` · `app/entities/notifications_ent.py` · https://redis.io/docs/latest/develop/interact/pubsub/
  - **Answer:** The phone holds one authenticated WebSocket, `/v2/ws`, and the rule is: authenticate on the handshake, register the socket on the worker that owns it, and let any other worker reach it through Redis pub/sub. ¶ Auth uses the same dependency as our HTTP routes. A FastAPI dependency typed as `Request` doesn't get injected on a WebSocket route, so the fix is to type it as Starlette's `HTTPConnection`, the common base of `Request` and `WebSocket`, which has headers and cookies. I did that first in February when I moved notifications to a WebSocket. Our March codebase reset dropped it, so I re-applied it in April, and a few days later I added the `Authorization: Bearer` path for the phone. The dependency reads the session cookie first and then `Authorization: Bearer`. Native mobile WebSocket clients can't set cookies, and browser WebSocket APIs can't set headers, so accepting both covers both. After accept, the socket goes into this process's map from user id to a set of sockets. A set, because one user can have a phone and a tab open, and both should get every event. A dead socket is dropped on its first failed send, and cleanup runs in `finally` with independent steps under `asyncio.gather`. ¶ The path there was mine, in steps, in February 2026. First, SSE with a Redis Stream per user: `XADD`, then an approximate `XTRIM` to about a thousand entries, and replay on reconnect using `Last-Event-ID` and an `XREAD` drain loop. Streams were in the middle for replay: a durable per-user log, so a client that dropped could catch up. The next day I replaced Streams with Pub/Sub, because Mongo was already the source of truth and the app reloads state over REST on reconnect, so the stream was a second copy with memory to trim and no expiry. That version had one subscriber per instance and a routing map from user to a set of bounded asyncio queues feeding the SSE responses. Three days later I moved it to a WebSocket, because the phone has to send audio and commands up, not just receive. A teammate added binary audio frames the next day, and my teammate Ananda later rebuilt the router in our restructured codebase and built the v2 socket with direct dispatch in July. ¶ Cross-worker delivery, as we ran it through October: anything produced off the socket's process, an agent finishing, new Gmail, a cron, is published on the Redis channel `notify` with the user id. Every instance subscribes at startup, checks its own map, and only the owner sends. The subscriber needs its own Redis connection, because a connection in subscribe mode can only run subscribe-family commands and sits in a blocking read. Ours has no read timeout, so an idle listen isn't treated as a failure, while the normal client uses 5-second timeouts. I also made it self-healing: reconnect with backoff from one second doubling to 30. ¶ One nuance about voice. Since that v2 direct dispatch, the realtime turn runs on the process that holds the socket, so its audio frames and screen updates go through a direct local path that never touches Redis. Redis pub/sub is for everything produced elsewhere. And through October we ran a single instance by decision. In November the team, led by my teammate Ananda, rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis and deployed two API regions, so this fan-out is the design I built and ran, not what master does now. ¶ Scaling horizontally: pub/sub broadcasts every message to every instance, which is fine up to a few dozen instances. Beyond that I'd keep a presence map from user to instance and publish to a per-instance channel, or shard by user hash. It's also fire-and-forget: if nobody is listening, the message is gone. That's acceptable only because state lives in Mongo. A known gap is that the listener awaits each send inline, so one slow client can delay others on that instance. The SSE version actually had bounded queues per connection; I'd bring that back as a per-socket queue with a writer task. ¶ Token expiry mid-connection, honestly: we check the token only at the handshake. The 20-second ping with a 60-second timeout is liveness, not auth, so a socket can outlive its token. I'd record the token's expiry at accept, close with an app-specific close code when it passes, between turns so it never cuts a sentence, and let the app refresh and reconnect.
  - `L2` A user's event is produced on worker A but their SSE connection is on worker B. How did it reach them? ↳ one Redis SUBSCRIBE per instance wakes the right local queue
  - `L2` Why does a subscribed Redis connection need to be separate from the normal client?
  - `L2` How did you make the same auth dependency work for HTTP and WebSocket routes? ↳ retyped `Request` to Starlette `HTTPConnection`
  - `L2` Native mobile WebSocket clients cannot set cookies. How did auth work then? ↳ `Authorization: Bearer` header accepted on the WS route
  - `L2` One user, two devices or tabs. How does the server fan out to both? ↳ routing map holds a set of sockets per user
  - `L2` The stream for a turn may run on a different worker than the one holding the socket. How does output reach the phone? ↳ publish on Redis notify channel; socket owner forwards
  - `L2` With several workers, how does an event on worker A reach a WebSocket on worker B?
  - `L3` How did AiRA bridge SSE → Redis Streams → WebSocket, and how does that scale horizontally?
