  - `L3` How do you send messages to a user whose WebSocket is on another worker or pod? ↳ Redis pub/sub or Streams fan-out
  - `L3` Explain the AiRA SSE → Redis Streams → WebSocket path. Why put Redis Streams in the middle?
  - `L3` How do you authenticate a WebSocket connection, and what happens when the token expires mid-connection? (Tie to AiRA voice.)
- [ ] File upload with SAS URLs: short-lived, write-only signed URL; bytes go phone → Blob Storage; the API only confirms and enriches ↳ FLOWS §13 · `app/adapters/azure_adp.py` `get_upload_url` · https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/common/storage-sas-overview
  - **Answer:** The bytes never touch our API. The phone or browser uploads straight to Azure Blob Storage with a short-lived signed URL scoped to one blob, and the API only hands out permission and checks the result afterwards. ¶ The flow has three calls. First, `POST /files/upload-url` with the size and MIME type. The server rejects anything over 50 MB or outside the MIME allowlist, builds the blob key itself from the user id and a fresh file id, so the client can't choose a path, and returns a SAS URL valid for three minutes. It's a user-delegation SAS, signed with a temporary key fetched through our managed identity, not with the storage account key. Second, the client PUTs the bytes directly to Blob. Third, `POST /files/{id}/process`: the server confirms the blob exists, re-checks the real size, because a SAS can't enforce the size the client declared, has Gemini write a title and description, and saves the record. Why: a 50 MB upload through FastAPI would tie up memory and bandwidth on the same workers serving realtime voice. ¶ The upload stack itself is my teammate's. My parts: the AiRA Drive web page, where users upload PDFs and images straight to Blob with client-side checks and a per-file status tray. Download links are signed only after an ownership check, and a batch download keeps only the ids the user owns. And file search, including semantic search over attachments in Qdrant, exposed to the model as a tool. ¶ For storage in general at AiRA: user uploads go through that SAS path, under a key the server builds from the user id and file id. Things the server generates, voice audio, generated images and film pictures, are uploaded by the server itself and served back through signed read URLs. ¶ Weak spots I'd fix. The SAS permission set is read, add, create and write; for an upload I'd narrow it to create and write. And on account deletion, blobs are left orphaned and unreachable rather than deleted, so I'd add a blob delete or a lifecycle rule to the purge.
  - `L2` AiRA Drive uploads go straight from browser to Azure Blob. Walk me through it. ↳ signed upload URL → browser PUT → backend process call
  - `L3` Where did AiRA or Jigsaw store files (audio, resumes, generated images) and how were they served?
- [ ] Security hygiene: leaked keys in git history, per-route ownership checks, guarded admin migration endpoints
  - **Answer:** Three habits here: secrets never in code, every query filtered by owner, and production changes going through a guarded path instead of a laptop. I'll be honest that we learned all three by getting them wrong first. ¶ Leaked keys: yes, and several were mine. In September 2025 I hard-coded an Exa key in source and moved it to settings the next day. In December 2025 I put Telegram, Razorpay, Periskope and WhatsApp tokens as overrides in the settings file, and in January local WhatsApp gateway credentials, which I took out later that month. Teammates committed others. What I learned is that removing a key from the file doesn't remove it from history; once it's pushed, treat it as exposed and rotate it. The fix that stuck was structural. Every secret is a `SecretStr` field loaded from Key Vault at boot, and an optional integration simply stays off until its secret exists. What prevents it now, honestly, is that plus review. There's no automated scanner in this repo's CI yet, and there are still two temporary fallback defaults in the settings model that need to go. In my own open-source engine's CI I run gitleaks, and I'd add the same here as a pre-commit hook and a CI gate, plus push protection on the repo. ¶ Ownership checks: I've shipped this bug myself. The speaker rename and delete endpoints I built in May 2026 took a speaker id without scoping it to the user, and a teammate added the owner scoping. Earlier, my first conflict-list endpoint had a count that wasn't filtered by user. The fix for the class of bug is a rule in our AGENTS.md, which my teammate wrote: every client-supplied id is cross-validated against the authenticated user and the parent resource before any read, write, signing or delete. In code that means `user_id` goes inside the query filter itself, not a separate check before it, and a miss returns 404, so you can't even probe whether an id exists. The vault, for example, refuses a task id that isn't yours with 'run not owned', which the API turns into a plain 404. What's still missing is enforcement. I'd add a data-access layer where every user-owned query requires a `user_id`, and a test per route that calls it with another user's id and expects a 404. ¶ Admin migrations: I built the runner when the `apple_sub` index had to be dropped and recreated on the live database. `POST /v1/admin/migrations/run` only accepts allowlisted admins, built on my teammate's allowlist, comparing the tokenized email in constant time. It's a dry run by default that writes a JSON report, and it only applies when you type the phrase 'RUN MIGRATION'. Scripts are chosen by filename only, validated against path traversal, run as an asyncio subprocess, and the report path is accepted only inside the reports folder. Every run goes into a `MigrationRuns` ledger. The index script refused to run if Apple users already existed. One honest blemish: after a revert and re-land, the 'drop' script ended up containing the recreate step, so its name no longer matches what it does. And the fuller framework, with dependency ordering, a lock and rollback, stayed on a branch.
  - `L2` How did you make running a production migration from an admin endpoint safe? ↳ allowlisted admins, typed phrase, dry run, path checks, run ledger
  - `L3` API keys and tokens were committed to the repo in the past. What did you do, and what process prevents it now?
  - `L3` Code review found routes with missing ownership checks (memory detail, speaker rename). How do you stop that class of bug? ↳ owner filter in every query; lint rule; tests per route

**Learn:** your own repos and `~/Desktop/airaai/aira_temp/contribution-audit/bullets.md`, `docs/DHANESH_*.md`
(code-verified numbers) · resume `career/profile/resume.tex` · LinkedIn copy `career/profile/linkedin-live-snapshot.md`.
Practise by recording yourself explaining each one in 5 minutes.

## 1.10 AiRA — product, architecture and stack
- [ ] The pitch, your role and the timeline: product phases, what you built first, proudest and hardest systems, competitors
  - **Answer:** AiRA is a voice-first AI assistant: you talk to it like a person, it remembers you across sessions, it paints its answer on the screen while it speaks, and it can run real tasks in the background, like research, your Gmail and Calendar, or a cloud browser, with your approval. The user wants one assistant that knows them and gets things done. It's in beta on iOS and Android, and I don't have a user number I'd stand behind, so I won't guess. ¶ I'm founding engineer and team lead: I lead a four-person frontend and backend team, own system design and PR reviews, and work directly with the CEO. In practice there was rarely a spec: I'd take a product idea, write the design doc, build the backend and often the client half. I also wrote several of the team's AGENTS.md coding rules and about 34 design docs. I don't log time, so no percentages for a typical week; git shows I ship on both repos, 89 backend and 32 app commits in September 2026, plus reviews. ¶ The product went through five phases. I joined in late July 2025 on the Next.js web chat, building auth and the streaming client, then moved to the backend in August to build memory: Redis working memory, a Memgraph graph and Qdrant retrieval. From autumn 2025 to January 2026 it was a WhatsApp assistant: I integrated three WhatsApp providers, then self-hosted WAHA, plus Gmail and Calendar agents and IRIS reminders. In February 2026 we moved to our own mobile app, and in May I built live voice mode on Deepgram, then Cartesia. In August I built **Omni**, which replaced live mode, chat and onboarding, and I deleted about 17.8k lines in one commit, which my resume rounds to 17,000. In October it became a persistent agent with durable background tasks. The direction was the CEO's call; the technical push was that WhatsApp gave us no voice or screen of our own, and three pipelines did one job. My latest commits, end of October, are Gmail push, choice handling in approvals and browser permission modes. Since November the team works on Ananda's backend rewrite on master; what I describe is what I built and ran through October. ¶ The system I'm proudest of is Lumen, the text-to-video engine I wrote alone. The model writes a JSON script, Lumen validates and repairs it scene by scene, and every frame is a pure function of time, so one bad scene is dropped instead of blanking the film. The one I'd delete is the Memgraph graph memory I co-built with a teammate; the traversals had to avoid the user's own node, which links to everything. I switched its traversal off myself in October 2025 in favour of simple scratchpads, and the March 2026 reset removed it. A second database for an unproven idea. ¶ The hardest problem was interruptions in Omni: keeping the stored conversation true to what the user actually heard. The model stream, the TTS receiver and phone playback run at once, and a cancel can land anywhere. So each audio frame carries exactly the words it speaks, and the server records the words it really shipped. When a reply is stopped, `stop_voice` cancels the TTS receiver before it closes the audio window, so audio Cartesia already made never plays, and the turn is sealed to the heard words. Since October 20 whether to interrupt is the app's call, a teammate's hold-and-judge flow; the server stops cleanly and records honestly. An answer held or cut off is carried into the next turn as "never heard", with the reason: held, interrupted or overtaken. That took iterations from August 22 to September 17; Shashwat worked on the "unheard output" part with me. ¶ On commits, the audit counts 1,528 of mine in aira-api: 398 merges and 1,086 unique non-merge patches. By subject prefix it's roughly 445 feat, about 100 fix and about 370 refactor, though my subjects were often wrong. The merges come from the v3 rewrite, when three of us rebuilt in parallel and I kept pulling teammates' branches in, and from restacking my work into stacked PR branches in August. ¶ I haven't measured voice versus typed usage. Every Omni turn row records audio and mode, so it's one aggregation away. We also didn't have one agreed success metric for most of my time. There was an internal retention dashboard, and at the end of October I proposed a north star, current-user return on value days, in our core-loop doc. It isn't shipped, so I haven't seen it move. ¶ How so much in 15 months? Honestly, not alone. Memory, the Jev gate and the LongMemEval result were shared with teammates. I reused one spine: a typed tool framework and later one tool registry, so each new agent was mostly declarations. And I build with an AI coding assistant, which several of my commits credit. Was the deleted work wasted? Mostly no. Live mode taught us the STT, barge-in and turn-buffer pieces Omni reused, and activities became guided runs. The social Ask and Posts feed was the real waste, and I removed it myself in August. From AiRA I'd reuse the gated tool registry, the durable run-row state machine, the write-ahead turn log and Lumen. ¶ ChatGPT voice, Gemini Live and Pi have better models and far more scale, so I wouldn't claim we beat them at talking; what differs is the product around the voice. It streams a UI the app renders natively while it speaks, keeps long-term memory, and runs durable tasks that act behind approvals and a vault, so passwords never reach the model. Any of them could build that; our edge is focus, not a moat.
  - `L1` Tell me about AiRA in two sentences. Who is the user and what problem does it solve?
  - `L1` What was your title and what did "founding engineer" mean in practice day to day?
  - `L1` Is AiRA live? Which platforms, how many users, and is it beta or GA? ↳ beta on iOS and Android; know honest user numbers or say you don't track them
  - `L2` The product pivoted several times. Walk me through the phases and what drove each change. ↳ web chat → WhatsApp assistant → mobile live mode → Omni → persistent personal agent
  - `L2` What did you build first when you joined in July/August 2025, and what are you working on now?
  - `L2` Pick the one system you are proudest of and explain why that one.
  - `L2` Pick the one system you would delete if you could, and why.
  - `L2` What is the hardest technical problem you solved at AiRA? Go deep on one.
  - `L2` What does a typical week look like for you: coding vs reviewing vs design vs talking to the CEO?
  - `L2` How many of your ~1,500 aira-api commits were features vs merges vs fixes? Why so many merges? ↳ ~400 merges; integration during v3 rewrite; stacked PR branches
  - `L2` "Voice-first AI assistant" — what fraction of real usage was voice vs typed text?
  - `L2` What metric did the company use to decide a feature worked? Did you ever see it move?
  - `L3` You list memory, voice, browser agent, reminders and video. That is a lot for one person in 15 months. How?
  - `L3` Several systems you built were later deleted (live mode, activities, Ask/Posts, speaker ID). Was that wasted work?
  - `L3` If AiRA shut down tomorrow, which of your work would you reuse at your next job and why?
  - `L3` Who are your competitors (ChatGPT voice, Gemini Live, Pi) and what can AiRA do that they cannot?
- [ ] Architecture in boxes: one FastAPI monolith, which datastore holds what, request path vs background, what fails when a store is down
  - **Answer:** One FastAPI monolith, aira-api, plus two side services. The phone hits `api.airaai.in`, which goes through Azure Front Door to an App Service container running uvicorn, over REST plus one authenticated WebSocket, `/v2/ws`. The turn loop, agent manager and crons run in that process. Mongo, on the Cosmos Mongo API, is the source of truth: users, sessions, turns, agent runs, tasks, approvals and vault rows. Redis is hot state: locks, slot counters, the per-turn log, screen deltas, one-time codes and the pub/sub `notify` channel the older features use. Omni's own frames, audio included, skip Redis: since a teammate's v2 direct dispatch in July they go straight to the socket this process holds. Qdrant holds memory and attachment vectors, Blob holds files, Key Vault holds secrets and the vault's wrapping key, and Memgraph held the graph until March 2026. Outward: six LLM providers, Cartesia, search APIs, Google, Swiggy, Stripe Link and Browserbase. The two side services were aira-api-bg, a 12-hourly consolidation of the shared knowledge store that I co-built with a teammate, and aira_relay, my WhatsApp MCP server. One framing note: this is what I built and ran through October. In November Ananda's first-principles rewrite merged to master with no Redis, native Cosmos DB, no APScheduler and two API regions; that's the team's later change, not my work. ¶ v3, or MACH, is the layout the team reset to in mid-March 2026: controller for HTTP only, service for the use case, entity for business logic, DB models, prompts and tools, and adapter as the only layer touching an SDK or a secret. It's hexagonal without ports, because we have one implementation per vendor and adapters already return our own neutral types. Part of the pull was one-way imports, since the old layout had circular-import trouble. The reset was a teammate's; my part was the big merges and later the coding rules. ¶ Request path versus background: if the user is waiting to hear or see it in this turn, it stays in the path, under a timeout. That's the ownership check, context loads in one `asyncio.gather`, the Jev gate at 1.5 s, the planner at 4 s, memory at 3 s, then the model streaming into TTS. Everything else is detached, long work becomes a durable agent row, and periodic work is a cron. ¶ **Redis down** on my version: boot pings it, so a restart fails. At runtime voice keeps working, because Omni frames don't touch Redis. What breaks is quieter: turn-log appends are best-effort, so turns finish but lose their crash evidence; cron firings can't take their lock; slot counters stop admitting background tasks; screen deltas and vault one-time codes fail; and older features on `notify`, like live session titles, go silent until the subscriber reconnects with backoff. For Qdrant, in 2025 I made the API boot with vector features off, but that guard didn't survive v3; from today's code I'd expect boot to fail, untested. At runtime a slow Qdrant costs only the memories through the 3 s timeout, but a fast hard error isn't caught there and would fail the turn. That's a gap. Mongo down means nothing works; the phone gets `omni.process_failed`. ¶ Redis and Mongo hold the same data for different jobs: Redis is the hot copy for a read every turn, Mongo the durable one. Short-term memory in 2025 was a Redis list with a 30-minute TTL on logout, PERSIST on login, and read-through rehydration from Mongo on a miss. The turn log is the same: Redis appends during the turn, one sealed Mongo row at the end. ¶ The single point of failure is the single instance, a deliberate choice that boot recovery even assumes, plus Mongo. The per-turn bottleneck is model latency: `TurnUsage` records milliseconds for gate, planner, retrieval, first token and total, and the model stages are where a turn waits; I never timed store calls separately. We never load-tested, so beyond that it's a guess. My suspects are the one shared Cartesia TTS socket per worker and sends awaited inline: on the Omni path a slow phone only slows its own turn, but the shared `notify` listener awaits each send in one loop, so one slow client there delays others. ¶ Redrawn, I'd keep the direct socket path and add a bounded writer queue per socket, make recovery safe for more than one instance from day one, drop Memgraph, and give agent workers their own pool. For 20 engineers the seams already exist: voice gateway, turn orchestration, agent runtime, connectors, memory, identity. I'd split the voice gateway and agent runtime first, because they scale differently.
  - `L1` Draw AiRA's backend in boxes: clients, API, workers, queues, databases, LLM providers, external APIs.
  - `L1` Which datastores does AiRA use and what lives in each? ↳ Mongo source of truth; Redis hot state/locks; Qdrant vectors; Memgraph graph; Azure Blob files
  - `L1` Monolith or microservices? How many deployable services were there? ↳ one FastAPI aira-api; separate aira-api-bg consolidation service; relay
  - `L2` What is the "v3 / MACH" layout (controllers, services, entities, adapters) and why did the team move to it?
  - `L2` What runs inside the request path and what runs in the background? How do you decide?
  - `L2` What happens when Redis is down? When Qdrant is down? When Mongo is down? ↳ Qdrant: API boots with vector features off; know the others honestly
  - `L2` Why does the API need both Redis and Mongo for the same data (e.g. short-term memory)?
  - `L3` What is the single point of failure in this architecture today?
  - `L3` Where is the bottleneck: LLM latency, Redis, Mongo, or the WebSocket workers? How do you know?
  - `L3` If you redrew it from scratch with what you know now, what boxes would change?
  - `L3` How would you split this monolith if the team grew to 20 engineers? Where are the seams?
- [ ] Stack choices: FastAPI, MongoDB, Qdrant, Memgraph, Redis, Azure, APScheduler, OpenRouter, Browserbase — and the trade-offs
  - **Answer:** Quick answers: the backend is FastAPI. The graph database was Memgraph and the vector database is Qdrant. Omni uses Cartesia for both STT and TTS, and Deepgram was the STT vendor before it. The browser stack is Browserbase plus Stagehand. Much of the base stack predates me: FastAPI from May 2025, Redis from July, Qdrant added by a teammate in early August. Memgraph I co-own: I wrote its first schemas on August 9, 2025, the same day a teammate swapped the Neo4j setup for Memgraph. So I'll say which choices I'd defend and which I'd change. ¶ Why Python and FastAPI rather than NestJS, which I used at Jigsaw? The work is I/O-bound: model calls, databases and sockets, so native async fits. The AI SDKs are Python-first, and we used numpy, Pillow and HDBSCAN. Pydantic gives us request validation and LLM schemas from the same models. The cost is CPU work under the GIL, which we push into `asyncio.to_thread`. Why MongoDB over Postgres? Our shapes are nested and keep changing: turns with typed parts, run rows with checkpoints, waits and an inbox. With a Pydantic model as the schema, a document fits. Honestly, Postgres would have given us joins, transactions and pgvector in one box, and I'd consider it seriously today. ¶ On Qdrant versus pgvector, Pinecone or Weaviate: we didn't run a bake-off, so I won't pretend we did. What we use are payload filters on `user_id` with payload indexes, named vectors in the feed prototype, an async client, and the fact that it self-hosts in Docker. Pinecone is managed but you can't self-host it. pgvector would remove a datastore, which is the real trade-off. FastEmbed gives local 384-dimension ONNX embeddings. That means no API call, latency or per-call cost on each memory write or search, and text never leaves our process. The price is weaker embeddings than the big APIs, and CPU on the API box, so it runs in a thread. ¶ Why a graph at all? The idea was to walk out from the topics in a question to related memory that text similarity misses. Memgraph speaks Cypher over Bolt, so the neo4j driver works unchanged and we could swap to Neo4j, it's in-memory and fast, and every query was scoped by `user_id`. It was our weakest bet. I switched graph traversal off myself in October 2025, and the March 2026 reset removed it. ¶ Why Redis for queues, locks, pub/sub and short-term memory instead of Kafka or RabbitMQ? We already ran it, our volumes are small, and anything that needs durability lives in a Mongo row, not in a queue. I actually tried durable first. In February 2026 I built SSE with a Redis Stream per user and replay. The next day I replaced it with plain pub/sub, because Mongo was already the source of truth and the app reloads over REST on reconnect. Kafka would buy replay and partitions we didn't need. ¶ Why Azure over AWS or GCP? That decision predates me, and I won't invent the business reason. What we lean on is Key Vault with managed identity for all secrets, Key Vault RSA wrapping for the vault's per-user keys, Entra auth for Redis, Blob with SAS links, and App Service slot swaps. Why APScheduler inside the API rather than Celery beat or a cloud scheduler? We ran one instance with a handful of async jobs, so a separate beat process was overhead. In August 2026 I made it multi-instance safe with a Redis `SET NX EX 50` lock per firing. IRIS settles due rows in one `bulk_write` before running them, so a reminder fires at most once even if the process dies. The cost is that the scheduler dies with the API, but the sweep catches up because it queries everything due up to now. Ananda's November rewrite on master has since removed APScheduler. ¶ OpenRouter sat in front through 2025 until the v3 reset. It gave us one key and many models, so each persona could route to its own model. A teammate put OpenRouter first in the chain, then OpenAI, then Gemini, and the same day I restructured the error handling and added JSON repair. It cost us an extra hop, and we had less control over provider features like Anthropic cache markers and later native async tools. In v3 we moved to direct SDKs behind our own facade. ¶ We went with Browserbase and Stagehand after I ran a measured feasibility study: 21 sessions, 8 tests. Parallel tabs in one Chrome worked, and secret fill through Stagehand variables kept passwords out of the model. A Browserbase Context kept the cart across release and a crash. We also get a captcha solver, a live view so the user can take over, and we pay per browser-minute instead of for idle VMs. It also showed the costs. Stagehand's action cache hit 0 of 103 actions on Amazon, because the cache service returned 413. Stagehand can't re-attach after a restart. Each action took a 7.2 s median, and the finder's tokens dominate. Our own Chrome fleet would mean owning anti-bot, captchas, sessions and scaling, too much for a small team. ¶ Mongo has no joins, and that hurt in the admin dashboard in January 2026. The first version ran about ten count queries per user. I replaced it with one aggregation on users: `$lookup` into seven collections and `$filter` to count tasks by status, so it's one round trip. Was five datastores over-engineering? At peak we ran Mongo, Redis, Qdrant, Memgraph and Blob. Memgraph was over-engineering: it should go first, and it did. Redis earned its place then; the team's November rewrite on master, Ananda's, removed it and moved to native Cosmos DB. I'd keep Mongo, a vector store and Blob.
  - `L1` Why Python and FastAPI for the backend instead of Node/NestJS, which you used at Jigsaw?
  - `L1` Why MongoDB over PostgreSQL for the main store?
  - `L1` Which web framework is AiRA's backend? ↳ FastAPI
  - `L1` Graph database? ↳ Memgraph
  - `L1` Vector database? ↳ Qdrant
  - `L1` Speech vendor in Omni for STT and TTS? ↳ Cartesia
  - `L1` Which STT vendor came before it? ↳ Deepgram
  - `L1` Browser stack? ↳ Browserbase + Stagehand
  - `L2` Why Qdrant over pgvector, Pinecone or Weaviate? ↳ payload filters/indexes, named vectors, self-host; know the trade-off honestly
  - `L2` Why Memgraph over Neo4j? Why a graph database at all? ↳ Cypher, Bolt driver compatible, in-memory speed; user-scoped traversals
  - `L2` Why Redis for queues, locks, pub/sub and short-term memory instead of Kafka or RabbitMQ?
  - `L2` Why Azure (Blob, Key Vault) and not AWS or GCP?
  - `L2` Why APScheduler inside the API instead of Celery beat or a cloud scheduler?
  - `L2` Why OpenRouter in front of model providers? What does it cost you? ↳ one key, many models, fallbacks; extra hop, less control over caching
  - `L2` Why FastEmbed / local 384-d embeddings in places instead of an embedding API?
  - `L2` Why Browserbase + Stagehand instead of running your own headless Chrome fleet?
  - `L3` Mongo has no joins. Show me a query where that hurt and how you worked around it. ↳ `$lookup` aggregation replacing N-per-user count queries
  - `L3` You run five datastores with a small team. Was that over-engineering? What would you cut?
- [ ] Incidents and debugging stories: a cached coroutine, sync clients blocking the event loop, Redis OOM, braces in f-string prompts
  - **Answer:** The worst bug I shipped myself was a synchronous client inside async code. On September 18, 2025, restructuring the LLM fallback code, I switched the OpenRouter client from `AsyncOpenAI` to the synchronous `OpenAI` class inside async methods, and my Exa search client the next day had the same pattern. A sync call inside a coroutine blocks the event loop for the whole model call, so nothing else on that worker runs: other users' streams freeze and unrelated endpoints wait on someone else's LLM call. There's no error; it just looks like "slow under load". My teammate Ananda moved it back to `AsyncOpenAI` on October 1, and another teammate replaced Exa with Perplexity on September 27. To find it: asyncio debug mode logs slow callbacks, or fire two concurrent requests and watch them take twice as long. ¶ The personality cache is a cousin. My teammate Ananda had put `functools.lru_cache` on an async function, which caches the coroutine object, not the result, so the second call with the same id gets an already-awaited coroutine and raises. On October 21, 2025 I replaced it with an `asyncio.Lock`-guarded `OrderedDict` LRU of resolved values; the weak spot I left is size 1 with no invalidation. ¶ Redis out of memory is my postmortem, because part of the cause was mine. Detection: the team hit Redis OOM in early July 2026; teammates made their own Redis changes that week, and I looked in my own code. My root cause: for speaker identification, live mode appended every mic chunk to a per-session key in every mode, with no TTL, and only passive mode ever popped it. At 16 kHz 16-bit that's about 1.9 MB a minute per session, forever. Fix, on July 2, in the `fix/redis_oom` PR, which held only my commit: buffer only in passive mode, pipeline `APPEND` with `EXPIRE NX 60` so a buffer lives at most a minute, and delete it on reconnect, stop and release. Prevention: the same day I built a Redis memory monitor, a locked minute cron snapshotting `INFO memory` and `DBSIZE`, plus an on-demand family scan capped at 100 keys per pattern with `MEMORY USAGE`, and the next day I removed speaker ID, dropping about 47 packages including torch. I don't have a user-impact number, and I can't say my keys were the only cause. Now I'd require a TTL on every key family. ¶ Braces, in September 2025: the storage prompts were Python f-strings with JSON examples inside, and an unescaped `{` there is parsed as an expression, so the prompt raised a format error. I escaped the examples, and I also doubled braces in the user's text, which honestly was wrong: a value interpolated into an f-string isn't parsed again, so that only showed the model doubled braces. User braces only bite if the finished string is formatted a second time. The real lesson is keeping template and data apart, which our later `{{variable}}` renderer does. WhatsApp, November 2025: my Meta Cloud API send URL put the recipient's number where our business `phone_number_id` goes, so sends failed; I fixed it the same day. ¶ Consolidation, October 2025: the prompt builder read `payload["summary"]`, but the records store text in `content`, so every cluster reached the model empty, and the next step overwrites the old records with the output. The commit doesn't say how I spotted it; the guard I'd add is refusing an LLM call with empty inputs. `list_events`: the code compared the HTTP status to the `fastapi.status` module itself, `exc.status == status`, never true, so a 401 never triggered the token refresh. I fixed it in October 2026 to check `HTTP_401_UNAUTHORIZED`. ¶ CPU work: the Resemblyzer voice encoder ran in `asyncio.to_thread` until I removed it with torch, and the film pipeline's Pillow and numpy cut-outs and FastEmbed run in threads too. That keeps the loop free, and those libraries release the GIL for most of their heavy work. I never measured the latency effect. ¶ Commit messages that don't match the diff are fair criticism: "Refactor code structure" was 21k lines of agent runtime, and the speaker-ID deletion was also called a refactor. It costs reviewers, blame, bisect and release notes, and my own audit had to read every diff. Now it's one change per commit with an honest subject.
  - `L1` Tell me about the worst production bug you shipped at AiRA.
  - `L2` Personality prompts were cached with `functools.lru_cache` on an async function. What went wrong? ↳ cached the coroutine, not the result; lock-guarded LRU of values
  - `L2` A sync OpenAI client inside async code: what symptom does that produce, and how do you find it?
  - `L2` The team hit a Redis out-of-memory problem. What did you find in your own code? ↳ per-session speaker audio buffers; `APPEND` + `EXPIRE NX 60`
  - `L2` Users who typed `{` or `}` broke the chat. Why? ↳ unescaped braces in f-string prompts
  - `L2` The WhatsApp send URL used the recipient's number. How did that show up and get fixed? ↳ must use business `phone_number_id`
  - `L2` The background consolidation sent empty inputs to the LLM. How did you notice? ↳ prompt read the wrong payload field
  - `L2` `list_events` never refreshed an expired token. What was the bug? ↳ compared status to the `fastapi.status` module, not 401
  - `L3` FastAPI is async. Where did sync code block the event loop and how did you find it? ↳ sync OpenAI/Exa clients inside async methods
  - `L3` Python GIL: did CPU work (Resemblyzer, Pillow/numpy) ever hurt latency? What did you do? ↳ `asyncio.to_thread`; later removed torch dependency entirely
  - `L3` Commit messages often didn't match the diff ("Refactor code structure" adding 21k lines). Why is that a problem, and what did it cost the team?
  - `L3` Pick one incident and run it as a postmortem: detection, impact, root cause, fix, prevention.
- [ ] Hindsight and scale: what you'd change, what breaks first at 10× / 100×, sweeps and shared graphs at scale, debt left behind
  - **Answer:** Three things I'd do differently. One: design for more than one instance from day one, with sticky socket routing and a bounded writer queue per socket. Audio already goes straight to the socket on our version, but sends are awaited inline and recovery assumes one process. Two: fewer stores, so no Memgraph, and probably Postgres with pgvector or Mongo plus Qdrant and nothing else. Three: tests and evals as part of done from day one: state-machine invariants, replays of real turns, a tool-choice set. For context, this is about the system I built and ran through October; in November Ananda's rewrite on master removed Redis, moved to native Cosmos DB and deployed two regions, which is the team's answer to some of this. ¶ At 10× the single instance breaks first, and adding a second breaks recovery, because boot seals every leftover turn buffer as failed, including the other process's live turns. Next is the voice path on each box: one shared Cartesia TTS socket per worker multiplexes every speaking user, downstream audio is about 384 kbps per speaking stream, and sends are awaited inline. Omni frames go direct, so Redis isn't in that path; only the older features fan out over one `notify` channel that every instance receives. Mongo and Qdrant are fine at 10× on the indexes we have. LLM spend grows linearly; it's the biggest bill, not the first break. At 100× I'd shard everything by `user_id`, since every query is already user-scoped: Mongo shard key on user, Redis, which already ran in cluster mode, keyed by the user hash tag that `slots:{user_id}` already uses, and sticky socket routing so events go straight to the owning instance. ¶ The minute sweeps read due rows on an index: IRIS settles them in one `bulk_write`, and the agent sweep runs four stages bounded by a semaphore of 8 under a 50-second lock. It stops working when one instance can't finish a minute's due set within the minute. Then I'd partition the sweep by user hash, or push timers into a delay queue keyed by due time, so work arrives instead of being scanned. ¶ Memgraph is in-memory, so one shared graph has to fit one machine's RAM. User-scoped indexes, depth shrinking from 6 to 3 and a 200-node cap keep each walk bounded, but a million users' writes land on one box. I'd split graphs per tenant or move edges into an adjacency table. Honestly, we never got there; we removed it. ¶ Durable agents at 3 slots times 1M users: slots are a cap, not a load, and most rows sleep. The infra is a separate worker pool claiming queued rows, timers in a delay queue, and Browserbase and provider rate limits. On cost, my only measured basis is the feasibility study: 1.5 to 4 minutes and about 100k to 200k finder tokens plus 14k to 50k worker tokens per add-to-cart on gpt-4.1-mini. I haven't multiplied that out, so no dollar figure. ¶ Debt I knowingly left: the single-instance assumption, inline socket sends, best-effort turn-log appends, no automated turn-loop tests, no check that the prompt cache hits, and losing in-flight async tool calls on restart. For a beta, iteration speed mattered more, and each one is written down. The feature I'd never have built had we talked to users first is the social side: the Ask opinions and the news-to-debate Posts feed I built from May to July 2026, which I removed myself in August. I didn't run user research then, and that's the lesson.
  - `L2` If you rebuilt AiRA from scratch, what three decisions would you make differently?
  - `L2` What breaks first at 10× users: WebSockets, Redis, Mongo, Qdrant, Memgraph or LLM spend?
  - `L2` What breaks first at 100×? What would you shard, and by what key?
  - `L2` The minute sweeps (reminders, agent upkeep) scan Mongo. When does that stop working?
  - `L2` Per-user Memgraph traversals in one shared graph: how does that scale to a million users?
  - `L3` Durable agents at 100×: 3 slots × 1M users. What does the infra look like, and what does it cost?
  - `L3` What technical debt did you knowingly leave behind, and why was that the right trade-off at the time?
  - `L3` Which feature would you never have built if you had talked to users first?

## 1.11 AiRA — tools, agents and LLM plumbing
- [ ] Typed tools and agent loops: Pydantic schemas, hidden credential injection, progressive disclosure, APEX, registry gates, async tools
  - **Answer:** Tool calling: you give the model tools, each a name, description and JSON schema, and instead of text it can reply with a structured call, a name plus JSON arguments. It never runs anything. Your code validates the arguments, runs the function, and sends the result back as a tool message with the same call id, and the model continues. An agent loop repeats that until the model answers without calling a tool, or until you stop it with a step cap, a timeout, stuck detection, a budget, or an explicit `done` tool. ¶ I built our typed tool framework in November 2025. Each tool is a `FunctionSpec` with an argument model, a return model and an async function that takes one validated object. The schema the model sees comes from `model_json_schema(mode="serialization")`, run through a cycle-safe pass that inlines every `$ref` and `$defs`, and every argument has a description. The executor validates the model's raw arguments with Pydantic before calling anything. Credentials never appear. `access_token` and `user_id` are `Field(exclude=True)`, so they're absent from the schema. The pipeline decrypts the user's Google token, and the loop injects it and the user id just before execution. Later versions inject them from the function's annotations, with the token decrypted per connector. ¶ Progressive disclosure fixes prompt bloat. Every tool's full schema in every prompt is big and slow, and choice gets worse. In December 2025 the orchestrator saw only agent names and descriptions, and a `GET_TOOLS_INFO` step loaded one agent's schemas. In April 2026 that became `get_agent_info` and `execute_agent_tool` over 8 agents. In August 2026 the unified registry made it general. Deferred tools sit in a catalog baked into the system prompt at startup, `tool_search` loads their schemas, and `execute_tool` runs them. `tool_search` tries a lexical match first, then falls back to cosine similarity over embeddings precomputed at startup and returns the top three, so "scheduling" finds the IRIS tools. ¶ APEX, in April 2026, was a separate agent because long jobs like canvas plus note plus email need many tool calls in a row, and we wanted them off the chat turn, reporting each step to the app. It streamed Claude tool use and ran all of a step's calls in parallel, matched back by `tool_use_id`. It had three meta-tools, `get_agent_info`, `execute_tool` and `get_skill`, 20 iterations, a 180 s per-tool timeout and a 90 s stream idle timeout, with live state in Redis and checkpoints in Mongo. The **circuit breaker** stopped the run after 5 consecutive turns in which every tool failed. On April 30 I flattened it into the main loop. Two agents meant hand-offs, a second state in every prompt and duplicated tokens; now one model had `get_agent_info` and `execute_agent_tool` plus `get_skill`, the loop went from 8 to 20 iterations, and the user saw each step live. ¶ The unified registry, from August 2026, has one declaration per tool: schema, handler and harness metadata (which agents, deferred or not, surfaces, required connector, and later an approval tier). Every dispatch runs fixed gates in order: resolve the name, check the calling agent, check the surface, check the connector is linked, validate the schema, then run the handler. The provider never sees the metadata, so tool arrays stay byte-identical, which keeps the prompt cache warm. Boot fails if any tool lacks a valid tier. ¶ For bad arguments, I tell the model. Crashing kills the turn, and blind retries repeat the same mistake. A tool error with the validation message usually gets fixed on the next step. The registry answers an unknown tool with "search for it first", and it's capped: three invalid `done` calls fail a background run. ¶ To stop loops running forever or burning money, there are layers. Iteration caps: the old orchestrator went from 25 to 5, the flattened main loop has 20, APEX had 20. Timeouts: 180 s per tool and 90 s on the stream. Then the circuit breaker. In durable agents there's **stuck detection**: the same tool with identical arguments three steps in a row that got back nothing new, or five such no-progress steps of any calls, stops the run with "no progress at" that tool. Plus a step budget per skill, 3 slots per user, and tool results capped at 40,000 bytes. ¶ Async tools: say a tool takes 40 seconds. In September 2026 I upgraded the OpenAI SDK for native async tool calls. The tool returns a pending output, the model tells the user it's on it, and the turn seals early. When the result lands, it re-enters on its original call id as a new system-triggered turn, and the model speaks it. Calls still running are listed in the volatile "now" message, so the model doesn't start them twice, and orphaned calls are repaired. The trade we accepted is that a restart loses in-flight async calls. ¶ Do I know the model picks the right tool? Not measured. We had tool events and every background model call stored on its run, so we read failures by hand. To measure it, I'd build a golden set of utterances, each labelled with the expected tool or "no tool", and run it nightly against the real tool arrays. I'd track exact-match accuracy and `tool_search` recall in the top three, plus argument-validation failures per tool in production.
  - `L1` What is tool calling / function calling? How does the model "call" a function?
  - `L1` What is an agent loop? When does it stop?
  - `L2` Async tools: a tool takes 40 s. What does the user hear and how does the result come back? ↳ turn seals early; result returns on same call id as system turn
  - `L2` How did your typed tool framework turn a Pydantic model into a schema the LLM sees? ↳ `model_json_schema`, cycle-safe `$ref` inlining, descriptions on every arg
  - `L2` How do access tokens and user ids reach a tool without the model ever seeing them? ↳ `Field(exclude=True)`; injected just before execution
  - `L2` What is progressive tool disclosure and what problem did it solve? ↳ agent summaries first; full schemas only for chosen agent
  - `L2` What was APEX and why did you build it as a separate agent? ↳ Claude loop, parallel tools, 3 meta-tools, 180 s tool timeout
  - `L2` What is the circuit breaker in APEX? ↳ stop after 5 consecutive turns where every tool failed
  - `L2` Why did you flatten APEX into the main chat loop? ↳ one model, fewer hand-offs, simpler state, user sees steps live
  - `L2` What is the unified tool registry and what gates run on every dispatch? ↳ resolve, agent, surface, connector linked, schema, then handler
  - `L2` How does `tool_search` find a deferred tool? ↳ lexical match plus cosine over embeddings precomputed at startup
  - `L2` Bad tool arguments from the model: crash, retry, or tell the model? ↳ return a tool error the model can fix
  - `L3` How do you stop an agent loop from running forever or burning money? ↳ iteration caps (25→5, later 20), timeouts, stuck detection, token budgets
  - `L3` 8 agents, ~100 tools. How do you know the model picks the right tool? Did you measure it?
- [ ] Durable task runtime: run-row state machine, waits and inbox, guarded transitions, boot recovery, minute sweep, stuck detection
  - **Answer:** A durable agent is a background task whose state lives in a database row, not in a Python coroutine. It survives restarts, it can sleep for hours waiting on the user, and it resumes at the next step. A plain asyncio task dies with the process, can't wait days and leaves no record. I built this runtime on October 7 and 8, 2026. The task kinds are research, canvas_doc, connector and browser. ¶ The main model calls `start_task` with a kind, skill, title, brief and optional parent run. It checks, in order: browsing is enabled on this server; the skill exists under that kind, or it lists valid names; every service the skill needs is connected, or it tells the model to show a Connect card; and the parent run is one of the user's finished tasks. Then a `queued` row goes into `AgentRuns`. Admission is a Redis `INCR` on `slots:{user}`; over 3, it decrements and stays queued; otherwise an atomic FIFO claim in Mongo, decrementing again if the claim fails. ¶ The state machine goes queued, running, then sleeping or paused, back through queued, and ends in ready, failed or stopped. A `wait` says why it sleeps, like a question, credential, approval, payment or timer, each with a deadline; the `inbox` holds what wakes it, like a steer, an answer or an approval. Waking moves the sleeper to queued and pushes the inbox entry in the same write, and the inbox drains only while the row is running. ¶ Every write goes through two guarded store methods. `update` is a `find_one_and_update` with filters. `transition` only moves a row from an expected status. A late writer, say a timeout after the task finished, matches nothing, so a finished run never reopens. ¶ At boot, `recover()` deletes every slot counter. It re-adopts each running row that has a checkpoint, takes one slot for it and wakes it with a restart note. A running row with no checkpoint fails as "server restarted mid-run". Then every user's queue is served, and the minute sweep waits until recovery finishes. It assumes a single instance, which is how we ran through October; Ananda's November rewrite on master runs two regions without Redis, so this exact recovery is my version, not what's deployed now. ¶ The minute sweep runs four stages, each bounded by a semaphore of 8, under the cron lock. First, due IRIS wakes and idle Chrome releases. Second, overdue sleepers. Timer and payment waits wake to re-check, approvals re-deliver their outcome, and question, credential, connect and blocker waits end as ready with a line saying what's missing. Third, slot-counter drift. Fourth, lost "done" turns not announced after 60 seconds, approval expiry, and checkpoint retention at 90 days. ¶ A task reports back as system turns. `[AGENT_PROGRESS]` means "so far, still looking". `[AGENT_NOTE]` carries a question. `[AGENT_DONE]` is the only one spoken as finished, with the findings fenced as external data. Plus task frames and a push. Stuck detection lives in the shared loop: the same tool with identical arguments three steps in a row that got back nothing new, or five such no-progress steps, stops the run and records "no progress at" that tool. ¶ Why Mongo and Redis instead of Temporal, Celery or a cloud workflow service? I wrote the trade-off into the plan. Extending the run row needed no new infrastructure. It fits our layering, and the primitives already existed: guarded claims, a checkpoint field, a sweep. Temporal meant a new server and rewriting the agent loop as deterministic workflow code right before a deadline, and Celery doesn't model human waits that last days. The cost is that we own timers, recovery and once-only behaviour, but the state machine sits behind one store, so it can be swapped. ¶ The `ask_main` restart case: a restart caught a task right after it asked the main model something. In the October build, on adoption, I scanned the checkpoint for an `ask_main` call answered "asked" with no user line after it. The task went straight back to sleep on that same ask instead of running and asking twice. After I split asking into `ask_main` and `ask_user` on October 19, recovery became simpler: the adopted task wakes with a note saying its last question may not have reached the user, to re-check and ask again only if it still needs to.
  - `L1` What is a durable agent and why can't a long task just be an asyncio task?
  - `L1` What kinds of background tasks exist? ↳ research, canvas_doc, connector, browser
  - `L2` Walk me through `start_task`: what is checked before a row is queued? ↳ browsing enabled, skill exists, services connected, valid parent run
  - `L2` Draw the state machine of a run row. ↳ queued → running → sleeping/paused → ready/failed/stopped
  - `L2` What is a `wait` and what is the `inbox`? ↳ why it sleeps with deadline; steer/answer/approval messages to wake it
  - `L2` How do you make sure a late writer can never reopen a finished run? ↳ guarded `transition` only from expected status via `find_one_and_update`
  - `L2` What happens at boot to runs that were mid-flight? ↳ reset counters; resume rows with checkpoint; fail rows without
  - `L2` What does the minute sweep do? ↳ due wakes, deadline expiry, counter drift, lost "done" turns, checkpoint retention
  - `L2` How does a task report back into the conversation? ↳ `[AGENT_PROGRESS]`, `[AGENT_NOTE]`, `[AGENT_DONE]` system turns, push notification
  - `L3` How does the agent loop detect it is stuck? ↳ same tool + args 3×, or 5 steps of already-seen results
  - `L3` Why build this on Mongo + Redis instead of Temporal, Celery or a cloud workflow service?
  - `L3` A process restart caught an `ask_main` mid-settlement. How did you avoid running it twice? ↳ scan checkpoint for unsettled ask; sleep on it again
- [ ] LLM providers and cost: model per job, fallback chain, prompt caching with stable prefixes, strict structured output, cost per turn
  - **Answer:** Prompt caching means the provider keeps the processed state of a prompt's beginning. A later request that starts with exactly the same bytes reuses it, so it's cheaper and the first token comes faster. It matches by prefix, so one changed byte early on invalidates everything after it. That's why the order has to be: static tools and system prompt first, then per-user, then per-session append-only, then volatile per-turn content last. The providers AiRA used are OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, DeepSeek, Groq and Cerebras, through OpenRouter in 2025 and through direct SDKs since v3, plus the Jev evaluation model on TypeSafe. ¶ The 2025 fallback chain tried OpenRouter, then OpenAI, then Gemini. A teammate put OpenRouter at the front on September 18, 2025, and the same day I restructured the error handling and added `json_repair` before Pydantic, which fixes trailing commas, missing quotes or brackets and prose around the JSON. On the version I ran, the facade falls back from Cerebras to Groq for the small GPT-OSS calls. ¶ On caching, precisely: the Anthropic marker code was Shashwat's and Ananda's, and the OpenAI cache key Omni uses is Shashwat's. My finding was that the new `stream()` facade never passed the flags, so no markers were being sent. On August 19 I added `CacheSpec` (system, tools, messages, a boundary index and a 5-minute or 1-hour TTL), carried it through the facade, and made other providers ignore it. I put the breakpoint on the message holding history. Five days later I saw the history was a sliding window, raised from 5 to 10 turns, so its leading bytes changed every turn. I split the fixed preamble out and moved the boundary onto the preamble alone, the last message whose bytes outlive a turn. That's also why memories and the "now" block go last, and why skills are appended after the tail: anywhere above the breakpoint they'd invalidate the session's prefix. Tool arrays must be byte-identical across sessions for the same reason. Tools sit at the front of the prefix, so one reordered field misses for everyone; the registry keeps its metadata off the wire. ¶ Did I verify hits? Not systematically. Cached tokens are recorded per stage in `TurnUsage` and per call in Ananda's observability layer, but nothing tracks a hit rate, and I never used it to confirm the breakpoint move, though our own caching doc says to check it first. When I looked by hand in working sessions, ordinary calls on Luna were around 99% cached and a landed async result re-read everything after the call once; that's my observation, not a dashboard number. A tracked hit rate is the first thing I'd add. ¶ Model per job comes down to the latency budget and the shape of the output. Jev answers typed yes, no or choice questions inside 1.5 s. The retrieval planner and session titles use GPT-OSS 120B on Cerebras, because the planner sits on the critical path, so throughput beats depth. The main turn was Sonnet 4.6, then OpenAI's Luna after my teammate Shashwat moved it on August 28. Memory notes use DeepSeek V4 Flash and Pro, the warm-start speaker uses V4 Flash, the film planner uses Sol, and attachments use Gemini Flash Lite. As for strict structured output rejecting our Pydantic models: strict mode needs every property listed as required and `additionalProperties: false`, and Pydantic leaves fields with defaults out of `required`. So `strict_schema(require_all=True)` closes every object and lists every property. Tool schemas stay lenient, because an optional argument really is optional. ¶ Cost: I don't have a cost per Omni turn. `TurnUsage` records tokens and milliseconds per stage, so it can be computed, and Ananda added per-call cost estimates at the end of September and widened them across providers in November. For a browser task, the only measured basis is the feasibility study: 1.5 to 4 minutes, about 100k to 200k finder tokens plus 14k to 50k worker tokens per add-to-cart. The Gmail onboarding scrape, up to 1,000 emails, was never costed. The per-user budget was a teammate's energy system: 1,000 units a day, with an LLM call costing tokens over 1,000, rounded up, times a model-tier multiplier of 1.0, 1.5 or 2.0. ¶ When Omni moved from Sonnet to an OpenAI model, the voice path changed in four ways. Caching went from explicit markers to automatic prefix caching with a cache key. Assistant tool calls had to be encoded as Responses items, which I'd already fixed in the codec on August 20. I added a reasoning-effort knob. And in September I added native async tools. One gotcha I hit in a working session: the model writes a commentary line before its tool calls, and the SDK parsed that line as the structured answer, dropping the calls. So we never pass a response model on a call that offers tools, and the structured answer comes from a separate tool-less call. ¶ If a provider has an outage mid-voice-turn, here's what actually happens. The main model has no cross-provider fallback. If its stream errors, the turn publishes `omni.process_failed` instead of vanishing, and whatever was already spoken stays spoken. The side calls degrade gracefully: the planner times out at 4 s to a plan built from the raw query, memory at 3 s to no memories, and a slow Jev only costs its head start. If TTS fails, the rest of the turn goes out as text. What should happen is one retry on a second provider for the main model, a short spoken apology, and an alert. ¶ On streamed tool-call fragments: the OpenAI-compatible streams from Groq, DeepSeek and Cerebras send a tool call in pieces, with the id and name first and the arguments split across chunks. In August 2026 I assembled fragments by index into complete calls before parsing the arguments.
  - `L1` What is prompt caching and why must the cached part be a stable prefix?
  - `L1` Name the model providers AiRA used. ↳ OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, DeepSeek, Groq, Cerebras, via OpenRouter and direct
  - `L2` How did the OpenRouter → OpenAI → Gemini fallback chain work? What does JSON repair fix?
  - `L2` What did you actually do for prompt caching? ↳ wired `CacheSpec` through the facade; markers were teammates' code
  - `L2` Where did the cache breakpoint go in Omni, and why did you move it five days later? ↳ history window slid each turn; boundary moved onto fixed preamble
  - `L2` Why put volatile context (memories, "now") at the end and skills after the tail?
  - `L2` How did you choose which model for which job (gate, planner, main, storage, titles)?
  - `L2` Strict structured output rejected your Pydantic models. Why? ↳ strict mode needs every property required, `additionalProperties: false`
  - `L2` What is the cost of one Omni turn? Of one browser task? Of one onboarding scrape?
  - `L2` What was the energy/budget plan for limiting per-user LLM spend? ↳ daily units; tokens × model-tier multiplier; built by teammate
  - `L3` Omni later moved from Sonnet to an OpenAI model. What changed in the voice path when the provider changed?
  - `L3` Why must tool arrays be byte-identical across sessions? ↳ keeps provider prompt-cache prefix stable
  - `L3` Did you ever verify cache hits? How? ↳ not measured in code; doc says check `cached_tokens` first
  - `L3` A provider has an outage mid-voice-turn. What should happen and what actually happens?
  - `L3` Streamed tool-call fragments differ by provider. What broke and how did you assemble them?
- [ ] Observability and testing: trace_id per turn, Langfuse vs a Mongo llm_calls log, Slack alerts, what was tested, agent evals
  - **Answer:** In December 2025 I put one `trace_id` per chat turn through every stage: the pre-LLM router, the orchestrator, the main stream, the storage call, speech-to-text, the Gmail summarizer and the memory flush. Every OpenRouter call sent a request id and an `llm_role` such as `pre-llm-pipeline`, `main-llm` or `storage-llm-pipeline`. I stored the trace id on the chat turn too. The role is what makes it useful: you see which stage of a turn costs the time and the tokens, not just the total. ¶ In January 2026 I stood up a self-hosted Langfuse v3 stack in Docker Compose (web, worker, Postgres, ClickHouse, Redis and MinIO), remapped its ports and wrote the team's setup guide. Every model call became a generation under one root span per turn. What I owned is the deployment, ports, guide and integration, not Langfuse itself. About ten days later I replaced it with a Mongo `llm_calls` collection: trace id, user, role, model, tokens, input and output, behind one `LLM_LOGGING_ENABLED` flag. It dropped a six-container dependency and its keys for the one thing we used, per-call tokens by trace; we lost the UI. ¶ `TurnUsage`, which I added in September 2026, records milliseconds and tokens, including cached tokens, per Omni turn for the gate, planner, retrieval, reviews, every model iteration with its first token, and total. It's stored on the turn row; Ananda's usage dashboard showed it briefly before he moved usage into his observability module at the end of September. Every background model call is also stored on its run as an iteration. For crons and webhooks, I added an async context manager in January 2026. It catches an exception inside its block and posts to Slack with the job name, error type, the last 1,000 characters of the traceback and context fields. I wrapped it per user in the IRIS loop, so one failure no longer stops everyone, and around background webhook handlers, where errors had been invisible. The Slack base was a teammate's. ¶ Testing the voice pipeline, honestly, was mostly manual. I drive the app in the iOS simulator, tail its console through Metro's Hermes inspector, and read the turn rows in Mongo. On the client I wrote Jest tests for the barge-in cancel in `screenVoice`, the speaker queue, the cue speaker and the wake-word pipeline, but they were dropped in the app's Expo migration in September. On the backend I instrumented speech latency, meaning TTS first audio and STT connect and first transcript, into a p50 to p99 panel with two unit tests on the summarizer. That was removed with the analytics subsystem in July. So automated coverage of what I built is thin: today it's essentially CI on Lumen. ¶ Done properly, I'd test a voice pipeline at three levels. Use recorded audio fixtures through the real STT. Use fake STT, TTS and LLM to fire a barge-in at every point and assert the sealed turn equals the heard words. And run a timing harness that measures mic end to first audio byte. ¶ Why were my pytest files dropped from the stacked PRs? The original commits had them, and the stacked copies I made only carried product code. I also took pytest out of dev dependencies in August. What it says is that tests weren't part of our definition of done. Teammates removed the last backend tests in September and October, and on October 27 I added `tests/` to `.gitignore` myself, so tests are local only and neither repo tracks unit tests today. We leaned on Ruff with every rule on and `ty` with all errors in CI, plus live runs on devices. I'd reverse that first. ¶ How do I evaluate an agent change? For the main model, mostly vibes plus targeted harnesses, and I'll say that plainly. For the browser agent, the feasibility study had 8 tests with written pass criteria. For films, Lumen has a paint recorder that checks what the engine actually drew, and a phone-scale report on text size, contrast and crowding. Deployment through October was Azure: Front Door in front of App Service with a health-checked slot swap. Ananda owns CI/CD; I have zero commits in `deploy.yml` or the Dockerfile, and since November his pipeline deploys two API regions. My deployment-side parts were Key Vault key wrapping for the user vault (loading secrets from Key Vault was Ananda's), Blob uploads with ownership checks, and multi-instance safety: cron locks, the pub/sub cancel broadcast and slot counters. ¶ With one week for testing, I'd start with the durable state machine and approvals against a real Mongo container. That means guarded transitions, `recover()` on crafted rows, slot-counter rollback, and an approved call running exactly once, because a bug there sends an email twice or spends money. Next I'd build a turn replay harness from stored turns with a scripted model, then a tool-choice golden set. ¶ For the browser agent's eval set, I'd use briefs across sites with a recorded expected end state, like the cart contents or stopping at the sign-in wall. I'd grade by reading the page, not by trusting the model's word, and add safety cases: deny-listed pages and prompt injection. For the film planner: briefs that must compile in Lumen with no dropped scenes, every object rendered, text size and contrast checks, a short human rubric for teaching quality, and cost and latency per film.
  - `L2` How did one `trace_id` per turn flow through every LLM call, and why tag each call with an `llm_role`?
  - `L2` You self-hosted Langfuse, then replaced it with a Mongo `llm_calls` log. Why? ↳ drop dependency and keys; one collection behind a flag
  - `L2` What did you own in Langfuse, and what not? ↳ deployment, ports, setup guide, integration; not Langfuse itself
  - `L2` What did `TurnUsage` record, and where could you see it? ↳ ms and tokens for gate, planner, retrieval, first token, total
  - `L2` How did cron and webhook failures become visible? ↳ async context manager posts traceback and context to Slack
  - `L2` How did you test the voice pipeline? Be honest about what was automated.
  - `L2` What automated tests exist for the things you built? ↳ few: wake-word Jest, barge-in tests, latency units, Lumen CI
  - `L2` How do you evaluate an agent change before shipping it — evals, golden sets, or vibes?
  - `L2` How was the backend deployed, and what part of deployment did you own? ↳ Azure; teammate owned CI/CD; know your parts: Key Vault, Blob, multi-instance safety
  - `L2` How do you test a voice pipeline?
  - `L3` Your pytest files were dropped from the stacked PR copies. Why, and what does that say about test culture?
  - `L3` If you had one week to add testing to AiRA, what would you build first and why?
  - `L3` How would you build an eval set for the browser agent or the film planner?
- [ ] Research agent and web tools: Exa, Perplexity, Serper verticals, Parallel extraction, rounds, typed Findings, delta updates
  - **Answer:** An assistant needs web search because the model's knowledge stops at its training date and is shaky on specifics: prices, opening hours, news, reviews. Search grounds the answer and gives you sources. Over time AiRA used four kinds of web tools. First Exa search, which I integrated in September 2025. Then Perplexity, after a teammate swapped Exa out later that month; another teammate added multi-search, and I wrapped it as a web-search tool for the orchestrator in December. Exa came back in January 2026 through a teammate. Then Serper Google verticals: image, video and maps in June 2026, then places, news, reviews and shopping in July. And Parallel.ai, from July, to read pages. ¶ The intent gate I built in 2025 was one structured call per message. It returned topics to search, a rewritten search statement, a `webSearch` flag and a progress message. Web search was the default unless the question was about the user's own data or AiRA itself. We searched with the rewritten statement because the raw message is full of pronouns and context, like "what about the second one?", while the rewrite stands alone and is searchable. "Fresh web results win" was a rule in the answer prompt: when the web and the model's memory disagree, trust the web, and anchor "today" to the UTC time we pass in. Without it you get stale facts and wrong dates. ¶ Parallel's `url_extract` takes one to five URLs plus an objective. It returns excerpts focused on that objective, up to 10k characters per page or the full content, with a 40k total cap, failures reported per URL, and sources for citation. The cap is about context cost, latency and focus, because a full page drowns the answer. The Serper verticals I added were places, news, place reviews, sortable lowest-rated first to find dealbreakers, and shopping. The tool specs teach batching: one maps search, then reviews for every candidate in parallel. ¶ The research agent, from September 2026, runs in the background. The main model calls `create_research` with a brief. The worker runs a Luna tool loop over Google search through Serper, maps and places, and page reads, and finishes by filling a typed `Findings` model. Later the closing verdict became a separate tool-less structured call. It works in rounds where every call in a round runs at once. Round one is every search you can name now. Round two is everything that needed round one's ids and URLs, done for all options together. A third round happens only for a fact the brief needs. Latency is paid per round, not per call, and the only reason to wait is a real dependency. That keeps the calls few and the cost down. ¶ While it runs, it talks to the main model through numbered delta updates, each carrying only results no earlier update carried, and only when they're worth seeing, usually once after round one. It can ask the main model one question, and the answer comes back as steering. Page reads that land late slot into their own round, and deltas are tracked per result, so nothing is lost or repeated. ¶ Venue details have to come from tools because otherwise the model writes hours, prices and ratings from memory, which look plausible and can be invented. In July 2026 I made maps search the default venue tool and wrote the rule that venue details come from tools, never memory. On the synchronous Exa client, which was mine: a sync SDK call inside an async method blocks the event loop for the whole search. Under load, every request on that worker queues behind searches, sockets stall, and latency on unrelated endpoints rises. The fix is an async client or `asyncio.to_thread`. In our case a teammate replaced Exa about a week later, so it never got real load, but it was my mistake. ¶ I haven't built a research eval yet, so this is how I'd do it. Golden briefs with known answers. A citation check, where every claim in `Findings` must map to a fetched source that actually contains it; that part can be automated. Coverage, which the typed schema makes measurable as required fields filled. Freshness, from source dates against the brief. And cost per brief: tokens plus Serper and Parallel calls, with rounds and wall time read from the iterations we already store on each run.
  - `L1` Why does an assistant need web search at all if the model already knows a lot?
  - `L1` Name the web tools AiRA used over time and what each was for. ↳ Exa search, Perplexity multi-search, Serper verticals, Parallel.ai page extraction
  - `L2` How did the intent gate decide when to search the web? ↳ `webSearch` flag from intent call; default unless about user's own data
  - `L2` Why search with the rewritten statement, not the raw message?
  - `L2` What does "fresh web results win" mean in the answer prompt? ↳ trust web over model memory; anchor "today" to given UTC time
  - `L2` What does Parallel.ai `url_extract` return, and why cap it? ↳ objective-focused excerpts up to 10k chars each, 40k total
  - `L2` What Serper verticals did you add, and how do tool specs teach batching? ↳ places, news, reviews, shopping; one maps search then parallel reviews
  - `L2` Walk me through the research agent: brief, tool loop, typed `Findings`. ↳ `create_research(brief)`; Luna loop over search, places, page reads
  - `L2` Why work in "rounds" where every call in a round runs at once? ↳ round two needs round one's ids and URLs; few calls
  - `L2` How does the research agent talk to the main model while running? ↳ numbered delta updates; can ask one question; answers come as steering
  - `L3` Why must venue details come from tools, never memory? What failure were you fixing?
  - `L3` The Exa client was synchronous inside async code. What would you see under load?
  - `L3` How would you evaluate research quality: citations, coverage, freshness, cost per brief?

## 1.12 AiRA — channels, connectors and other features
- [ ] WhatsApp channel: provider moves, fast-200 webhooks with HMAC, stale-message drop, dev/prod split, WAHA LIDs, AiRA Relay MCP
  - **Answer:** AiRA lived inside WhatsApp because in late 2025 there was no AiRA app to install. Our mobile app repo only starts in late January 2026 and the only front end was a web app, so the assistant was something you messaged, and WhatsApp is where people already were. That's my read of the product call, not something written down. Voice arrived as voice notes we transcribed, and from December it also sent reminders and proactive messages. ¶ One typed message in that era: Meta posts to `/webhooks/v1/whatsapp`, the handler queues the work in FastAPI `BackgroundTasks` and answers 200 straight away. The job parses the message, drops anything older than 5 minutes, downloads media, finds or creates the user from an HMAC phone token, and uses their one `whatsapp_session`, shared with the app chat. Then TEMPEX, a Redis hash per user and chat changed only under a `SET NX PX` lock, merges a burst of short messages; if a newer one lands mid-reply, the stream is cancelled and the sent part carried into the next prompt. Then the normal streaming pipeline, each chunk sent through the Graph API. ¶ The provider moves. I built most of each step; the commits record what changed, not why, so the reasons are my recollection. Twilio, 21 October 2025, was the quickest way onto a number. Infobip, 14 November, was a second provider behind the same bridge. On 28 November I went direct to Meta's Cloud API and deleted the other two: no middleman, typed payloads. On 3 December the channel became Telegram, and on 20 December I moved Telegram into `deprecated/` and made Cloud API the channel again, with reply and CTA buttons; as I remember it, our users simply lived on WhatsApp. Periskope, 24 December, was for groups, since a bot number only sees messages sent to it, never the user's own groups; it ran beside the Cloud API DMs. On 22 January 2026 we started on WAHA, and I removed the Periskope webhook the next day. ¶ A webhook must answer 200 fast because providers retry on timeouts and errors, and a retry means a double reply. Retries still come after outages, so I drop messages older than 5 minutes on WhatsApp and 300 seconds on Telegram: a late answer to a stale question is worse than none. **The weak spot is that `BackgroundTasks` is in-process**, so a crash after the 200 loses the message; a durable queue plus a dedup key on the message id is what I'd add. ¶ Signatures: my Periskope webhook checks HMAC-SHA256 over the raw body against `x-periskope-signature` with `hmac.compare_digest` and returns 401; my WAHA webhook does HMAC-SHA512 on `x-webhook-hmac`, and my Razorpay one HMAC-SHA256 with a 400. The Meta Cloud webhook's `X-Hub-Signature-256` check was not mine: my teammate Shivam added it on 16 January 2026. Before that it only had the `hub.challenge` handshake, which proves you own the URL, not that a message is genuine. You sign raw bytes because re-serialised JSON differs. `compare_digest` takes constant time; a plain `==` stops at the first differing byte, so timing leaks how much of a forged signature is right. ¶ WAHA is a self-hosted WhatsApp HTTP API: the user links their own account as a linked device by typing a pairing code. Sandy and Shivam wrote the first WAHA wrappers and connect endpoint; I built the signed webhook, session lifecycle, chat import, LID resolution and typing. A LID is WhatsApp's privacy id, `...@lid`, which hides the phone number, so one person can appear as a LID or a phone id. On import I fetch chats and the LID map in parallel and map them; for mentions, resolution goes cheapest first: local cache, the moderated-chat map, a global LID cache, Mongo `w_lid`, and only then the WAHA API. Every outgoing text marks the chat seen, shows typing and waits 50 ms per character, clamped to 0.6 to 6 seconds. The intent: replies don't land as an instant bot-like wall, and a long one never stalls. ¶ Dev and prod received the same Meta webhook, so a test message got two replies. I added one `whatsapp_testing_phone_number` setting checked two ways: a local server only handles messages sent from that number, and production ignores messages addressed to it. I also wrote a small distributor, Nginx in front of Express, that answers Meta's verification handshake at the edge and fans each `/webhooks/*` POST out to local backends with `Promise.allSettled`. Its limits: no forward timeout, and it re-serialises the body, which can break raw-body signatures. The onboarding bot number is in our dashboard: I pointed its `wa.me` connect link at a new bot-number setting instead of the general AiRA number, reasoning that the connect message must reach the bot running the connect flow. ¶ AiRA Relay is my own repo, 19 commits between 28 February and 9 March 2026; every Python line in it is mine, though it vendors the open-source WAHA Plus server. It gives any agent a WhatsApp account: a FastMCP server with about 20 tools (pair a phone, send media, summarise chats, find a contact, scan unreplied messages) plus a webhook receiver that pushes WAHA events back to the agent. MCP because any client, Claude Desktop or the OpenClaw gateway, gets WhatsApp with no custom integration. On 6 March I rewrote a 2,500-line module of module-level singletons as hexagonal: `typing.Protocol` ports, core services, adapters, an event bus and one composition root. It paid off two days later: replacing OpenClaw's completion endpoint with OpenRouter was a new adapter behind `ILLMAdapter` plus wiring; the port gained one optional argument, and interactive summaries kept MCP sampling. (The earlier move from MCP sampling to OpenClaw predates the rewrite.) Why OpenRouter, as I remember it: webhook-triggered work has no live MCP session to borrow a model from. ¶ The risks are real. WhatsApp's terms forbid unofficial clients and automation, so it's the user's own number that can be banned, not ours; human-like typing reduces that, it doesn't remove it. We also process messages from people who never agreed to AiRA, and a linked session is effectively the account, sitting on our server. Now I'd insist on a consent screen that names the ban risk, opt-in chats only, short retention and an encrypted session store.
  - `L1` Why did a voice assistant live inside WhatsApp at one point?
  - `L2` Walk me through one typed chat message in the 2025 WhatsApp era, from webhook to reply.
  - `L2` Twilio → Infobip → Meta Cloud API → Telegram → back to Cloud API → Periskope → WAHA. Why each move?
  - `L2` Why must a WhatsApp/Meta webhook always answer 200 fast? ↳ provider retries; process in background tasks
  - `L2` Messages older than 5 minutes are dropped. Why? ↳ redelivery after failure; never answer stale messages late
  - `L2` How did you verify webhook signatures, and why constant-time comparison? ↳ HMAC over raw body; `hmac.compare_digest`
  - `L2` What is WAHA and what is a LID? How did you resolve LIDs to phone numbers? ↳ privacy ids; cached chain: local, moderated map, Mongo, WAHA API
  - `L2` Why the typing delay of 50 ms per character, clamped to 0.6–6 s? ↳ replies look human, avoid bot-like bursts
  - `L2` Developers and production received the same webhook. How did you stop double replies? ↳ testing-number rule: local handles only it, prod ignores it
  - `L2` What did the local webhook distributor do? ↳ Express + Nginx; answers Meta handshake, fans events to local backends
  - `L2` What is AiRA Relay? Why build it as an MCP server? ↳ ~20 WhatsApp tools for any agent over FastMCP
  - `L2` Why give the onboarding bot its own WhatsApp number setting? ↳ connect message must reach the bot running the connect flow
  - `L3` Why did you rewrite the relay into a hexagonal architecture, and what did it buy you? ↳ swapped LLM backend MCP sampling → OpenClaw → OpenRouter without core changes
  - `L3` Linking a user's personal WhatsApp through an unofficial gateway: what are the ToS, ban and privacy risks?
- [ ] WhatsApp group monitoring: Periskope rules, deterministic ids, per-group working memory, reply loops, unreplied-message scan
  - **Answer:** The rulebook let a user write rules like "tell me if anyone in my society group mentions water supply", and AiRA watched their WhatsApp groups and classified each message against the owner's rules. It was shared work. Shashwat built the rule and group models and the web app endpoints, Shivam built the classification pipeline, and I built the ingestion side: the Periskope webhook and its security, group and member sync, deterministic ids, group working memory and mentions. Later I added multi-chat rules, and on WAHA, saved suggestions and the unreplied scan. ¶ The Periskope webhook verifies HMAC-SHA256 over the raw body against `x-periskope-signature` with `hmac.compare_digest` and returns 401 if it's missing or wrong. Only then does it parse the typed payload and hand the work to `BackgroundTasks`, so the provider gets its answer immediately and never retries because we were slow.
