One thing I'm not proud of: that first commit also hard-coded the Periskope secret and API key in settings. My teammates stripped the hard-coded values at the end of December, and today secrets load from Azure Key Vault; anything that ever hit git should also be rotated, not just deleted. ¶ Group and member ids are deterministic ObjectIds: the first 12 bytes of a SHA-1 of the chat id or the phone number. Users created from chat get their `_id` from the same hash. So a person seen as a group member and the same person messaging AiRA one to one resolve to one user without a lookup table, and re-syncing a group is an idempotent upsert. The trade-off is that a hash of a phone number is an id, not privacy; the phone-number space is small enough to brute-force. ¶ Per-group working memory was a Redis list per user per group. A pipelined `LPUSH` plus `LTRIM` keeps the last N messages along with the previous decisions, the matched rule ids and urgency. It's fetched together with the user's rules in one `asyncio.gather` and goes into the classifier prompt. The point is that messages need context ("it's back now" means nothing alone), and seeing its earlier decisions tells the classifier it already alerted on this thread. If the user is @-mentioned, the message is prefixed "user has mentioned you saying". ¶ The reply loop: when AiRA posted in a group on a user's behalf, the webhook saw that post as a new group message and AiRA reacted to itself. In January I added a group-number setting and a duplicate check that skips a message when the sender is AiRA's number and the first mention is that user. On WAHA this became an ignored-numbers list checked in the webhook, plus dropping messages from the user's own account. For one rule spanning several chats, I merged groups and DMs into one `WhatsappChats` model and made a rule's chat ids and automations lists. On save the code diffs attached against detached chats and, in one `asyncio.gather`, creates an IRIS automation for each new chat, updates existing ones, and deletes the automation and its global task for removed chats. That keeps exactly one per chat, but it isn't transactional, so a partial failure can leave an orphan; my teammate Sandy's relationship model later took over that link. ¶ Suggestions: the groups service, Shivam's and Sandy's code, already had LLM prompts that read the chats a user chose to watch and proposed rules, but the results only came back in one response. I made them durable. They're written to a `suggestions` collection, `GET /v1/suggestions` returns the user's pending ones with chat names from one `$in` query, creating a rule with a `suggestion_id` marks it accepted, and accept and delete are both owner-scoped. Suggestions are generated only for chats where the user has sent at least one message, `fromMe`. The commit just says to skip those chats, so the why is my reasoning: chats you only read, like big announcement groups, produce rules nobody wants and burn LLM calls. ¶ The unreplied scan: I wrote it on 20 February and Ananda ported it into the restructured tree the same day. It reads `last_checkin_at`, makes sure the user's own LID is known, takes the 100 most recent moderated chats, and fetches DMs since the check-in in parallel. A DM counts only if the last message isn't the user's. A group counts only if someone @-mentioned the user, and we keep messages from that mention on. Mentions can be LIDs, so each goes through the chain: local cache, moderated-chat map, global LID cache, Mongo, then the WAHA API. The output is readable transcripts with real names. It's bounded with `asyncio.Semaphore(10)` because every chat is a call to our self-hosted WAHA serving one linked phone session, and a burst of 100 requests at once is the risk. 10 is also where I'd landed for suggestion generation after trying 15 and 30, but honestly nobody load-tested WAHA, so it's a sensible cap, not a measured one. ¶ The privacy problem is real. The other group members never agreed to anything, yet their messages went to an LLM provider, and we stored summaries and chat memory. What I'd do: process only chats the owner opted in, keep the alert rather than the transcript, short retention, no reuse beyond the owner's rules, and be explicit that AiRA only shows the owner what they could already read themselves.
  - `L1` What was the "rulebook" feature for WhatsApp groups? ↳ "tell me if anyone in society group mentions water supply"
  - `L2` How did the Periskope webhook stay secure and fast? ↳ HMAC-SHA256 signature, constant-time compare, 401; work in background tasks
  - `L2` Why deterministic ObjectIds from SHA-1 for groups and members? ↳ group member and same person's 1:1 account resolve to one user
  - `L2` What is per-group working memory, and why keep previous decisions in it? ↳ Redis `LPUSH`+`LTRIM` last N messages with matched rules and urgency
  - `L2` AiRA reacted to its own relayed group messages. How did you stop the loop? ↳ skip messages from AiRA's number; later ignored-numbers list
  - `L2` One rule now spans several chats. How did you keep exactly one scheduled automation per chat in sync?
  - `L2` How were rule suggestions saved and accepted? Why only for chats where the user has sent a message? ↳ `suggestions` collection; owner-scoped accept/delete; avoid read-only chats
  - `L2` Walk me through the unreplied-messages scan. ↳ since last check-in; DMs where last message isn't user's; groups only after @-mention
  - `L2` Mentions may use LIDs instead of phone numbers. How did you resolve them? ↳ chain: local cache, moderated map, global cache, Mongo, WAHA API
  - `L2` Why bound the scan with `asyncio.Semaphore(10)`?
  - `L3` Reading users' group chats to monitor rules: what privacy problems does this raise for the other group members?
- [ ] LinkedIn via Unipile: hosted auth link, profile URL → knowledge file, state misuse, ToS and account-ban risks
  - **Answer:** Unipile is a third-party API that connects a user's LinkedIn account through a hosted login and then lets you read profiles and messages through its endpoints. Why not LinkedIn's own API: I built that direct OAuth first, code exchange at `/oauth/v2/accessToken`, the token stored AES-encrypted on the user, then `/v2/userinfo`, and with OpenID sign-in that only gives you the signed-in member's basic profile. As I remember the decision, the Darwin recruiter persona needed other people's profiles, which the open API doesn't give you, so we went through Unipile. ¶ Hosted auth: we request a hosted link that expires in 15 minutes and carries our internal user id. The user logs in on Unipile's page, the callback tells us the account, and we fetch it and parse it into typed models (account, premium features, organisations, proxy), then upsert into `unipile_accounts`. A reusable httpx wrapper came out of that work. For enrichment, a regex URL extractor with platform classification spots a LinkedIn link in a chat message, Unipile resolves the profile, the full payload goes into `unipile_profiles`, and an LLM "recruiter analyst" writes a structured summary that's stored as a knowledge file. Darwin's orchestrator can ask for it by id, and a profile arriving moves the funnel forward. ¶ Using the user id as the OAuth `state` was a mistake, and I call it out myself. `state` must be unguessable, single use and bound to the user who started the flow. A user id is predictable, so an attacker can authorise their own LinkedIn and deliver a callback carrying the victim's id. That links the attacker's LinkedIn to the victim's AiRA account, classic OAuth CSRF, and since nothing is single use, a callback can be replayed. By October our connectors did it properly: the Google flow used an HMAC-signed state carrying a one-time nonce kept in Redis, and the newer Swiggy and Stripe Link connectors added PKCE on top. ¶ Those commits also carried dev scaffolding: a hard-coded tunnel URL for callbacks, a fixed profile id and `print` debugging. In one merge I did keep our variable over the other side's hard-coded token. How I keep this out of main now: URLs and ids come from typed settings validated at boot, never literals. Lint has to fail the build, and today our Ruff config selects `ALL`, which bans `print`, while CI runs `ruff check`, a format check and `ty check` on every PR. Scaffolding stays on a local branch or behind a local-only setting, and anything secret that ever hit git gets rotated, not just deleted. ¶ The ToS risk falls on the user. LinkedIn's terms prohibit scraping and third-party automation, and a service like Unipile drives the user's real session, so LinkedIn can restrict or ban a professional account the user cares about far more than they care about us. There's also a third-party problem: the profile owner never agreed to be summarised. I'd keep it read-only and low volume, show a clear consent with that risk, store a summary rather than the raw payload, and prefer official APIs wherever they cover the need.
  - `L1` What is Unipile and why use it instead of LinkedIn's own API?
  - `L2` Walk me through Unipile hosted auth: link, callback, stored account. ↳ 15-minute hosted link carrying user id; fetch account; upsert typed models
  - `L2` How did AiRA notice a LinkedIn URL in a chat message and turn it into knowledge? ↳ regex URL extractor → Unipile profile → LLM summary stored as knowledge file
  - `L3` The direct LinkedIn OAuth used the user id as `state`. What attack does that allow?
  - `L3` Some LinkedIn commits had a hard-coded tunnel URL, a fixed profile id and print debugging. How do you keep dev scaffolding out of main?
  - `L3` Scraping or automating LinkedIn through a third party: what are the ToS and account-ban risks for users?
- [ ] Multimodal chat input: image, document and audio content parts, early STT, resume parsing, the image-generation agent
  - **Answer:** A chat message could carry text, images, documents and several audio clips at once. In November 2025 I moved the chat endpoint to multipart form data with `q` plus lists of images, files and audio. In an OpenAI-style API each item becomes a content part next to the text: `image_url` with a base64 data URL, `file` with the filename and data, and `input_audio` with base64 data and a format. I read all the attachments concurrently with `asyncio.gather` and sent them to OpenRouter. ¶ The "closed file" bug: FastAPI's `UploadFile` objects belong to the request. Once the handler returns a `StreamingResponse`, request handling is over and the uploads' temp files get closed, but the generator body only runs after that. So any read inside the generator hit a closed file. The fix was to read the bytes before returning the `StreamingResponse` and pass the bytes in. It's a lifetime bug: work that outlives the request must not hold request-owned resources. ¶ Speech-to-text before Deepgram and Cartesia was Gemini 2.5 Flash through OpenRouter. It started as a task at the very beginning of the request, so it overlapped the other setup, and the transcript was added to the query before selection, generation and storage. At first it returned strict JSON, `{text}`. In December, while adding Telegram voice notes, I rewrote it as a plain-text multimodal completion, and the same helper also handled files. The commit doesn't say why; my reasoning was that a transcript is one string, so a JSON wrapper only adds ways to fail, like an escaping problem or a truncated object that breaks the whole parse, plus extra tokens. ¶ Voice-note bug: the transcript drove the reply but wasn't written into the stored chat, so history and memory saw an empty user message. In January 2026 I wrote the transcript back into `body.query`, so everything after transcription sees the spoken text. The lesson is one source of truth for the turn's text. For Telegram media types I took the response header first, then the file extension, then magic bytes for JPEG, PNG and GIF, because downloads often come back as a generic binary type. Voice was downloaded in parallel with other media, and a media error degraded to "no media" instead of failing the turn. ¶ Resume parsing ran in a background task: attached files went to a strict-JSON parser for contact details, experience, skills, education, projects and certificates, and the result was saved on the user's profile. Background because the user is waiting for a reply, not for a profile update, and a parser failure must never break the turn. ¶ The image agent, December 2025: the orchestrator calls `generate_image_and_send_to_user` with a detailed prompt. The service calls OpenRouter chat completions with `modalities=["image"]` on a configurable model, decodes the base64 data URLs that come back, uploads the bytes to WhatsApp's media endpoint to get a media id, and sends the image by that id; for its first days it was a Telegram multipart upload. The tool also tells the assistant not to announce the image twice. The first version used a blocking `requests.post`. Inside an `async def` that blocks the event loop thread, so every other request on that worker freezes for the seconds an image takes. I swapped it for our async HTTP client a day later. ¶ The tool description banned real people and NSFW content and gave a prompt formula. **Prompt rules are not enough.** They're advice to a model that can be talked around. You still need the provider's own filters, a moderation check on the prompt, and logging so abuse is visible. ¶ A 40 MB PDF plus three voice notes at once: the slow parts are the upload over a phone network, three transcriptions (run them in parallel), and a model reading a huge document before its first token. The expensive part is the PDF's tokens, paid on every turn it's re-sent. I don't recall a size cap in that first multipart version, and the commits show none. Later designs fixed the shape: bytes go phone to Azure Blob through a short-lived SAS URL so the API never carries them, enrichment has a size cap with a 413 (my 5 MB before Claude in April 2026, 50 MB on today's Gemini path), and the model sees one-line stubs and opens a file only when it needs it. I'd also cap audio duration and page count, and extract and chunk big PDFs instead of sending them whole. ¶ On resumes I should have asked: did the user explicitly agree to parsing, and for what purpose? How long do we keep it, and can they see and delete it? Is it encrypted at rest? Does the LLM provider retain it? It's sensitive personal data, so purpose limits and deletion on request should come first.
  - `L1` What kinds of input could a chat message carry? ↳ text, images, documents, multiple audio clips
  - `L1` How do you send an image or audio file to an LLM through an OpenAI-style API? ↳ `image_url`, `file`, `input_audio` content parts
  - `L2` Uploads gave "closed file" errors inside the streaming response. Why, and what was the fix? ↳ read bytes before returning `StreamingResponse`; generator ran after request closed
  - `L2` How did speech-to-text work before Cartesia and Deepgram? ↳ Gemini 2.5 Flash via OpenRouter, started as a task at request start
  - `L2` Why did STT change from a structured-JSON parse to a plain-text completion?
  - `L2` A voice note's transcript was not stored with the chat. What broke, and how did you fix it? ↳ transcript written back into `body.query`
  - `L2` How did Telegram media get the right MIME type? ↳ response header, then file extension, then magic bytes
  - `L2` How did resume parsing work, and why in a background task? ↳ strict-JSON parser for contact, experience, skills; saved on profile
  - `L2` Walk me through the image-generation agent: prompt, model, delivery to WhatsApp. ↳ OpenRouter `modalities=["image"]`; decode data URLs; upload media; send by id
  - `L2` The image tool used a blocking `requests.post`. Why does that matter in FastAPI? ↳ blocks the event loop; swapped for async HTTP client
  - `L3` What content rules did the image tool carry, and are prompt rules enough? ↳ bans real people and NSFW in description; provider filters still needed
  - `L3` A user sends a 40 MB PDF and three voice notes at once. What is slow, what is expensive, and what would you cap?
  - `L3` Parsing personal resumes into a profile: what consent and retention questions should you have asked?
- [ ] Attachments opened on demand (stubs, open_file, semantic search, SAS downloads) and FCM reply push notifications
  - **Answer:** A forced tool call is setting `tool_choice` to one named tool, so the model must answer by calling it, and its output is the tool's arguments, shaped by the schema. That's reliable structured output. I built attachments in April 2026: the bytes land in Azure Blob, then `POST /v1/attachments/{fId}` loaded them and sent them to Claude Haiku 4.5 as a document or image block with `tool_choice` forced to `record_attachment_metadata`, returning a title of up to 80 characters and a one or two sentence description, with 415 for unsupported types and 502 if the tool call was missing. Five days later my teammate Shashwat moved enrichment to a Gemini Flash Lite call with schema-validated structured output, which is what runs today. ¶ Enrichment is idempotent. If a record exists we return it without a new LLM call. Under a race, two requests both miss and both call the model, but a unique key means the loser gets a duplicate-key error and returns the winner's record. Worst case is one wasted LLM call, never two records. ¶ The model sees one-line stubs instead of the files (`[ATTACHMENT_FILE]` in my version, `[FILE]` blocks today), because re-sending a PDF on every turn costs thousands of tokens each time. A turn's attachments are linked to that turn's memory cluster, so retrieval surfaces the stub when it's relevant. If the model needs the content, it calls `open_file("attachment:<id>")`, and we download the file and add it to the next user message as a real Anthropic document or image block. Before sending to Claude I capped files at 5 MB; my code comment gave the reason as Anthropic's per-block limits and the latency of large payloads. Today's Gemini path allows up to 50 MB. Either way the model knows every file exists but pays for one only when it reads it. ¶ Semantic search: after enrichment, "title plus description" is embedded and upserted into Qdrant with a deterministic uuid5 id, so re-indexing is idempotent, and an indexing failure never fails the upload. The always-loaded `search_attachments` tool embeds all queries in one call, runs the vector searches in parallel, keeps the top 5 at score 0.45 or more filtered by user, de-duplicates, and fetches the records with one `$in` query. That's how "the PDF I sent last week" works. ¶ Download links: the route checks the record belongs to this user, then signs. The URL is a read-only SAS from a user delegation key, valid one hour by default, and the adapter caps any link at seven days. The blob key embeds the user id, so another user's id produces a key no URL is issued for, and the batch endpoint filters requested ids down to the ones the user owns before signing. If a link leaks, it dies in an hour. ¶ FCM is Firebase Cloud Messaging. A push token identifies one app install on one device, not a user, so a user can have several and tokens rotate. Reply push, March 2026: after a non-system turn, `send_fcm_if_registered` looked up the user's Android token and called my teammate Ananda's Firebase wrapper, with title "AiRA" and the reply minus expression tags, cut to 200 characters. It's best-effort: errors are logged and never break the turn, because the reply already reached the app live, and a Firebase outage must not fail a chat. The gap was Android only; today push goes through a device entity Ananda and Shashwat built that stores both APNs and FCM tokens. ¶ On the revert: on 13 March 2026 I pushed two client commits, titled just "revert", that removed Ananda's Firebase push integration and his dev CI workflow, merged as one PR. The message records no reason, so what I can give you is what the diff shows plus my recollection: it was a clean removal across Android, iOS and JS, plus restoring the PCM player bridge, rather than a partial disable, which fits it breaking our builds. The same day I wired his backend Firebase wrapper into the reply flow, so it wasn't a rejection of push. I won't dress up the conversation; the right way is to tell him first with the evidence, say a revert isn't a judgement, and help re-land it in smaller pieces. ¶ If the description is wrong, search silently fails. To detect it, I'd log searches that return nothing and then see the user browse to the file anyway, which is a labelled miss, and keep a small eval set of queries against known files. To fix: embed the filename and some extracted text too, add keyword matching on the title, let the user edit the title, and re-enrich with a better model. Because the ids are deterministic, re-indexing is safe.
  - `L1` What is a forced tool call (`tool_choice`) and why use it to get structured output?
  - `L1` What is FCM and what does a push token identify?
  - `L2` What happens when a user uploads a PDF? ↳ Claude Haiku with forced `record_attachment_metadata` writes title and description
  - `L2` How is the enrichment call idempotent, even under a race? ↳ existing record returned; duplicate-key race returns winner's record
  - `L2` Why does the model see one-line stubs instead of the real files? ↳ files not re-sent every turn; saves tokens
  - `L2` How does `open_file("attachment:<id>")` get the real PDF into the model's context? ↳ added to next user message as document/image block
  - `L2` Why the 5 MB cap before sending to Claude? ↳ per-block limits and latency of large payloads
  - `L2` How does semantic attachment search work? ↳ title+description embedded, uuid5 ids, parallel searches, score ≥0.45, one `$in` fetch
  - `L2` How are download links protected? ↳ ownership checked in DB and blob key; 1-hour SAS URL
  - `L2` How did reply push notifications work, and why best-effort? ↳ after non-system turn, Android token lookup; body trimmed to 200 chars
  - `L3` You reverted a teammate's Firebase push integration on the client. Why, and how did you handle that conversation?
  - `L3` The model-written description is wrong, so search never finds the file. How would you detect and fix that?
- [ ] News ingestion and the global fact store: RSS/Atom feeds, triage editor, tombstones, user-contributed facts, poisoning risk
  - **Answer:** This was our social news platform, Posts, Asks and Takes, in July and August 2026 before the product moved to Omni. The surrounding post code was shared with Shashwat and Ananda, and the pieces below are mine. RSS and Atom are both XML feeds a publisher exposes: title, link, date, a summary. Atom is the stricter spec with required ids and update times; RSS is looser. Both are free and come straight from the publisher, but you get what they put in, usually no full text, and no search or cross-source dedup. A paid news API gives search, ranking and normalised sources, and you pay per call. I wrote one stdlib `RssAdapter` for both formats, later adding Google Trends with a traffic filter. ¶ JSON-LD is schema.org data publishers embed in a `<script type="application/ld+json">` tag for search engines. A news article there carries the body text, headline, dates and image. Reading it gives clean article text without fighting each site's HTML. The fallback was Parallel.ai extraction, and the hero image came from JSON-LD or `og:image`. ¶ I replaced SerpApi, which I'd added myself, with RSS on 5 August. The commit doesn't state the reason; mine was cost: SerpApi is paid per search and we swept the home feed plus every section every 6 hours, while RSS costs nothing. About 20 curated publisher and Google News feeds are fetched in one gather. Then a DeepSeek V4 Flash "triage editor" dedupes the same story arriving from many feeds, drops junk and picks at most 100, which caps what everything downstream costs. A link that keeps failing gets a `NewsDrops` tombstone after 3 failures, so a paywalled or broken page doesn't burn an extraction every run forever. The composer can answer `publishable=false`: if you force a model to always produce a post from thin or contradictory text, it pads or invents. A structured "no" is cheaper than a hallucinated story, and the pipeline deletes any partial work. ¶ User facts: only public chats contribute, and I have to be straight that chats were public by default, with the user able to mark one private, so it was an opt-out, which is weaker than it should have been. A fact the user states is queued, and an hourly Redis-locked cron routes it. It looks for candidate posts from the last two days by vector search at a 0.8 floor, then a router picks one of three actions, or drops the claim as not routable: attach it as a Take on a recent post, have a web-verifying composer agent write a new story, or absorb it into the knowledge store. Executors run four at a time. Honest gap: my own docstring says a crash mid-batch can restage a Take, because the executors aren't guarded against re-runs. ¶ The store has two tiers: raw `Knowledge` points and consolidated `Facts`. The hourly synthesis refines the raw claims with nearby existing facts as context. For each new fact it finds stored facts at 0.88 similarity or more, and a cheap model decides whether the new one supersedes them, meaning a later value, a correction or a restatement. If that check fails or it can't tell, both facts are kept. Recall uses a 0.65 floor, over-fetches three times and reranks with an exponential decay of 0.0006 per hour; anything at 0.90 or above is exempt, so decay only breaks near-ties. A content patch from the opinion agent applies only if its citations came from pages the agent actually opened: its tools record every URL they surfaced, so the model can't cite a page it never read or make one up. That's a grounding check in code, not trust in a prompt. ¶ Poisoning is the real problem: one user's false claim becomes everyone's answer, injected into chat turns without anyone asking. **What we had was not enough.** There was the public-chat gate, web verification and the right to decline on the composer path, and a supersession rule that keeps both facts when unsure. I'd add corroboration from independent users or a source before anything reaches the shared tier, per-user rate limits and trust, quarantine for new facts, provenance on every fact so one user's input can be purged, and showing it as "users report", not as truth. ¶ On 18 August I deleted the whole platform, 43 files and about 4,900 lines, with no data migration. I think it was right. The commit doesn't spell out the reasoning, so this is how I remember deciding: the product had moved to Omni, nothing reachable used it, and dead code costs every reader and every refactor. Our own rule is that removing a feature removes its whole surface. Git keeps the code and the collections were simply no longer read, so it was reversible. What I'd do differently is have a plan for the data left behind, because those collections still hold user contributions, and orphaned personal data needs a retention decision too.
  - `L1` RSS vs Atom vs a paid news API: what do you get from each?
  - `L1` What is JSON-LD and why read article text from it?
  - `L2` Why replace SerpApi with free RSS/Atom and Google Trends feeds? ↳ cost; about 20 curated feeds fetched in one gather
  - `L2` What does the "triage editor" LLM do? ↳ dedupes stories, drops junk, at most 100 picks
  - `L2` How do you stop retrying a link that keeps failing? ↳ `NewsDrops` tombstone after 3 failures
  - `L2` The composer can decline with `publishable=false`. Why give an LLM the right to say no?
  - `L2` A user states a fact in a public chat. Walk me through what happens to it. ↳ queued; hourly locked cron attaches, creates story, or absorbs
  - `L2` What are the two tiers of the knowledge store, and what does the hourly synthesis do? ↳ raw `Knowledge` and consolidated `Facts`; supersession check; recency decay on recall
  - `L2` Why only accept a content patch whose citations came from pages the agent actually opened?
  - `L3` User-contributed facts become shared knowledge for everyone. How do you stop poisoning and misinformation?
  - `L3` You deleted the whole platform (~4.9k lines) with no data migration. Was that right, and how did you decide?
- [ ] Smaller systems: Razorpay subscriptions, coin purchases, quests, Darwin persona, crawler, vote tallies, multi-session chat, speaker ID
  - **Answer:** Five things I built at AiRA that aren't on my resume: the WhatsApp channel and the MCP relay, Razorpay subscriptions, the gamification layer of coins, bond levels and quests, the Darwin recruiter persona, and the Ask and Takes opinion tallies. Also a Playwright crawler, multi-session chat, a relevance-engine prototype, and speaker identification, which I later deleted. ¶ Razorpay, December 2025: during onboarding the assistant agreed a monthly price and called `get_subscription_link` with the negotiated amount. The backend created a per-user plan and a subscription, stored it as pending, and returned the link. Amounts at or below ₹10 were refused, and so was a second plan when one was already active. The webhook checks HMAC-SHA256 on the raw body with `compare_digest` and returns 400 if it's invalid. Processing upserts the subscription on its id and the payment on invoice plus subscription, setting `created_at` only on insert, so Razorpay's retries are harmless: the same event writes the same state. Weak spot: the model sets the price and the only server-side guard was the floor; I'd bound the range on the server. ¶ Coins: a purchase is a single Mongo update filtered on `coins >= price`, so two concurrent buys can't both pass, and you get 402 if you're short. Later it returns 409 if you already own the item, and the inventory insert became an upsert on user plus item. The decrement and the insert aren't in one transaction, so a crash between them could cost coins; a transaction would close that. Quests cost no extra LLM call: the chat request carries `quest_id`, and the storage LLM that already runs after every turn gets an active-quest block and returns `quest_completed` and progress notes. A daily cron assigned quests, 20 users at a time. The energy system was my teammate Sandy's. The crawler, October 2025, used one shared headless Chromium, a semaphore of 10 pages, a depth limit I tuned from 2 down to 1, and a Redis list as the queue, with child links pushed at depth plus one. I don't quote a pages-per-minute figure because we never measured one. ¶ Darwin was a recruiter persona that walked candidates on WhatsApp through a staged funnel. The script is data: ordered objective files, knowledge files and sample dialogues. Each turn an orchestrator LLM sees the current objective, key moments and recent turns, and returns whether to transition, which objective, and which knowledge to load. After the reply, a state tracker returns only the changed fields, which are deep-merged into the candidate's record. The thinking behind data was that non-engineers could change the funnel without a deploy; behind changed-fields-only, that the output stays small and the model can't clobber fields it never meant to touch. ¶ Ask and Takes: counts must never come from the LLM, because they're arithmetic and a model will happily produce plausible wrong numbers. The model only classifies each Take into an option, and code does the tally. An `AskStats` document is updated with `$inc` deltas (old option minus one, new plus one, total plus one only on a first submission), so placement reads one document whatever the number of Takes. An hourly exact rebuild repaired drift, then it was paused and deleted, so at the end there was no repair if a write was lost. Stale submissions are blocked with a `find_one_and_update` guarded on the exact staged text. Multi-session chat, May 2026: short-term memory moved from one Redis key per user to `stm:{user}:{session}`, and history and rewind were scoped to the session. From then on every WebSocket frame, and every TTS audio chunk, carried `sId`, so the app routes each stream to the right tab. A background job titles the session, and status writes were folded into existing gathers so they cost no extra wait. ¶ The relevance engine was a prototype: one commit of about 3,500 lines on a branch, never merged. Events come in batches of up to 50, are deduplicated by idempotency key with `$setOnInsert` upserts, and are folded into one per-user-per-post state document, with no LLM on that path. An LLM scores each post on 12 factors, stored in Qdrant as two named vectors, 384-d semantic and 12-d factors. A compactor builds interest clusters from weighted events (like 1.0, save 2.0, share 2.3, not interested minus 2.0). The mixer pulls candidates in parallel, filters, ranks with fixed weights, then applies diversity rules: never the same author twice in a row, at most two of one type or cluster. The weights were hand-set with no evaluation. ¶ Speaker identification, May 2026: in passive mode we sliced buffered audio by Deepgram's diarized word timestamps, made a Resemblyzer voice embedding on a thread, and matched it in Qdrant at cosine 0.80, so transcripts said who spoke. I built the core; Shashwat added the review endpoints. I deleted it on 3 July, in a commit I titled as a readability refactor, which I'd label honestly today. The commit gives no reason, so this is my recollection: it dropped about 47 packages including torch and CUDA, the per-session audio buffers were costing Redis memory the same week we fixed a Redis out-of-memory problem, and we were moving to one STT profile without diarization. **The threshold moved 0.80, 0.85, 0.80 and we never measured accuracy**, so we were paying for a feature we couldn't show worked.
  - `L1` Name five AiRA features you built that are not on your resume.
  - `L2` Razorpay with "LLM-negotiated pricing": how did the webhook stay idempotent and secure? ↳ HMAC-SHA256 on raw body; upserts matched on subscription/invoice ids
  - `L2` Coin purchases: how did you prevent double spends? ↳ update filtered on `coins >= price`; 409 if owned; upsert on (user, item)
  - `L2` Daily quests cost no extra LLM call. How? ↳ reused the existing post-turn storage LLM call
  - `L2` What was the Darwin recruiter persona and why store objectives as data? ↳ orchestrator picks objective; state tracker returns only changed fields
  - `L2` How did the Playwright crawler bound its concurrency and depth? ↳ shared Chromium, semaphore of 10, depth limit, Redis queue
  - `L2` Ask/Takes: why must vote counts never come from the LLM, and how did you keep stats constant-cost? ↳ `$inc` tally document; exact rebuild later removed
  - `L2` Multi-session chat: what changed in Redis keys and in WebSocket frames? ↳ `stm:{user}:{session}`; every frame carries `sId`
  - `L3` Walk me through your relevance engine prototype: ingestion, interest clusters, mixer. ↳ idempotent events, named vectors, weighted ranking, diversity rules; branch only
  - `L3` Speaker identification with voice prints: why did you build it, and why did you delete it? ↳ Resemblyzer + Qdrant; torch/CUDA weight, Redis memory, no measured accuracy
- [ ] Client and web pieces: wake-word matching, film WebView player, streaming web chat, Next.js SSR and route guard, meeting recap blocks
  - **Answer:** I committed to four front ends. The legacy Next.js web app, July to November 2025, about 180 commits of my own plus merges. The React Native app, later Expo, from February to October 2026, about 90 authored commits, plus co-author credit on about 20 of my teammates' squash merges that carried my code or re-listed my commits. The aira-home website, 17 commits across Nuxt and then Hugo. And the small Next.js dashboard, 2 authored commits plus 5 merges of teammates' work. A Next.js middleware, renamed `proxy` in Next.js 16, runs before a route renders and can redirect or rewrite based on the request, typically cookies. ¶ My dashboard guard is about 19 lines. Public prefixes like `/setup`, `/_next` and `/assets` pass, and anything else without an `access-token` cookie redirects to `/setup`. **That is not security.** Anyone can set a cookie with that name. It's UX, so a signed-out user doesn't land on a broken, empty page. Real authorization is the backend verifying the JWT's signature and expiry on every API call. My teammate Sandy later added CSP nonces and security headers to that file. ¶ The SSR migration in the web app, on a branch that reached our dev branch, moved pages to async server components. They read the auth cookie with `next/headers`, call the backend, prefetch in parallel with `Promise.all` (the memory page loads user, counts, status and the first 100 memories at once), and pass `initial` props down so the client hooks skip their first fetch. The Redux store became one per request: `makeStore()` per React tree, hydrated with the user. A module-level store on a server is shared by every request, which can leak one user's data into another's render. ¶ Streaming chat on the web: a `fetch` POST, then the body piped through `TextDecoderStream`. I keep a buffer across chunks, cut at each `}\n` and parse NDJSON objects one by one, so a half-received object waits for its tail. `m` is a status line, `d` an answer delta, `l` the number of retrieved contexts. For reloads, the chat page opens a second GET stream on `/v1/chats/status` when it mounts and keeps the input locked until it reports completed, so a reload mid-answer can't fire a duplicate request. ¶ The wake-word classifier, inside the Live Mode pipeline my teammates Sahad and Sanket built, strips fillers and punctuation, then runs trigger regexes in priority order, stop over deactivate over activate, each capturing the next word. An accept set (aira, ira, ayra, kira) and a reject set (are, our, array, error, aria) decide instantly, so "hey Ira" passes and "hey array" doesn't. Anything else must pass a Metaphone gate, sounding like AR, IR or ER, and then Jaro–Winkler similarity to "aira" of at least 0.85. It scans a rolling 8-second window of final utterances plus the current partial, because the iOS recogniser can split "hey" and "aira" across two recognition tasks, and it debounces each verdict for 1.5 seconds so a partial and its final don't trigger twice. False accept and reject rates were never measured, and that file went away in the Expo migration. I also deleted our custom Swift VAD, 279 lines; my reasoning was that the recogniser's own endpointing already detects the pause, and two detectors disagreeing is worse than one. That left one tested TypeScript pipeline. ¶ The doubled Deepgram audio: an activity screen and its voice sheet each mounted a second mic PCM forwarder while Live Mode's forwarder stayed mounted underneath, so every frame went up twice and the transcript came out corrupted. I don't have notes on the exact debugging steps; the symptom, a garbled, duplicated transcript only on those screens, points at the input side, and the cause was the extra forwarders. I removed them, and Sahad then made a single mic-capture owner. The lesson is that a hardware resource needs exactly one owner. ¶ The film player, in its first version, is a self-contained HTML page in a WebView with a strict CSP: `default-src 'none'`, images only as data URLs, inline script plus `'unsafe-eval'` because the engine's schema validators compile with `new Function`. Navigation is locked, and the spec is embedded as an escaped JSON string so model text can never close the script tag. Native audio is the clock: film time follows the audio position, and `requestAnimationFrame` interpolates between progress events with a clock that never goes backwards. My reasoning: WebView timers drift and get throttled, while a viewer notices audio glitches instantly, so the picture should follow the sound. Going full screen moves the only WebView without restarting the audio. ¶ The meeting recap became data: I refactored Sanket's recap screens into a session plus chapters, each a list of `{type, args}` blocks, rendered from a 30-entry registry. The LLM picks blocks and arguments, so new recap layouts need no app release. An old app skips unknown block types and hides missing chapters instead of crashing. Speaker avatars get a stable colour from a string hash mod 6, so the same name always gets the same colour, plus the first letter as the initial. ¶ The MIND page on the old Nuxt site laid out our memory paper as web sections; I built its first version, Ananda renamed and extended it the same day, and I'm listed there as a co-author. The download fetches the PDF into a Blob, makes an object URL and clicks a temporary link with `download="MIND.pdf"`, because a plain link often just opens the PDF in a tab. On the static Hugo site, signed-in state comes from a script that calls `/v1/users/me` with `credentials: "include"` and shows or hides nav elements marked for signed-in or signed-out users, re-checking after each client-side navigation. A static page has no server session, so asking the API is the only reliable signal.
  - `L1` Which client codebases did you commit to, and roughly how much? ↳ legacy Next.js web app, React Native/Expo app, aira-home site
  - `L1` What does a Next.js middleware/`proxy` route guard do?
  - `L2` How does your wake-word classifier handle "hey Aira" misheard as "hey Ira" or "hey array"? ↳ fillers out, trigger regex, accept/reject sets, Metaphone, Jaro–Winkler ≥0.85
  - `L2` Why a rolling 8-second window and a 1.5 s debounce? ↳ "hey" and "aira" split across recognitions; no double triggers
  - `L2` Why did you delete the custom Swift VAD? ↳ recognizer's own endpointing; one tested TypeScript pipeline
  - `L2` Every audio frame went to Deepgram twice. What caused it and how did you find it? ↳ second PCM forwarder mounted under live mode; duplicate transcript
  - `L2` How does the film player work inside a WebView, and why is native audio the clock? ↳ sandboxed WebView, strict CSP; audio drives timing for sync
  - `L2` How did you build streaming chat on the web with `fetch` and `ReadableStream`? ↳ NDJSON status/answer messages; status stream stops duplicate requests on reload
  - `L2` What did the SSR migration change in the Next.js app? ↳ server components prefetch in parallel; Redux store per request, hydrated
  - `L2` Walk me through your dashboard route guard. ↳ public prefixes pass; no `access-token` cookie → redirect to `/setup`
  - `L2` The guard only checks that the cookie exists. Is that security? Where does real authorization happen? ↳ backend checks signature and expiry; guard is UX only
  - `L2` What is the composable meeting recap format, and why let the LLM pick blocks? ↳ chapters of `{type, args}` blocks, 30-entry registry; no client release
  - `L2` Unknown block types arrive from a newer backend. What does the old app do? ↳ unknown blocks skipped; missing chapters hidden
  - `L2` How did you give speakers stable avatar colours without backend data? ↳ string hash mod 6 picks colour; first letter as initial
  - `L2` What did the MIND research page contain, and why fetch the PDF into a Blob to download it? ↳ forces a download with a fixed filename, not a new tab
  - `L2` How does the signed-in state work on a static Hugo site? ↳ script calls `/v1/users/me` with cookies; toggles nav elements

## 1.13 AiRA — generative UI and canvas
- [ ] OpenUI Lang: UI statements streamed beside speech, REPAINT vs PATCH, client-resolved image slots, prompt generated from components
  - **Answer:** Generative UI means the model decides what goes on the screen, not just what it says. In a voice-first app that matters a lot: AiRA should say one or two sentences while the screen holds the list, the map or the steps. Our prompts put it as: the screen does the explaining and the voice points at it. ¶ How we got there: we started with about a dozen `render_*` tools, one per card. In August I first collapsed them into one card tool, then replaced the tools with OpenUI Lang. The model writes `name = Component(args)` lines inside its normal text, inside a ui block. A stream filter splits it: text outside the block goes to speech, and the Lang lines go to the phone, which draws each line as it arrives. The fence started as `<<ui>>` when I built it in August; in September my teammate Shashwat moved it to a Markdown code fence; and on October 16 I changed it to a `<ui>` line and a `</ui>` line, because a code block inside a component's Markdown text closed the backtick fence early and the code leaked into speech. Now `</ui>` only closes at a line edge, and a Lang statement is one line, so a string can't end the program. ¶ Why a streamed language instead of tools? Tool arguments are a JSON blob, so the screen waits for the call; Lang paints line by line, and the model names a child before defining it so the layout mounts while content streams. Speech and screen come out of one generation, and the prompt shrinks to one stable catalog. Arguments are positional only, and the protocol tells the model never to write `name=value`, because a named argument lands in the wrong slot and breaks the render. ¶ Keeping the prompt in sync with the components: first I had a Node generator in the API repo that built the protocol prompt from one component schema; it was removed in early September. Then I wrote one in the app using the TypeScript compiler API: it reads each component's JSDoc signature and Props interface and fails if they disagree, and Sahad later extended it to cross-check the parser's own component table. The honest gap is across repos: the catalog the backend loads is still a prose file people edit in the API repo, so it can drift. I'd add an API CI check against the app's table. ¶ The model never writes image URLs; it writes `Image("description")` or `GeneratedImage("description")`. Models invent URLs, real ones need signing and expire, and the description is the better input anyway. The phone draws a placeholder and asks the server to resolve the slot. The server checks the user owns the session, runs image search or generates the picture into blob storage with a signed URL, writes it back into the line, streams the filled line, and stores it so a cold start restores it. Every failure still answers, because the phone has no timeout. ¶ REPAINT versus PATCH: the screen persists across turns and each name keeps its last value. A repaint is a new `root` line, which replaces the page; a patch re-sends only the lines that changed. Patches are fewer tokens, faster, and keep the reader's place. New things always get fresh names, because an old name that's off screen may still be bound and would come back. Changes the user makes between turns, like paging through steps, sat in a Redis hash on my branch and were handed to the next turn; in November the team rewrote the backend on Cosmos without Redis, so that piece is theirs now. ¶ Versioning with old app builds still installed: we don't have a protocol version on the wire today. The levers are coarse: a health endpoint returns the current app version per platform, which is the hook for asking old builds to update, and the client keeps aliases so old stored screens still replay. What I'd build: the app sends a hash of its component table on the socket handshake, the server picks the matching catalog for the prompt, changes stay additive, so new components and optional arguments only at the end and never reordering positional ones, and an unknown component draws nothing and gets reported. The backend protocol and slot resolution are mine; the component folder is my teammate Sahad's, and on the client I removed about 7,700 lines of old card-envelope and duplicate activity code, in a screen mostly built by Sahad and Sanket, when it moved to one Lang renderer.
  - `L1` What is generative UI? Why let the model "paint" the phone screen?
  - `L2` What is OpenUI Lang and how is speech separated from UI in one text stream? ↳ `name = Component(args)` inside `<<ui>>` fences; stream filter splits
  - `L2` Why replace ~12 `render_*` tools with a streamed language? ↳ paint before the call finishes; small stable prompt
  - `L2` How did you stop the prompt drifting from the real components? ↳ prompt generated from one component schema / TS compiler API
  - `L2` Why does the model never write image URLs? ↳ writes `Image("desc")`; client asks server to resolve slot
  - `L2` REPAINT vs PATCH: why both? ↳ patch re-sends only changed statements
  - `L3` How would you version this protocol when old app builds are still installed?
- [ ] Jev gate and warm-start opener: typed pre-LLM decisions with a 1.5 s timeout, failing open, never speaking at the wrong moment
  - **Answer:** Jev is a fast typed evaluation model from TypeSafe AI. It never writes text; it only answers multiple-choice and yes/no questions with a probability for each option. We use it as a gate before the main model with a 1.5 second budget. My teammate Shashwat wrote the adapter; I built the gate itself, the questions and how the answers are used. ¶ Before each turn, one batched evaluation decides: should we fetch memories, which short acknowledgment clip AiRA plays as the user finishes, and, when the screen has them, whether to move the reader's step or beat in a guided run, which ranking chips or sort to apply, and which component to scroll to and highlight. The first version in September also chose an early web search, with an objective and up to three queries, and which skills to load; we moved both back to the main model within about a week, web search on September 24 and skills on October 1, since it has those tools anyway. Each answer has a floor, for example 0.30 for memory and 0.50 for moving the screen. The decisions are stored on the turn, sent to the phone, and given to the main model in a short section, so it doesn't redo a screen move Jev already made. While the user is still talking, Jev also picks AiRA's face and listening sound. Why a gate: these are classification questions, and a typed answer is faster than a full LLM call and can't invent a tool argument. ¶ If the gate is slow or fails, it fails open. After 1.5 seconds we give up and use a fixed default: fetch memory, since that's the safe side, and make no screen moves. The main model still has its own tools and can do all of it, so a missed gate costs one turn its head start, never the turn. Every call is recorded with its duration and every probability. The weak spot is that the floors are hand-set constants with no labelled set behind them to tune against, and I haven't built a percentile view of gate latency yet. That's what I built through October; the team's November rewrite carried the gate over with the same 1.5 second budget. ¶ The warm-start opener is mine, later extended by Shashwat: AiRA can speak first when you open the app. Ahead of time, a small fast model turns follow-up briefs into up to three waiting lines per user. When the app opens, Jev reads the moment: the clock, the last conversation, the screen, and recent app events like connects and moving to the foreground. It picks a waiting line, a greeting category or silence, with a 2 second budget, because a fresh connection's first call ran past one second. To avoid speaking at the wrong moment: silence is a real option that can win, a line that's no longer relevant loses to "not relevant", and nothing is voiced while the user is speaking; if the mic says they're talking, the open goes out silent and spends nothing. A failed Jev call here means silence. That's the opposite of the gate on purpose: for a turn, failing open is cheap; for an opener, being quiet is cheap and speaking at the wrong moment is expensive.
  - `L1` What is Jev? ↳ fast typed pre-LLM gate, 1.5 s timeout
  - `L2` What is the Jev gate and what does it decide before the main model runs? ↳ memory/web lookups, skills, UI cursor, ack sound; 1.5 s timeout
  - `L2` The gate is slow or fails. What happens to the turn? ↳ only loses the head start, never the turn
  - `L3` What is the warm-start opener and how do you avoid AiRA speaking at the wrong moment? ↳ Jev reads the moment; silence allowed; never while user speaks
- [ ] Canvas files: AI-written HTML mini-apps with a Node vm sandbox, slides and Word as JSON with deep-merge patches, skill files
  - **Answer:** A canvas file is a document AiRA writes and the app shows at once, and later finds and edits. It's one of three types: an HTML mini-app, a PowerPoint-style slide deck, or a Word-style document. I built the first version in March, my teammate Sandy extended it, and I rebuilt the tool on my own in April for the new architecture. ¶ The canvas tool has create, update, rewrite, search and theme calls, and every change re-indexes the file's description in Qdrant and pushes a WebSocket event. Rewrite is the interesting one. When AiRA changes an app's structure, the user's saved data has to be reshaped, so the model writes a JavaScript migration function. That's untrusted code on our server, so it runs in a short-lived Node subprocess, in a `vm` context with only safe globals like JSON, Date and Math and a 5 second timeout, with a rollback snapshot kept on the file. Honest weaknesses: Node's `vm` isn't a security boundary, since host objects passed in can be walked back to the real process; the subprocess inherits our environment; and the timeout only covers synchronous code. I'd move it to an isolate or a locked-down container with an empty environment and a hard wall-clock kill. ¶ Why the server rejects `localStorage`: the app saves an HTML app's state through `window.data`, which the app tracks and persists through the native bridge and our API. If the model uses `localStorage`, state lives inside the WebView where we can't see it, save it or restore it on another device. Prompt rules alone didn't stop it, so the server rejects HTML that uses `localStorage`, `sessionStorage` or the bad init patterns, with an error telling the model what to fix. The `window.data` idea was my teammate Sandy's; the server enforcement is mine. ¶ Slides were JSON objects of type text, image or shape, each with x, y, w and h on a 10 by 7.5 inch grid. Word documents were sections with headings, paragraphs, bullet and numbered lists, and tables. JSON, because a model can't reliably write OOXML and we wanted to edit pieces. Edits send a partial patch that's deep-merged, so changing one theme colour keeps everything else: fewer tokens, and no risk of the model dropping a slide while retyping the deck. The gotcha is that only objects merge and lists are replaced wholesale, so editing one slide means re-sending the slide list; id-keyed items or JSON Patch operations would fix that. ¶ `update_file` on HTML replaces the first exact occurrence of `old_str` with `new_str`. Two things go wrong. If the snippet appears twice, it silently edits the first, maybe the wrong button. Worse, if it isn't found at all, Python's replace returns the content unchanged and the tool still reports success, so the model thinks it edited. The fix is to count matches: zero is an error, more than one is an error asking for more context, exactly one applies. ¶ Finding a file from last week: every file's description is embedded in Qdrant with the user and source as filters; a search embeds the query, takes the top 10, drops duplicates and loads the files from Mongo. For "last week" specifically I'd add a date filter. ¶ Skill files: the orchestrator prompt used to carry every file type's rules. I moved them on demand: the model sees a short list, frontend, canvas-html, canvas-powerpoint and canvas-word, loads one by setting `skill_name`, and its rules are injected for that task. The canvas prompt shrank by about 400 lines, for the cost of one extra step when a task needs a skill. ¶ `python-docx` was added and removed the same day, and as far as the code shows, rendering stayed in the app, from the structured JSON. The JSON is the source of truth, the app draws it natively and a patch shows instantly; server-side rendering would mean generating files and re-rendering on every edit. A .docx export would be a separate on-demand step. ¶ And `frontend_skill.md`: honestly, it reads like a public skill I pulled in, and I committed it without credit; I removed it from the prompts in April. The right way is to check the source's license, keep a header in the file with the source and license, list it in third-party notices, which I do in Lumen, or rewrite it in my own words if the license doesn't allow reuse. Prompts are content, and licenses apply to them.
  - `L1` What is a canvas file, and what three types could it be? ↳ HTML mini-app, PowerPoint, Word
  - `L2` What is the canvas tool, and why run data migrations in a Node `vm` subprocess? ↳ AI-written apps; migration code is untrusted; 5 s timeout, safe globals
  - `L2` Why does the server reject `localStorage` in AI-generated HTML? ↳ app state must live in `window.data` so app can save/restore
  - `L2` How were slides described for the model? ↳ JSON objects (text, image, shape) with x, y, w, h on 10×7.5 inch grid
  - `L2` How were Word documents described? ↳ sections with headings, paragraphs, lists, tables as structured JSON
  - `L2` Why deep-merge a partial JSON patch instead of rewriting the document? ↳ change one theme colour, keep everything else
  - `L2` How did `update_file` edit HTML, and what goes wrong with first-match string replace?
  - `L2` How could AiRA find a canvas file the user made last week? ↳ description embedded in Qdrant with user filter; top 10, dedupe, load from Mongo
  - `L2` What are skill files, and how did they shrink the canvas prompt by about 400 lines? ↳ short skill list; load one via `skill_name`; rules injected on demand
  - `L3` `python-docx` was added and removed the same day. Where does rendering happen, and why there?
  - `L3` The `frontend_skill.md` you committed reads like an imported public skill. How do you credit and license borrowed prompts?
- [ ] JSON-driven native UI engine: state, derived, actions and ui tree; safe expression parser; action steps; validation before render
  - **Answer:** This was a branch-only prototype from April 2026 that didn't ship. The problem: AI-made mini-apps were HTML in a WebView, which starts slowly, doesn't feel native and runs arbitrary JavaScript. Before building, I compared three ways to render LLM-generated UI in a spike repo: HTML and JS in a WebView, React Native code generated and compiled on the device, and native components driven by JSON. I picked the third for safety, native feel and payload size. For an LLM product, JSON is a small target the model can hit and you can validate it before anything shows. ¶ A schema has four sections: `state`, `derived`, `actions` and `ui`. The flow: normalize, which parses the JSON, expands compact aliases and maps HTML-ish names like div to View; validate every component, action type and expression up front; hold state in a store and recompute derived values when it changes; render the tree recursively from a component registry, skipping nodes whose `visibleWhen` is false and repeating `each` with the item and index in scope; then events run named action flows, and state saves back through the canvas API. Props resolve four ways: `bind` reads a state path and wires onChange, `expr` evaluates an expression, `action` wires the executor, and double-brace strings are interpolated. I validate the whole schema first because a half-drawn broken app is worse than a clear error, and a bad expression should fail at load, not on a tap. If rendering still throws, a try/catch shows a "Canvas render failed" panel instead of crashing the screen. ¶ Why not `eval` the model's expressions? Because that hands model output the whole JavaScript runtime: network, storage and the native bridge. So I wrote my own tokenizer, parser and evaluator. The tokenizer uses sticky regexes for identifiers and numbers and matches multi-character operators first; a recursive-descent parser builds an AST; the evaluator walks it and can only call whitelisted helpers. Recursive descent encodes precedence as a chain of functions, loosest first: conditional, logical or, logical and, equality, comparison, additive, multiplicative, exponent, unary, then member access and calls, then primary values. Each level parses its operands by calling the next tighter level, so tighter operators bind first; left-associative levels loop, and exponent recurses to the right. Prototype escapes are blocked by refusing `__proto__`, `prototype` and `constructor` on every member access, including computed brackets, and the scope is a whitelist with no globals. ¶ Then the part I'd own up to. A later commit added a fallback: when the safe parser couldn't handle a model expression, it ran it as real JavaScript through `Function` with a `with (scope)` block, and the validator accepted anything that compiled. The reason was that models wrote block arrow functions, spreads and `new Date().toISOString()`, and screens went blank. But it removed the sandbox: the forbidden-key check only applies on the safe path, unknown names fall through the scope proxy to real globals, and any safe-path error, not just a parse error, re-ran the expression as JavaScript. The right fix was to grow the safe language to what models actually write, or reject and feed the error back. I wouldn't ship it that way. ¶ Actions are ordered steps: run, set, condition, validate, api, log and alert, each with an optional `when`, plus onSuccess and onError branches. When `validate` fails it collects field errors and throws; the flow stops, records the error, and runs onError if there is one. Steps before it are not rolled back, so validate goes first, or I'd snapshot state at the start of an action and restore it on failure. The `api` step was a bare fetch to whatever URL the schema resolved, with no allowlist. To make it safe: no direct fetch from schema, every call through our server as a proxy with a per-app host allowlist, auth added server-side, never a secret in the schema since it's model output, and rate limits. ¶ Styling started as Tailwind class names plus a mapper to React Native styles, about 377 lines when I deleted it. I switched the model's contract to plain React Native style objects, because the model writes those directly and the mapper was a lossy layer that re-resolved classes against screen width in every component. The alias normaliser expands compact keys, like `s`, `d`, `a` and `u` for the four sections, back to full names on load; in the spike's report, an example schema measured about 3.0k tokens minified against about 5.7k for the original. ¶ Why it didn't ship: it never reached master, and in August the team moved to OpenUI Lang. My read is that a whole JSON document has to finish before it can render, while in voice-first Omni the screen has to paint while the model speaks, and Lang streams line by line. What would have made it ship: a streaming format, no `Function` fallback, a real test suite, and versioning. There's no schema version field today. I'd add one at the root, run code-owned upgrade steps from version to version on load so last month's saved UIs still render, keep component changes additive, and keep a set of saved schemas as a regression suite. ¶ Testing the evaluator: table tests of expression, scope and expected value; hostile inputs like `constructor.constructor`, `__proto__` through brackets and built-up strings, very deep nesting and very long inputs; grammar-based fuzzing with properties like "never throws anything but a typed error, never reaches a global"; and a differential test against real JavaScript for the safe subset. The engine, the components and the prompt were mine; my teammate Sahad moved the renderer to TypeScript and worked on the viewer.
  - `L1` What problem was the native UI engine meant to solve compared with HTML in a WebView?
  - `L1` What problem does a JSON UI engine solve for an LLM product?
  - `L1` Which three ways to render LLM-generated UI did you compare? ↳ WebView HTML/JS, on-device generated code, JSON-driven native components
  - `L1` Why not just `eval` the model's expressions?
  - `L2` Walk me through the flow from a model-written schema to rendered components. ↳ normalize → validate against registries → state + derived → render tree → action flows
  - `L2` What goes in the schema? ↳ state, derived values, actions, UI tree with `visibleWhen` and `each`
  - `L2` Why write your own tokenizer, parser and evaluator for expressions? ↳ model-written code; block `__proto__`, `prototype`, `constructor`; no `eval`
  - `L2` Which action step types did the engine support? ↳ run, set, condition, validate, api, log, alert, each with optional `when`
  - `L2` Why switch styling from Tailwind class names to plain React Native style objects? ↳ LLM contract changed to RN styles; mapper deleted
  - `L2` A model-written schema crashes the renderer. What does the user see? ↳ try/catch around render; "Canvas render failed" panel
  - `L2` What are the four sections of a schema? ↳ state, derived, actions, ui
  - `L2` How does the renderer walk the tree? ↳ recursive render, component registry, `visibleWhen`, `each` with item scope
  - `L2` How do `bind`, `expr`, `action` and `{{…}}` props get resolved?
  - `L2` Walk me through your expression language: tokenizer, parser, evaluator. ↳ sticky-regex tokens, recursive descent, AST, whitelisted helpers
  - `L2` How do you block prototype-chain escapes? ↳ forbid `__proto__`, `prototype`, `constructor`; whitelist the scope
  - `L2` How does an action run, and what happens when validation fails halfway? ↳ ordered steps; `validate` aborts with field errors
  - `L2` Why validate the whole schema before rendering anything?
  - `L2` How did the alias normaliser cut output tokens, and what did you measure? ↳ compact keys expanded on load; ~3.0k vs ~5.7k tokens
  - `L3` A later commit added a `Function(...)` fallback for expressions the safe parser couldn't handle. What did that do to the sandbox?
  - `L3` The engine stayed a branch-only prototype. Why didn't it ship, and what would have made it ship?
  - `L3` An `api_request` step comes from model output. How do you stop it calling any URL it likes? ↳ host allowlist, no secrets in schema, server proxy
  - `L3` How does recursive descent encode operator precedence?
  - `L3` How would you version the schema so UIs saved last month still render?
  - `L3` How would you test the expression evaluator? ↳ table tests, fuzzing, hostile inputs

## 1.14 Other projects and take-homes
- [ ] Kilnic: bitmask RBAC with tenant caps, four gRPC services each with its own Postgres, refresh-token rotation, JWKS verification
  - **Answer:** Kilnic is a multi-tenant clinic platform backend I built with a friend, Rohit Raj, in April and May 2026. I wrote 21 of the 24 commits and about 6,600 of roughly 8,100 code lines on the branch. Only my RBAC models PR is merged to main; the rest sits on a feature branch, unmerged and never deployed, so I describe it as built, not shipped. Multi-tenancy means many clinics share one system and never see each other's data. A tenant is a `Tenant` row (a clinic) with a `service_mask` of the services it subscribes to. Roles are scoped per tenant, and every clinical row carries a `tenant_id`. ¶ gRPC is RPC over HTTP/2 with Protocol Buffers: typed stubs from `.proto` files, binary encoding, streaming. REST is resource URLs with JSON, easier to debug and call from a browser. Kilnic is my gRPC project. HS256 signs with one shared secret, so every verifier could also mint tokens; RS256 signs with a private RSA key, so only identity issues tokens and everyone else verifies with the public key from a JWKS endpoint, `/.well-known/jwks.json`, a JSON list of public keys keyed by `kid`. ¶ The RBAC is bitmasks. Every permission and feature is defined in the database with a bit value that must be a positive power of two. A role holds a service mask and a permission mask per resource, and a check is one bitwise AND. It's compact and fast, and capping is trivial. Rohit's first mock applied user overrides with XOR, and I changed that to OR. XOR toggles, so an override that "grants" a bit the role already has silently removes it. OR can only add. The tenant cap: effective permissions are `(base | override) & cap`. On write I check every key is a known, active service or resource, masks use only defined bits, and a role or override is a subset of the tenant's subscription. Role changes go to an audit log with before and after values. ¶ I split the Django monolith into four services in five planned phases: identity, medicines, prescriptions and vitals, each with its own Postgres. To be straight about how: the split, the managers-refactor spec and the async fan-out were pair-built with Claude Code, spec then plan then execute, and those commits carry its co-author line; I can defend every choice in it. The cost is real. There are no cross-database foreign keys, so references are plain ids validated over gRPC at write time. Medicines has one call for active medicines (for writes) and one that includes deactivated ones, so old prescriptions still render. Client wrappers have a 5 second timeout and map NOT_FOUND to a validation error. Was it right for two people? I'd take it back. A modular monolith with clear app boundaries would have shipped faster. The split was partly learning and partly a bet on Rohit's much bigger planned design (Go, FastAPI, NestJS, Kafka), none of which was built. ¶ Access tokens are RS256 JWTs that last 900 seconds. Refresh tokens are opaque random secrets that last 30 days, and I store only their SHA-256, so a database leak gives nobody a usable token. They're long and random, so a fast hash is fine, unlike passwords. Rotation runs under `select_for_update` on the session row. Presenting an already-rotated token is treated as theft and revokes every live session of that user, and the revocation commits before the error is raised. Other services verify tokens locally against JWKS, cached for an hour and refetched when an unknown `kid` appears, falling back to the stale cache if the fetch fails. They still call `CheckSession` over gRPC, because otherwise a revoked session keeps working for up to 15 minutes. The trade-off is one identity call per request, the coupling JWTs were meant to avoid; a short revocation cache softens it. ¶ Medicine search embeds the query with `embed_anything`, a Rust BERT model loaded lazily as a singleton and run with `asyncio.to_thread`. It searches Qdrant filtered by `tenant_id`, drops hits under 0.85, and hydrates the rest from Postgres, skipping inactive rows. Sync to Qdrant runs on `transaction.on_commit` in a 2-worker thread pool with no retry. A crash after commit or a Qdrant outage loses the update for good. The fix is an outbox row written in the same transaction, a worker that retries with idempotent upserts keyed by medicine id, and a periodic reconcile. ¶ On the async side, prescriptions and vitals run the three independent identity lookups (tenant, doctor and patient) with `asyncio.gather`. That should turn about three round trips into about one, but that's inferred, not measured. If one fails, plain `gather` raises the first error while the others keep running. A `TaskGroup` cancels the siblings, and gRPC codes map to domain errors. `check_permission` reads bits from the database on every call; there's even a TODO for a cache, and it ignores its service argument. I'd cache the effective mask per user keyed by a role version, bump that version on any role, override or tenant-cap change, with a short TTL as a safety net. ¶ The worst gap is the RBAC update view: it had `permission_classes = []`, so any signed-in user could change role assignments. That's privilege escalation. A test where a non-admin calls the update and expects 403 would have caught it, and so would a test that walks every view and fails on empty permission classes. Honestly, the final branch has no tests: my auth commit deleted the earlier pytest suites, so I don't claim Kilnic as tested.
  - `L1` What is Kilnic, and which part did you build? ↳ clinic platform backend, with a friend; built on a branch, not shipped
  - `L1` What is multi-tenancy? How was a tenant represented?
  - `L1` What is gRPC, and how is it different from REST?
  - `L1` RS256 vs HS256 for JWTs: what's the difference?
  - `L1` What is a JWKS endpoint?
  - `L1` Which of your projects use gRPC?
  - `L2` Explain your bitmask RBAC. Why bitmasks? ↳ one power-of-two bit per permission; role masks; tenant service cap
  - `L2` Overrides changed from XOR to OR. Why does XOR surprise people? ↳ XOR can silently remove a permission the role had
  - `L2` How does a tenant's subscription cap every role and override? ↳ effective = (base | override) & cap; subset validation on write
  - `L2` Why split into 4 services, each with its own Postgres? What did it cost? ↳ no cross-DB foreign keys; validate ids over gRPC
  - `L2` How do refresh-token rotation and reuse detection work? ↳ store hash, rotate under row lock, reuse revokes all sessions
  - `L2` Why store only the SHA-256 of refresh tokens?
  - `L2` How do other services verify a token without calling identity every time? ↳ JWKS cached an hour, refetched on unknown `kid`
  - `L2` Then why also call `CheckSession` over gRPC? ↳ a revoked session dies now, not at token expiry
  - `L2` How did semantic medicine search work? ↳ BERT embeddings, Qdrant filtered by tenant, 0.85 cutoff, hydrate from Postgres
  - `L2` Postgres-to-Qdrant sync runs after commit in a thread pool with no retry. What can go wrong? ↳ crash loses updates; outbox + worker
  - `L3` `asyncio.gather` on 3 gRPC lookups: what did it save, and what if one fails? ↳ ~3 round trips to ~1; cancel the rest, map errors
  - `L3` `check_permission` reads bits from the database on every call. How would you cache it safely? ↳ cache per role version; invalidate on change
  - `L3` The RBAC update view had no permission classes. What's the risk, and which test would have caught it?
  - `L3` Was splitting into microservices right for a two-person project? Defend it or take it back.
- [ ] StatusPulse: multi-tenant status pages, HttpOnly JWT cookie, invites, org scoping, live updates over a websockets bridge
  - **Answer:** StatusPulse was my Plivo take-home: a multi-tenant status page and incident tool. A company's team manages its services and incidents, and its customers see a public status page. I built it in three days, June 28 to 30, 2025, with Django REST Framework and React, deployed on Render. It's the project of mine with a public status page. A service has four levels: operational, degraded performance, partial outage, major outage. An incident goes investigating, identified, monitoring, resolved, with a severity from low to critical and a resolved timestamp. ¶ The JWT sits in an HttpOnly cookie because JavaScript can't read it, so an XSS bug can't steal the token, while anything in localStorage is readable by every script on the page. The SPA and the API were on different Render subdomains, which count as different sites. The cookie therefore needed `SameSite=None; Secure` to be sent on credentialed cross-site calls. That opens CSRF: any site can make the browser send it. The fixes are a CSRF token (double submit) or putting both under one domain and using `SameSite=Lax`. The token lasts 24 hours with no refresh token. That's simple, but a stolen cookie stays good for a day, I can't revoke it, and users get logged out daily. ¶ Invites: an admin creates an invite bound to the invitee's email, with a UUID token, single use and valid for one day. The link goes to the signup page, which pre-fills the organisation and email. After joining, the admin grants or revokes access per member. Org scoping: every user belongs to one organisation with a role (admin, team or user). Every read is filtered by the caller's organisation, and every write checks the object belongs to it, with custom DRF permission classes like `IsOrganizationAdmin`. One gap: incident update and delete only require login, not the access flag, though the org check still applies. The public page uses unauthenticated endpoints. Anonymous visitors see only services marked `publicly_visible`; signed-in members see everything. ¶ The timeline is one table that points at either a service or an incident through a GenericForeignKey (`content_type` plus `object_id`). It stores who did it and old and new values for status changes. I get one events table and one query for a mixed history. I lose database-level integrity: there's no real foreign key, nothing cascades, deleting a service can leave dangling rows, and joins are awkward. ¶ Live updates: a `post_save` or `post_delete` signal on Service or Incident builds an event and sends it over a raw TCP socket to port 9000. A standalone asyncio `websockets` server listens there and broadcasts to rooms like the organisation room or a service's incident room. The React client keeps one WebSocket manager, joins rooms per page, and on any event re-runs the Redux thunks that refetch data; it doesn't trust the payload. Why not Django Channels? It was in the requirements file but unused. A separate small server was quick to reason about next to plain Django. Channels would have been the more standard choice, and it would have avoided my deploy problem. ¶ That problem: the socket server listened on localhost, and Render exposes one public port, so in production the browser's socket most likely never connected. What kept the UI current was the fallback I added on the last day: after create, update or delete, the client refetches over HTTP when the socket is down. That covers the person making the change, not other viewers. The signal also runs inside the request. The bridge call has a 2 second timeout and catches its errors, so a dead bridge doesn't fail the request, but it can add up to 2 seconds, and the event fires even if the transaction later rolls back. I'd send it on commit, best-effort, through a queue. ¶ The organisations list was public and showed user counts. Yes, that's a leak: anyone can enumerate a vendor's customers and their team sizes. I'd list only organisations that opted into a public page, drop the counts, and address pages by slug. Automatic status from uptime checks: each service gets a check config (URL, interval, timeout, expected status). A scheduled worker runs the checks and stores results. Status changes only after several consecutive failures or successes, so it doesn't flap. High latency maps to degraded and failures to outages, and the change goes through the same write path, so the timeline and live events fire and an incident can open automatically. Ideally the checks run from more than one region. ¶ What I cut for three days: SQLite instead of Postgres, a hard-coded secret key and a committed database file, no tests, no refresh tokens or CSRF tokens, Django's dev server on Render, and a socket deploy I never verified end to end. Day one was auth, signup, invites and Redux. Day two was services, incidents, the timeline, and swapping my custom middleware for a proper DRF authentication class. Day three was WebSockets, a run of Render fixes for CORS, cookies and URLs, and the HTTP fallback.
  - `L1` What is StatusPulse, and who is it for?
  - `L1` What statuses can a service have, and what states can an incident go through? ↳ 4 service levels; investigating → identified → monitoring → resolved
  - `L1` Why keep a JWT in an HttpOnly cookie instead of localStorage?
  - `L1` Which project has a public status page?
