  - `L2` Why `SameSite=None; Secure` on the cookie, and what risk does it open? ↳ cross-site SPA to API; CSRF — add tokens or share a domain
  - `L2` A 24-hour access token and no refresh token: trade-offs?
  - `L2` How did team invites work? ↳ single-use, email-bound, expiring tokens; admin grants access
  - `L2` How did you scope every query to the caller's organization?
  - `L2` How does the public status page decide what anonymous visitors see? ↳ `publicly_visible` flag; members see everything
  - `L2` Why a GenericForeignKey for the timeline, and what do you lose? ↳ one events table; no DB-level integrity, awkward joins
  - `L2` Walk me through a status change reaching the browser live. ↳ post_save signal → TCP :9000 → WS server → org room → refetch
  - `L2` Why a standalone `websockets` server instead of Django Channels?
  - `L3` The WebSocket server listened on localhost on Render. What kept the UI live in production? ↳ the HTTP refetch fallback; one public port
  - `L3` Signals fire inside the request. What happens if the WebSocket bridge is down? ↳ request slows or fails; send after commit, best-effort
  - `L3` The organizations list was public, with user counts. Is that a leak?
  - `L3` How would you add uptime checks that change a service's status automatically?
  - `L3` You built this in 3 days. What did you cut to make the deadline?
- [ ] AI take-homes: PdfAnalyser page-cited RAG, browser_ai plan-then-execute agent, the AI stack builder node graph
  - **Answer:** These were three job-hunt take-homes from July 2025, each built in a day or two. PdfAnalyser is chat with a PDF, where every answer jumps to the page it came from. browser_ai is an LLM browser agent: a planner writes steps and an executor turns each step into Playwright actions. The AI stack builder is a drag-and-drop canvas for wiring query, knowledge base, LLM and output nodes, with a FastAPI and Postgres config backend. ¶ PdfAnalyser: the upload goes to Express, then to LlamaParse. That's LlamaIndex's cloud parser, which turns complex PDFs (tables, columns, odd layouts) into per-page markdown, where a plain text extractor scrambles columns and loses table structure. Parsing is a job, so the client polled a status endpoint on an interval. When it said success, the server fetched the per-page markdown. A webhook or an SSE push would be better than polling. I chunk each page separately, 500 characters with 30 of overlap, keeping the page number in metadata. Overlap stops a sentence from being cut in half at a boundary, and chunking per page means a chunk never spans pages, so the citation is exact. Embeddings were MiniLM through HuggingFace Inference. The top 10 matches go into the prompt labelled "Page N", and Gemini 2.5 Flash must return strict JSON `{text, page}`. The UI shows a page chip, and clicking it scrolls the embedded PDF viewer to that page. ¶ The store was LangChain's in-memory vector store, replaced on every upload. That's fine for a single-user demo with one document. It's wrong for production: it's lost on restart, there's no multi-user or multi-document isolation, it's bounded by RAM, and it can't be shared between server instances. The file was even named after Chroma, which was never used. ¶ browser_ai: one call asks the model for a JSON plan with a goal, steps, expected outcomes and error handling. For each step I take a compact DOM snapshot and ask an execution prompt for concrete actions (click, fill, navigate, wait, press, scroll), run them with 3 second pacing, scroll and retry on failure, and stop when the model reports all steps done. To shrink the page I keep only elements that are visible in the viewport and a short attribute whitelist (`id`, `aria-label`, `role`, `name`, `placeholder`, `title`, `alt`, `type`), and I drop scripts and styles. The model backend is chosen by an environment variable that maps to a handler with one interface: Gemini 2.5 Flash by default, local Ollama models, or a llamafile server. ¶ It ran in the user's own Chrome profile through a persistent context. That way it's already logged in everywhere, which is the point, and also the risk: the agent can act as the user, so a misread step or a malicious page can send, buy or delete for real. The defence against injection is to treat page text as data, fenced and labelled as untrusted. Plan from the user's goal, not from page text. Allowlist domains and actions, and require confirmation for anything risky like submit, pay or send. At AiRA our browser agent works that way: risky steps need the user's approval. To measure task completion: a fixed task suite with checkable end states (the order exists, the field holds the value), then success rate, steps, tokens or cost, time and human interventions, repeated several times because runs are not deterministic. ¶ The stack builder was built overnight, from about 6 pm to 2 am. I put the night into what a reviewer clicks first: the canvas with four node types, stack creation and saved config. I faked the chat reply with a timeout instead of calling a model, and I should have said so plainly in the README. To actually run the graph: validate it's acyclic, sort it topologically, give each node type an executor (query passes text, knowledge base retrieves, LLM gets query plus context, output streams), pass outputs along edges, and surface errors per node. ¶ Before pushing a take-home I now check .gitignore first, then that there's no `node_modules`, then a secret scan, then a README that says what's real and what's stubbed. I learned that the hard way: browser_ai went up with `node_modules` and a `.env` holding an API key. The right fix is to rotate the key and purge it from history before I link those repos anywhere.
  - `L1` What did PdfAnalyser, browser_ai and the AI stack builder each do?
  - `L1` What is LlamaParse, and why use it instead of a plain PDF text extractor?
  - `L2` PdfAnalyser: how did an answer jump to the right page of the PDF? ↳ per-page chunks with page metadata; model returns `{text, page}`
  - `L2` Why chunk per page at 500 characters with 30 overlap? What does overlap buy?
  - `L2` Parsing is asynchronous. How did the client know it was done, and what would be better? ↳ polling job status; webhook or SSE
  - `L2` Why is an in-memory vector store fine for a demo and wrong for production?
  - `L2` browser_ai: explain the plan-then-execute loop. ↳ planner writes JSON steps; executor maps step + DOM snapshot to Playwright actions
  - `L2` How did you shrink the page before sending it to the model? ↳ only visible in-viewport elements, attribute whitelist, no script/style
  - `L2` Why run the agent in the user's own Chrome profile, and what is the risk? ↳ logged-in sessions; the agent can act as the user
  - `L2` How did you make the model backend swappable (Gemini vs local Ollama)?
  - `L2` The stack builder's chat reply was simulated. How would you actually execute the node graph? ↳ topological order, executor per node, outputs flow along edges
  - `L3` How do you defend a browser agent against prompt injection from the page it reads? ↳ page text is data; allowlists; confirm risky actions
  - `L3` How would you measure whether a browser agent completed a task? ↳ task suite, end-state checks, steps taken, cost
  - `L3` You had one night for a take-home. How did you decide what to build and what to fake?
  - `L3` What do you check before pushing a take-home to public GitHub? ↳ .gitignore first, no node_modules, secret scan, README
- [ ] ML prototypes: GRU behaviour classifier exported to ONNX, live speaker ID with Resemblyzer embeddings and thresholds
  - **Answer:** Both are small personal ML prototypes from 2026: a behaviour classifier that guesses what a user is doing from UI events, and a live speaker-ID loop. I present them as pipelines I can explain end to end, not as models with proven accuracy. The behaviour tracker predicts one of 9 behaviours: skimming, hunting, normal, ignore, frustrated, engaged, confused, comparing and deep reading. A GRU is a recurrent cell with an update gate and a reset gate and a single hidden state. An LSTM adds a separate cell state and three gates. A GRU has fewer parameters and trains faster, and on short sequences it usually does about as well. Mine is one layer with hidden size 48. ¶ Input: each raw event (scroll, click, touch, selection, idle, error, navigation) becomes a vector with a one-hot event type, coordinates normalised by screen size, and the time gap since the last event. Training uses windows of 50 events with stride 8, keeping sessions with at least 8 events. Windowing gives fixed-length sequences and many examples per session. A small stride gives more samples, but neighbouring windows share 42 events, so they're highly correlated, and the split must be by session or test windows leak into training. Streaming inference scores one event at a time and carries the GRU hidden state per session between events, resetting it after a long idle gap. Training on 50-event windows while serving on an unbounded stream is a mismatch I'd test. ¶ Most of the training data is synthetic, from a generator script. The model mostly learns the generator's rules, so any accuracy on that data says little about real users. I also built a React collector app that records real labelled sessions and writes JSONL into the repo through the File System Access API, but only 720 real events are committed. ONNX is an open model format, so ONNX Runtime can run the model without PyTorch on a server, in a browser or on a phone. I exported through a wrapper that makes the hidden state an explicit input and output, and a parity test runs the same inputs through PyTorch and ONNX Runtime and compares within a tolerance. For browser or phone I'd use ONNX Runtime Web or Mobile and keep the hidden state per session in app code. At hidden size 48 it's tiny. No evaluation results are committed, so I say exactly that: the pipeline, the export and the parity test are done, and the missing piece is real labelled sessions and a held-out evaluation. ¶ A speaker embedding is a fixed-length vector that captures how a voice sounds, so the same speaker lands close in cosine similarity. resemblizer, my live speaker-ID prototype, captures mic audio at 16 kHz into a queue. The main loop keeps a rolling buffer capped at 3 seconds and drops older audio with a warning if it falls behind. It embeds each 1-second slice with Resemblyzer (256 dimensions) and compares it to every stored centroid. A best score of 0.95 or more merges into that centroid. Between 0.75 and 0.95 starts a new cluster for that same speaker. Below 0.75 registers a new speaker. Each speaker keeps up to 8 running-mean centroids, so loud, quiet and far-from-the-mic versions of one voice each get their own centre instead of being averaged into mush. ¶ I also benchmarked four encoders behind one interface: Resemblyzer plus SpeechBrain ECAPA-TDNN, x-vector and ResNet. I ran resemblizer as a spec, plan, implement loop with Claude Code, and the benchmark commits carry its co-author line. Each needed its own threshold (0.75, 0.30, 0.65 and 0.40) because each has its own embedding space and score distribution, so a cosine of 0.7 means different things in each. The benchmark writes a report per encoder with latency per chunk and a speaker timeline, and the store and encoders have unit tests. In the same weeks of May 2026 I built voice-print speaker identification inside AiRA on the same idea: Resemblyzer embeddings and Qdrant cosine search at a 0.80 threshold. I deleted it in July to drop torch, CUDA and the Redis memory it used. ¶ Evaluating it properly means labelled recordings and a diarisation error rate, a threshold sweep to choose the operating point, confusion tests on similar voices (same gender and accent), and latency. With two people talking at once, a 1-second window holds both voices, so the embedding is a blend and may match neither: you get a wrong label or a fake new speaker. I'd add overlap detection, mark those windows low-confidence, and require several consistent windows before creating a new speaker.
  - `L1` What did the behaviour tracker predict? ↳ 9 behaviours: skimming, hunting, frustrated, engaged, confused, deep reading…
  - `L1` What is a GRU, and how is it different from an LSTM?
  - `L1` What is a speaker embedding?
  - `L1` What is ONNX, and why export a model to it?
  - `L2` How did you turn raw UI events into model input? ↳ one-hot event type, screen-normalised coordinates, time gaps
  - `L2` Windows of 50 events with stride 8: why window at all, and what does the stride trade off?
  - `L2` How does streaming inference carry the GRU's hidden state between events? ↳ per-session hidden state, reset after a long idle gap
  - `L2` Most training data was synthetic. What does that do to your results? ↳ the model learns the generator; need real labelled sessions
  - `L2` How did you check the ONNX model matches PyTorch? ↳ parity test on the same inputs within a tolerance
  - `L2` resemblizer: walk me through identifying who is speaking, live. ↳ 16 kHz frames, rolling 3 s buffer, 1 s embeddings, cosine to centroids
  - `L2` Explain the thresholds: merge at ≥ 0.95, new cluster at 0.75–0.95, new speaker below 0.75. ↳ several centroids per speaker absorb voice variation
  - `L2` Why did each of the 4 encoders need its own threshold? ↳ different embedding spaces; cosine scores aren't comparable
  - `L3` How would you evaluate speaker ID properly? ↳ labelled recordings, diarisation error rate, similar-voice confusion
  - `L3` Two people talk at once. What happens, and how would you handle it?
  - `L3` How would you run the behaviour model in the browser or on a phone? ↳ ONNX Runtime Web/mobile, hidden state as explicit input
  - `L3` No evaluation results were committed for the GRU. How do you present this project honestly?
- [ ] Agent-built apps and browser media: fitness tracker merge, reel_share game runtime, MotionEase worker, Fieldom Stream overlay
  - **Answer:** These four are small apps where the interesting part is one design decision each. In two of them, the fitness tracker and reel_share, I wrote the specs and drove an AI coding agent through them, and I say so up front. The fitness tracker is an Expo React Native app from May 2026 for spends, nutrition, water, weight, loans and a cycle tracker. All data lives on the device as one typed JSON document in AsyncStorage. Old data keeps loading after model changes because the loader merges stored data with the seed, filling any missing section, and runs on-load migrations. Old free-text meals become the newer labelled shape with items, for example. A `hydrated` flag stops the seed from overwriting storage before the load finishes. ¶ Backup is a versioned JSON envelope, and import refuses files from a newer schema and shows counts before applying. The merge is idempotent, additive and local-wins. Records match by a stable key (bank plus account, card name, loan id). Transactions and meals have no id, so they match by a content hash over time, label and sorted items. Importing twice adds nothing, and on conflict the local copy wins. The trade-off is written in the spec: there are no tombstones, so deletions don't travel. Cycle prediction is pure functions. Flow days become contiguous periods, periods become cycles between start dates, and the average uses the last 6 cycles. It shows an estimate-only state until 2 cycles exist. ¶ reel_share, from August 2026, is a platform for playable posts: short interactive games that sit in a vertical feed like a reel, with 17 experiences across DOM, Canvas 2D and Babylon.js 3D, each loaded lazily. The runtime is an explicit state machine: created, initializing, ready, running and paused, ended, destroying, destroyed, plus error, with an allowed-transitions table. The host can pause at any moment and the tab can hide, so illegal transitions should throw instead of leaving half-states. Teardown happens exactly once, and pause reasons are a set, so a visibility pause and a user pause don't cancel each other. To talk to a Flutter WebView, every event is a versioned envelope (protocol version, game id, session id, timestamp, type, payload) sent as a CustomEvent and also to `GameHost.postMessage` when a native host exists. The host controls the game through pause, resume, restart and mute. ¶ The bot arena is an open 3D space, so it gets a Yuka state machine (patrol, search, chase, attack, retreat) and Recast navmesh routing, and Recast loads only with that arena. Endless Dodger is a few lanes, so a small controller that scores lanes by time to collision does the job without the extra bundle. A seeded random generator, `?test=1&seed=42`, makes every run replayable, which keeps Vitest and Playwright tests deterministic. There are 13 unit suites and 5 end-to-end specs behind one check command. About 17,700 lines arrived in one commit, built by an agent against three long implementation plans. I own it by saying that plainly, defending the architecture choices above, and walking through or changing any file live. ¶ MotionEase, from December 2024, is an Electron overlay for motion sickness, similar in idea to Apple's Vehicle Motion Cues: dots on screen move opposite to your head movement, so your eyes get a cue that matches the motion you feel. I never tested whether it actually reduces sickness. TensorFlow.js MoveNet pose detection runs in a Web Worker so the UI thread stays free. Frames come from `ImageCapture.grabFrame()` as an `ImageBitmap`, transferred to the worker zero-copy. The window is transparent, always on top and click-through with `setIgnoreMouseEvents`. Hovering the reset button turns mouse events back on through a whitelisted IPC bridge, and leaving turns them off. Context isolation is on and Node integration off. ¶ Fieldom Stream was a four-day sprint with Rohit in April 2025. Headless Puppeteer opens a CricClubs scorecard and installs a MutationObserver, and every change pushes the page HTML over Socket.IO to the streamer's browser. There it's parsed into teams, score, overs, batsmen and bowler, and drawn onto the webcam canvas, which `captureStream` turns into a video stream. Rohit's side piped video to YouTube over ffmpeg and RTMP. To be precise, in the final code my overlay path recorded locally, and the chunks that reached YouTube came from the plain webcam path. `html2canvas` on every frame rasterises the whole DOM each time and caused heavy re-renders. My first fix was merged and then reverted; the lasting one drew the scoreboard directly with Canvas 2D. Sending the whole HTML on every mutation is wasteful. I'd extract only the needed fields in the page, diff against the last values, debounce, and send just the changes.
  - `L1` Fitness tracker: what does it track, and where does the data live? ↳ spends, nutrition, cycle; on the device in AsyncStorage
  - `L1` reel_share: what is a "playable post"?
  - `L1` MotionEase: how does it help with motion sickness?
  - `L1` What did MotionEase run in a Web Worker?
  - `L2` Fitness tracker: how does old saved data keep loading after the data model changes? ↳ merge with seed, migrate old entries on load
  - `L2` Explain the idempotent, additive, local-wins import merge. ↳ match by stable key or content hash; no tombstones, deletes don't travel
  - `L2` How does the cycle tracker predict the next period? ↳ pure functions: periods → cycles → last-6 average; estimate until 2 cycles
  - `L2` reel_share: why model the game runtime as a state machine with an allowed-transitions table?
  - `L2` How does a web game talk to a Flutter WebView host? ↳ versioned event envelope; CustomEvent + `GameHost.postMessage`
  - `L2` Why Yuka + Recast for the arena bot, but tiny controllers for Endless Dodger?
  - `L2` What does a seeded random generator give your tests? ↳ deterministic replays; `?test=1&seed=42`
  - `L2` MotionEase: why run pose detection in a Web Worker, and how did frames get there cheaply? ↳ frees main thread; ImageBitmap transferred zero-copy
  - `L2` How do you make an Electron overlay click-through but still let one button be clicked? ↳ setIgnoreMouseEvents, toggled on hover; contextIsolation on
  - `L2` Fieldom Stream: how did a live cricket score end up inside the video? ↳ Puppeteer + MutationObserver → socket → canvas overlay → captureStream
  - `L2` Why was drawing the scoreboard on canvas better than `html2canvas` on every frame?
  - `L3` reel_share arrived as about 17,700 lines in one commit from an AI agent. How do you show you own that code?
  - `L3` A MutationObserver sends the whole page HTML on every change. What would you improve? ↳ extract only needed fields, diff, debounce
- [ ] Web3 with Foundry: Chainlink VRF raffle, over-collateralised stablecoin and health factor, reentrancy, on-chain NFTs
  - **Answer:** These were course projects. I did Cyfrin Updraft's Foundry track between November 2024 and March 2025, and I present them that way: none is an original protocol. A smart contract is code deployed on a blockchain whose state changes through transactions. Gas is the fee for computation and storage, which makes every loop and write cost money. Foundry is the Solidity toolkit: `forge` builds, tests and runs deploy scripts, `cast` calls contracts and the chain from the command line, and `anvil` is a local chain. ERC-20 is fungible tokens, just balances. ERC-721 is unique tokens, each with an owner and its own metadata URI. ¶ A contract can't make its own random number because execution must be deterministic: every node re-runs it and gets the same result. Anything on-chain like a timestamp or block hash is predictable or can be nudged by validators. Chainlink VRF solves it. The raffle requests randomness, an oracle generates random words with a cryptographic proof that's checked on-chain, and the coordinator calls `fulfillRandomWords`, where I pick the winner as the first random word modulo the player count, reset the raffle and pay out. Mine uses VRF v2.5, and a script creates and funds the subscription and adds the consumer programmatically. Chainlink Automation calls `checkUpkeep` off-chain. It returns true when the interval has passed, the raffle is open and it has a balance and players, and then `performUpkeep` runs on-chain and requests the randomness. There's an OPEN and a CALCULATING state so nobody can enter while the draw is pending and change the player list under the random number. ¶ I tested it with about 12 Foundry unit tests: entry-fee reverts, player recording, events, entry blocked while calculating, `checkUpkeep` true and false cases, the request id read from logs, and a full payout test that's skipped on forks. The fuzz test feeds random request ids to the mock coordinator's `fulfillRandomWords` and expects a revert, because no request exists yet. ¶ The stablecoin is over-collateralised: you deposit wETH or wBTC worth more than the stablecoin you mint. With a liquidation threshold of 50, you need 200 percent collateral. The health factor is collateral value times the threshold divided by debt, and below 1 the position can be liquidated. My `liquidate()` is a stub with its body commented out, and I say so. It should check the user's health factor is below 1, let the liquidator burn some of the user's debt, give the liquidator that value in collateral plus a 10 percent bonus, and require that the user's health factor improved and the liquidator's stays healthy. ¶ Reentrancy is when a contract makes an external call before updating its own state, and the callee calls back in and repeats the action, like withdrawing twice. The defences are checks-effects-interactions (update balances before sending) and a reentrancy guard. Honestly my engine has neither a guard nor an oracle staleness check; it reads `latestRoundData` and uses the price. With a stale feed, collateral is valued wrong: healthy users get liquidated, or bad debt gets minted. I'd check `updatedAt` against the feed's heartbeat and the price is positive, and pause when it's stale. The NFT builds its whole metadata on-chain and points the image at a Base64 SVG data URI, so it doesn't depend on someone keeping an IPFS file pinned. The cost is gas. ¶ I also wrote a tiered crowdfunding contract with a factory, following thirdweb's tutorial, with active, successful and failed states and a backer refund on failure. Done safely: pull payments, where each backer withdraws their own refund instead of the contract looping over everyone. Zero their balance before sending, check the deadline and state on every path, and never let a failing transfer block others. What carried over to backend work: thinking in state machines and invariants, assuming every external call can fail or call back, idempotent state changes, and fuzz-style tests for the paths you'd never think to write by hand.
  - `L1` What is a smart contract, and what is gas?
  - `L1` What is Foundry? What do `forge`, `cast` and `anvil` do?
  - `L1` ERC-20 vs ERC-721: what is the difference?
  - `L1` Why can't a contract make its own random number? ↳ execution is deterministic; validators can predict or influence it
  - `L2` How does Chainlink VRF make your raffle fair? ↳ request → oracle returns random words with proof → `fulfillRandomWords`
  - `L2` What do `checkUpkeep` and `performUpkeep` do in Chainlink Automation?
  - `L2` Why does the raffle have an OPEN and a CALCULATING state?
  - `L2` How did you test the raffle? Describe one fuzz test. ↳ `fulfillRandomWords` can't succeed before a request exists
  - `L2` How does an over-collateralised stablecoin work? What is the health factor? ↳ collateral × threshold ÷ debt; below 1 is liquidatable
  - `L2` Your `liquidate()` was left as a stub. How should it work? ↳ repay debt, seize collateral + 10% bonus, health factor must improve
  - `L2` What is a reentrancy attack, and how do you prevent it? ↳ checks-effects-interactions, ReentrancyGuard
  - `L2` Why build an NFT's image as an on-chain SVG data URI?
  - `L3` The price feed returns stale data. What goes wrong in the stablecoin? ↳ wrong liquidations; check `updatedAt`, pause on staleness
  - `L3` How would you design the refund path of a crowdfunding contract safely? ↳ pull payments, state machine, deadline checks
  - `L3` These were course projects. How do you present them honestly, and what carried over to backend work?
- [ ] Trading backtests (lookahead, survivorship, costs, placebo, out-of-sample) and the TradingView MCP server
  - **Answer:** The question was whether retail indicator strategies make money on NSE after real costs, and whether any beat simply holding the index. The core runs were at the end of October 2026, with sub-studies into early November, as private research with Claude Code as my pair: I set the questions, the bias checks and the pass rule, and the agent wrote much of the code, as the commits' co-author line shows. Lookahead bias is using information you wouldn't have had yet, like filling at the close that made the signal. Survivorship bias, in one line: testing only survivors flatters the past. Overfitting is tuning to noise; out-of-sample testing judges settings on data they weren't picked on, and walk-forward repeats that on a rolling window. ¶ To avoid lookahead, signals use a bar's close and fill at the next bar's open. A gap through the stop exits at the open, and if stop and target hit in the same bar I assume the stop. Higher-timeframe filters use only the last fully closed bar, the daily trend filter uses yesterday's close, and GARCH and HMM models are refit each January on data up to the previous year-end. Tests check no-lookahead for the fill rule, GARCH, HMM and the higher-timeframe side, ATR, EMA and ADX match hand-computed values, and trades were re-checked against raw candles, for example 400 random trades in one sub-study with zero mismatches. The suite has 34 pytest tests, and testing found real bugs, including weekly Sharpe being 2.2 times too high because weekly returns were annualised as if daily. ¶ Survivorship is my biggest caveat. The universe was today's Nifty 200, because I didn't have historical membership. The momentum backtest made 35 percent a year since October 2020, while the real Nifty 200 Momentum 30 index made 14.6 percent, so the absolute numbers are inflated. Costs are real broker charges plus slippage: with FYERS on 10 lakh rupees, a round trip was about ₹402 intraday versus about ₹2,287 for delivery, which is why intraday signals held as delivery were the worst of both worlds. Slippage was 0.02 percent per side, re-run at 0 and 0.03, and the "best" short-timeframe settings died there: in my SuperTrend study, a 15-minute setting went from about +₹13,000 to about −₹1.25 lakh just by adding 0.02 percent. An edge thinner than costs isn't an edge. ¶ The placebo keeps exits, sizing and trade count the same but picks entries at random, over 20 seeds, so a strategy has to beat luck, not zero. Weekly pullback made 18.5 percent out-of-sample against a random median of 10.6 and beat all 20 runs. Momentum, compared against random portfolios, made 27.1 against 14.0. The daily breakout beat only 75 percent. Daily and weekly split 2015–2021 in-sample, 2022 to October 2026 out-of-sample; hourly 2023–24 versus 2025–26; 15-minute and 5-minute data were too short to split. The pass rule: positive out-of-sample expectancy after costs over at least 100 trades, Sharpe above Nifty or positive alpha, and drawdown no worse than 1.5 times Nifty's. Out-of-sample, the 200-day market filter was the one equation that helped: plus 0.26 Sharpe and about 8 points less drawdown on average across 16 cases. Volatility targeting, GARCH and the HMM regime filter averaged slightly negative. True walk-forward exists only as those yearly refits. ¶ I tested hundreds of variants, 224 runs in one break-even analysis alone. My guards: parameters picked in-sample and judged out-of-sample, the placebo, few parameters, robustness across neighbouring settings (all 8 weekly-pullback settings were profitable out-of-sample), and naming the families to test before seeing results. I didn't apply a deflated Sharpe or any formal multiple-testing correction; that's next. On 3.5 months of 5-minute bars I trust almost nothing: the opening range breakout was profitable in 17 of 20 settings, but that's about 170 trades in one market phase, and the 15-minute version lost money in all 43 settings on Nifty 200. It's a lead, not a result. ¶ The TradingView MCP server isn't mine: it's an open-source, MIT-licensed project, `tradesdontlie/tradingview-mcp`. MCP, the Model Context Protocol, lets a server expose tools (actions), resources (readable data) and prompts to a model over a transport like stdio or HTTP. This one drives the TradingView desktop app through the Chrome DevTools Protocol and exposes 84 tools for charts, indicators, Pine Script and replay. Around it, I registered it in Claude Code as a stdio server pointed at TradingView's debug port 9223 and fixed its path after a folder move. With the agent I also wrote an exporter that pulls candles to CSV on its own debug port, because my browser held the default 9222, plus Pine strategies and Python scripts that re-cost TradingView's results with real charges and slippage. It relies on undocumented app internals, so an update can silently break it: pin versions, health-check each session, validate exported data. ¶ Could the agent place a real trade? It exposes no order tools; its only "trade" is inside TradingView's replay simulator, and its README lists real trading as out of scope. I'd still make that structural: no broker keys on that machine or anywhere the agent can reach, and any future order path behind human confirmation. My 30-second verdict: most retail indicator strategies lose after costs, and intraday showed no proven edge. What survived out-of-sample was monthly momentum rotation, about 27 percent a year in both periods with Sharpe around 1.15 but inflated by survivorship, and a weekly pullback at 18.5 to 26 percent with Sharpe 1.12 to 1.28. Taxes aren't modelled, and my own guess, not a backtest number, is that real returns sit much nearer the real momentum index's 14.6 percent.
  - `L1` What question were the backtests trying to answer? ↳ do retail strategies make money on NSE after real costs?
  - `L1` What is lookahead bias?
  - `L1` What is survivorship bias?
  - `L1` What is overfitting in a backtest, and what is walk-forward / out-of-sample testing?
  - `L1` What is MCP (Model Context Protocol)? What are tools, resources and transports?
  - `L1` Survivorship bias in one line.
  - `L2` How did your simulator avoid lookahead? ↳ next-open fills; higher-timeframe filters use only closed bars; tests
  - `L2` The universe was today's Nifty 200. How much did that inflate results? ↳ momentum 35%/yr vs the real index 14.6%/yr
  - `L2` How did you model costs, and why did they decide the short timeframes? ↳ FYERS charges + slippage; ~₹400 intraday vs ~₹2,287 delivery round trip
  - `L2` The "best" settings died at 0.02–0.03% slippage. What does that tell you?
  - `L2` What is a random-entry placebo, and why compare against it? ↳ same exits, random entries; strategy must beat luck
  - `L2` How did you check the simulator itself was right? ↳ hand-computed indicators in tests; trades re-checked against raw candles
  - `L2` How did you split in-sample and out-of-sample, and what was the pass rule?
  - `L2` Which filters held up out of sample? ↳ walk-forward 200-day market filter helped; vol targeting, GARCH, HMM didn't
  - `L2` How does the TradingView MCP server control the app? ↳ Chrome DevTools Protocol on a debug port; 84 tools
  - `L2` You didn't write the MCP server. What did you build around it? ↳ registration, port fix, data exporter, Pine strategies, re-costing scripts
  - `L3` You tested hundreds of variants. How do you avoid fooling yourself? ↳ out-of-sample, placebo, few parameters, deflated Sharpe
  - `L3` You had only 3.5 months of 5-minute bars. How much can you trust an intraday result?
  - `L3` The MCP relies on undocumented TradingView internals. What are the risks for an agent built on it? ↳ breaks on update; pin version; health checks
  - `L3` An LLM agent drives charts and writes Pine Script. How do you guarantee it never places a real trade? ↳ no order tools exposed; separate broker keys; confirmations
  - `L3` Give your verdict on retail indicator strategies in 30 seconds. ↳ most lose after costs; momentum rotation and weekly pullback survived
- [ ] learning-os and the portfolio: one subagent per job, checker agents, Swift PDF/OCR tool, git submodules
  - **Answer:** learning-os is my personal learning workspace: books go in, study pages come out, and the projects I build to practise sit beside them. It's one private Git repo that holds the instructions, the agent definitions and the tools, with the content in submodules. I used submodules because visibility differs. The books are copyrighted PDFs, so that repo is private. My notes are public. Each project is its own repo, the portfolio public and the trading work private. I built all of it, the portfolio included, with Claude Code as my pair, and nearly every commit carries its co-author line; my part is the design, the rules and the checking. ¶ A Claude Code subagent is a separately defined agent with its own instructions, tools and fresh context that the main session hands a task to and gets a report back from. My pipeline from a PDF chapter to a study page has eleven steps. Pick the chapter and its page range, then crop the book's figures to files with a manifest. A page-builder agent writes the page, then does a completeness pass against the PDF and an enrich pass. A scene-writer agent writes picture prompts and never touches HTML. Pictures come from Gemini through a relay or from an SVG-illustrator agent that renders its own SVG and looks at it. A script inserts the pictures, another adds the light/dark toggle, the index is rebuilt, and a page-checker agent checks the result. Then I commit inside Notes and bump the submodule, and only when I ask for it. ¶ One agent per job instead of one big agent because each gets a narrow context and a clear contract, and the one that checks isn't the one that wrote. That's where most of the quality comes from. The checker script verifies that every book figure is used exactly once, every image exists, there are no external resources, and every inline script parses with `node --check`. It exits non-zero on any problem. The checker agent adds browser checks: no console errors, the theme toggle works, contrast is readable in both themes, and no horizontal scroll at 375 pixels. The insert script must be idempotent: running it twice changes nothing. ¶ The PDF tool is about 120 lines of Swift on PDFKit and Apple's Vision framework. It does info, outline, text, find, render and OCR, and the OCR runs offline at about 0.35 seconds a page. I wrote it because this Mac had no poppler, the agents' file reader couldn't render PDFs, and there was no network to install a Python PDF library. Gemini's page won't fetch localhost, but it will navigate to it, and a URL hash survives both ways. So my relay redirects to Gemini with the job in the hash. A page script sends the prompt and waits for a new image, then navigates back to a local save URL with the image as base64 in the hash. I also added a duplicate guard using a small average hash, after a reopened chat saved an old image under the next name. ¶ Pages are written plate by plate: each section is written to disk as it's finished, behind an insertion marker, so a run that gets cut off leaves a resumable partial, and every step can be re-run. Long agent runs do get cut off, so that matters more than it sounds. For the relay hitting Gemini's daily limit, about 20 images a day on that account, the written rule is stop, report and resume after the reset. Never switch accounts or go incognito, because that's evading a limit I agreed to, and the work cloud credentials never go into it. ¶ The portfolio is vanilla TypeScript and Vite; the only dev dependencies are TypeScript and Vite. It's a handful of sections and small animations, so a framework would be weight with nothing to manage. The demo films are plain markup: an element with a duration and child elements with data attributes for when they appear, type, tap or change class, while CSS variables drive bars and timers. An IntersectionObserver plays a film when it's on screen and pauses it off screen, only one plays at a time, and with reduced motion a film rests on its last frame. My rule for employer work on a public site: describe product behaviour only, the kind AiRA already shows publicly. No internal architecture, vendors, code names, security design or unreleased work. I also ran a pass that removed claims an audit couldn't back up. ¶ With submodules, if you push the workspace repo before the children, it points at commits the remote doesn't have, and a fresh clone breaks. So the rule is to push children first and the workspace last. What it taught me for production agents: narrow tools and narrow roles, a checker that isn't the writer, idempotent and resumable steps, honest stops when a resource runs out, and a human approving anything that commits or publishes.
  - `L1` What is learning-os? ↳ books → study pages → projects; a workspace repo with submodules
  - `L1` What is a Claude Code subagent?
  - `L1` Why git submodules for books, Notes and projects? ↳ different visibility: private books, public notes, one repo per project
  - `L2` Walk me through the pipeline from a PDF chapter to a finished study page. ↳ figures, page-builder, scenes, pictures, insert, theme, check, commit
  - `L2` Why one agent per job (page-builder, scene-writer, svg-illustrator, page-checker) instead of one big agent? ↳ narrow context, clear contract, independent checking
  - `L2` How do you know an agent-written page is complete and correct? ↳ checker: each figure once, images exist, scripts parse; browser checks
  - `L2` Why write a PDF tool in Swift on PDFKit? How does OCR work, and how fast is it? ↳ no poppler; Apple Vision offline, ~0.35 s a page
  - `L2` Gemini blocks requests to localhost. How did your relay move prompts and images? ↳ data in URL hashes via top-level navigation
  - `L2` Pages are written "plate by plate". Why does that matter for long agent runs? ↳ a cut-off run resumes; every step is re-runnable
  - `L2` Portfolio: why vanilla TypeScript and Vite with no framework?
  - `L2` How do the portfolio's demo "films" work? ↳ timeline data attributes; play on screen, pause off; reduced motion
  - `L2` What rule did you follow when describing employer work on a public site? ↳ product behaviour only; no internals, vendors or unreleased work
  - `L3` The image relay hit a usage limit mid-run. What did you do, and why not rotate accounts? ↳ stop, report, resume after the reset
  - `L3` With submodules, what goes wrong if you push the workspace repo before the child repos? ↳ it points at commits the remote doesn't have
  - `L3` What did building your own agent harness teach you that applies to production agents? ↳ narrow tools, checkers, idempotent steps, human approval
